Functional calculi for sectorial operators and related function theory Thanks: This work was partially supported financially by a Leverhulme Trust Visiting Research Professorship and an NCN grant UMO-2017/27/B/ST1/00078, and inspirationally by the ambience of the Lamb & Flag, Oxford.
Charles Batty, Alexander Gomilko, Yuri Tomilov

Abstract
. We construct two bounded functional calculi for sectorial operators on Banach spaces, which enhance the functional calculus for analytic Besov functions, by extending the class of functions, generalizing and sharpening estimates, and adapting the calculus to the angle of sectoriality. The calculi are based on appropriate reproducing formulas, they are compatible with standard functional calculi and they admit appropriate convergence lemmas and spectral mapping theorems. To achieve this, we develop the theory of associated function spaces in ways which are interesting and significant. As consequences of our calculi, we derive several well-known operator norm-estimates and provide generalizations of some of them.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 47A60, Secondary 30H10 46E15 47B12 47D03

Dedicated to Vladimir Müller on the occasion of his
70
th birthday

1.
Introduction

The theory of functional calculi forms a basis for the
study of sectorial operators and semigroup generators.
In particular,
there were two functional calculi used extensively in the
research on operator semigroups and sectorial operators through the past
fifty years.
One of them, the Hille-Phillips (HP) functional calculus for semigroup generators,
probably stemmed from the foundational monograph
[
37
]
,
and it became an indispensable part of semigroup theory.
The systematic approach to the other one, the holomorphic functional calculus
for sectoral operators,
was initiated by McIntosh and his collaborators in the 1980s.
While the two calculi appeared to be very useful in applications,
the operator norm-estimates within them are often problematic.
The estimates within the HP-calculus are direct but rather crude,
and the task of getting bounds within the holomorphic functional calculus
is a priori cumbersome since the calculus is not, in general, a bounded Banach algebra homomorphism.

To circumvent those problems, a number of additional tools and methods appeared in the literature.
In particular, the advanced notions and techniques related to bounded
H^{\infty}
-calculus,
R
-boundedness, Fourier multipliers and transference were developed in depth, and one may consult
[
38
]
,
[
41
]
and
[
35
]

for many of these function-theoretical developments.
Moreover, various implications of positivity of functions and their derivatives (completely monotone, Bernstein,
\mathcal{NP}_{+}
-functions) were adjusted to the operator-theoretical set-up.
For clarification of the role of positivity, see
[
51
]
,
[
31
]
and
[
6
]
, for example.

Recently, in
[
7
]
, a new functional calculus was constructed,
the so-called
\mathcal{B}
-calculus. First of all, the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus offers
a simple and efficient route to operator norm-estimates for functions of semigroup generators, thus
unifying a number of estimates in the literature and leading to new ones.
No supplementary arguments are required and the estimates underline the strength of the calculi.
Moreover, the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus possesses all attributes of classical functional calculi, see
[
7
]
.
When combined properly they lead to new spectral mapping theorems
and generalizations of fundamentals of semigroup theory, see
[
8
]
.
To put our results into a proper context and to use some of the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus properties
in the sequel, we briefly recall the set-up for the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus, see
[
7
]
for more details.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the algebra of holomorphic functions on the right half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that

(1.1)

\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|\,d\alpha<\infty.

These functions
have been considered in some detail in
[
7
]
(see also
[
53
]
).
In particular, every
f\in\mathcal{B}
belongs to
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\cap C(\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, and
\mathcal{B}
is a Banach algebra with the norm

(1.2)

\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}.

Moreover, the algebra
\mathcal{B}
modulo constants is isomorphic to the holomorphic Besov space
B^{+}_{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}),
see
[
7
, Proposition 6.2]
.
In the setting of power bounded operators on Hilbert spaces, the unit disc counterpart of
\mathcal{B}
was employed for the study of functional calculi in
[
47
]
.

Let
A
be a densely defined closed operator on a Banach space
X
such that

\sigma(A)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, and

(1.3)

\sup_{\alpha>0}\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\langle(\alpha+i\beta+A)^{-2}x,x^{*}\rangle|\,d\beta<\infty

for all
x\in X
and
x^{*}\in X^{*}
.
This class of operators includes two substantial subclasses, namely the negative generators of bounded
C_{0}
-semigroups on Hilbert spaces
X
and the negative generators of (sectorially) bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroups on Banach spaces
X
. On the other hand, every operator in the class is the negative generator of a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup.

The study of functional calculus based on the algebra
\mathcal{B}
was initiated in
[
56
]
for generators of bounded semigroups on Hilbert spaces and in
[
53
]
for generators of holomorphic semigroups. These works adapted and extended the approach from

[
47
]
to a more demanding and involved setting of unbounded operators. Most researchers were unaware of
[
56
]
until it became accessible a few years ago. Meanwhile the line of research put forward in
[
56
]
and
[
53
]
was continued in
[
36
]
and
[
52
]
proceeding in two different directions (additional related references can be found in
[
7
]
).
In
[
36
]
, by means of a new transference technique,
counterparts of the results from
[
53
]
were proved in the framework
of bounded semigroups on Hilbert space and certain substantial subclasses of
\mathcal{B}
, while
[
52
]
offered a number of generalisations and improvements of estimates from
[
53
]
.
Only
[
52
]
and
[
53
]
considered all functions in

\mathcal{B}
, applied to generators of bounded holomorphic semigroups in both papers. In
[
7
]
, we
introduced a bounded
\mathcal{B}
functional calculus for all operators satisfying (
1.3
),
and we extended the theory in
[
8
]
.

For
f\in\mathcal{B}
, set

(1.4)

\displaystyle\langle f(A)x,x^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=f(\infty)\langle x,x^{*}\rangle-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}{f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}\langle(\alpha-i\beta+A)^{-2}x,x^{*}\rangle\,d\beta\,d\alpha

for all
x\in X
and
x^{*}\in X^{*}
, where
f(\infty)=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to\infty}f(z)
. Using (
1.3
) and the definition of
\mathcal{B}
(and the Closed Graph Theorem), it is easy to show that
f(A)
is a bounded linear mapping from
X
to
X^{**}
, and that the linear mapping

\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{L}(X,X^{**}),\qquad f\mapsto f(A),

is bounded.

It was discovered in
[
7
]
that much more is true. If
A
belongs to any of the classes of semigroup generators mentioned above, then the formula (
1.4
) defines a bounded algebra homomorphism

\Phi_{A}:\mathcal{B}\to L(X),\qquad\Phi_{A}(f):=f(A).

It is natural to call the homomorphism
\Phi_{A}
the (
\mathcal{B}
-)calculus of
A
.
It was proved in
[
7
]
that
\Phi_{A}
possesses a number of useful properties.
In particular, it admits the spectral inclusion (spectral mapping, in the case of bounded holomorphic semigroups) theorem and a Convergence Lemma of appropriate form. The utility of the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus depends on the facts that it (strictly) extends the Hille-Phillips (HP-) calculus and it is compatible with the holomorphic functional calculi for sectorial and half-plane type operators.

Moreover, the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus
\Phi_{A}
is the only functional calculus that one can define for
A
satisfying (
1.3
) and for functions in
\mathcal{B}
. Indeed, let
A
be an operator on
X
with dense domain, and assume that
\sigma(A)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. A
(bounded)
\mathcal{B}
-calculus
for
A
is, by definition, a bounded algebra homomorphism
\Phi:\mathcal{B}\to L(X)
such that
\Phi((z+\cdot)^{-1})=(z+A)^{-1}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. As shown in
[
8
]
, if
A
admits a
\mathcal{B}
-calculus, then the resolvent assumption (
1.3
) holds, and the calculus is
\Phi_{A}
.

While the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus is optimal and unique for generators of Hilbert space semigroups,
the situation is far from being so for generators of bounded holomorphic semigroups on Banach spaces
(as this paper will, in particular, show).
Thus using the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus ideology as a guiding principle, it is natural to try to extend
it beyond the Besov algebra
\mathcal{B}
keeping all of its useful properties such as
availability of good norm-estimates,
spectral mapping theorems, Convergence Lemmas, compatibility with the other calculi, etc.
Moreover, it is desirable to cover all sectorial operators regardless of their sectoriality angle.

In this paper we will construct some functional calculi encompassing wider classes of functions (including some with singularities on
i\mathbb{R}
) and providing finer estimates for all negative generators of (sectorially) bounded holomorphic semigroups, and eventually for all sectorial operators.
Functional calculi for generators of some classes of bounded holomorphic semigroups were constructed in
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
28
]
, and
[
40
]
.
However, most of the results in those papers concern sectorial operators of angle zero, and the approaches there are based on fine estimates for the corresponding semigroups rather than fine analytic properties of resolvents.

Let
A
be a densely defined sectorial operator of sectorial angle
\theta_{A}\in[0,\pi)
on a Banach space
X
. It is well known that
-A
is the generator of a (sectorially) bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
if and only if
A
is sectorial and
\theta_{A}<\pi/2
(we may write
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
for this class).
In this paper we address the question whether the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus for
A
can be extended to more functions.
Since the resolvent of
A
satisfies the estimate

M_{\psi}(A):=\sup_{z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}}\|z(z+A)^{-1}\|<\infty,\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}.

for all
\psi\in(\theta_{A},\pi)
,
a direct way to define an appropriate function algebra would be to introduce a Banach space
of functions
f
which are holomorphic on sectors
\Sigma_{\psi}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\arg(z)|<\psi\}
such that

(1.5)

\|f\|_{\psi}:=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\frac{|f(z)|}{|z|}|dz|<\infty.

In order to apply this to all
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
,
f
should be holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
the assumption (
1.5
) should hold for all
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,
and in order to provide an estimate which is uniform in
\theta
it is desirable to have

\sup_{\psi\in(0,\pi/2)}\|f\|_{\psi}<\infty
. To our knowledge, no spaces of this type have been studied systematically in the literature, although they appear naturally in
[
38
, Appendix H2 and Chapter 10.2]
,
[
34
, Section 6]
or
[
33
, Appendix C]
. This class of functions is strictly included in each of the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s},s>0
(see Proposition
4.15
and a discussion following it), which we now define.

To define a functional calculus for all
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{s},s>-1
, be the linear space of all holomorphic functions
f
on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that

(1.6)

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})^{(s+1)/2}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha<\infty.

If
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, then there exists
a finite limit
f(\infty):=\lim_{|z|\to\infty,\,z\in{\Sigma}_{\psi}}\,f(z)
for all
\psi\in(0.\pi/2)
.

For every
s>-1
the space
\mathcal{D}_{s}
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}:=|f(\infty)|+\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},

is a Banach space, but not an algebra. However, the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
increase with
s
, and we prove in Lemma
3.21
that

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{s>-1}\mathcal{D}_{s}

is an algebra.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, so
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for some
s>-1
, and let
A
be sectorial with
\theta_{A}<\pi/2
.
Define

(1.7)

f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A):=f(\infty)-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)(A+\alpha-i\beta)^{-(s+1)}\,d\beta\,d\alpha.

Then
f_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)
whenever
\sigma>s
. The following result sets out other properties of this functional calculus. The proof will be given in Section
7
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let A be a densely defined, closed operator on a Banach space
X
such that
\sigma(A)\subset\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

A\in{\rm Sect}(\pi/2-);

(ii)

There is an algebra homomorphism
\Psi_{A}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\to L(X)
such that

\Psi_{A}((z+\cdot)^{-1})=(z+A)^{-1},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

and
\Psi_{A}
is bounded in the sense that there exist constants
C_{s},\,s>-1
, such that, for every
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,

(1.8)

\|\Psi_{A}(f)\|\leq C_{s}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}.

When these properties hold,
\Psi_{A}
is unique, and it is defined by the formula (
1.7
):

\displaystyle\Psi_{A}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\mapsto L(X),\qquad\Psi_{A}(f)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A),\quad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}.

The homomorphism
\Psi_{A}
will be called the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus
for
A
. It will be shown in Section
7
that the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus is compatible with the Hille-Phillips calculus and the holomorphic calculus for sectorial operators, and a spectral mapping theorem is given in Theorem
9.3
. Corollary
7.8
provides a version of this functional calculus based on the Banach algebra
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\cap\mathcal{D}_{s}
for a fixed value of
s
.

The
\mathcal{D}
-calculus defined as above does not take into account the sectoriality angle of
A\in{\rm Sect}(\pi/2-)
.
However, it can be used to construct a functional calculus which does not have this drawback.
To achieve this aim we introduce the Hardy-Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, on sectors
\Sigma_{\psi}
.
First, for any
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, we define the Hardy space
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
as the linear space of functions
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi})
such that

(1.9)

\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\psi}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|f(te^{i\varphi})|+|f(te^{-i\varphi})|\bigr)\,dt<\infty.

Note that
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
coincides with the classical Hardy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
in the right half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. It is well-known that
(H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi}),\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})})
is a Banach space, and every
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
has a boundary function on
\partial\Sigma_{\psi}
. The boundary function exists as the limit of
f
in both an
L^{1}
-sense and a pointwise (a.e.) sense. Moreover, the norm of
f
in
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
is attained by the
L^{1}
-norm of its boundary function. See Section
4.1
for a succinct approach to the Hardy spaces on sectors.

The space
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
induces the corresponding Hardy-Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
on
\Sigma_{\psi}
as

\mathcal{H}_{\psi}:=\left\{f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi}):f^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})\right\}.

Any function
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
has a finite limit
f(\infty):=\lim_{t\to\infty}f(t)
,
and moreover
f\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})
. Then
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
becomes a Banach algebra in the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}}:=\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})},\quad f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}.

The relationship between these spaces and the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all
s>-1
is set out in Corollary
4.3
and Lemma
4.13
; in particular, for each
s>-1
,
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
is contained in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{s}
is embedded in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for
\psi<\pi/2
.

To make use of the angle of sectoriality of
A
, we can adjust the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus to sectors as follows.
If
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
where
\psi\in(\theta_{A},\pi)
,
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
, and
f_{1/\gamma}(z):=f(z^{1/\gamma})
, then

f^{\prime}_{1/\gamma}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
f_{1/\gamma}(\infty)=f(\infty)
, and hence
f_{1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
. This observation allows us to extend the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus to the class of all sectorial operators, and makes the next definition (based on the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus) natural and plausible.

If
A
is sectorial and
\psi\in(\theta_{A},\pi)
, define

(1.10)

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A):=f(\infty)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{1/\gamma}(\alpha+i\beta)(A^{\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta)^{-1}\,d\beta\,d\alpha.

One can prove (see (
8.2
)) that

(1.11)

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+\frac{M_{\pi/2}{(A^{\gamma})}}{\pi}\|f_{1/\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\leq|f(\infty)|+M_{\pi/2}({A^{\gamma}})\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

Then (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) hold for any
\gamma\in(1,\pi/(2\theta))
, and the definition of
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
does not depend on the choice of
\psi
.

Now we are able to formalize our extension of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
A
be a densely defined operator on a Banach space
X
such that
\sigma(A)\subset\overline{\Sigma}_{\theta}
, where
\theta\in[0,\pi)
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta);

(ii)

For each
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
, there is a bounded Banach algebra homomorphism
\Upsilon_{A}:\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}\mapsto L(X)
such that

\Upsilon_{A}((z+\cdot)^{-1})=(z+A)^{-1},\quad z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
.

When these properties hold, the homomorphism
\Upsilon_{A}
is unique for each value of
\psi
, and it is defined by the formula (
1.10
):

\Upsilon_{A}:\mathcal{H}_{\psi}\to L(X),\qquad\Upsilon_{A}(f)=f_{\mathcal{H}}(A),\quad f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}.

The homomorphism
\Upsilon_{A}
will be called the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus
for
A
.

The
\mathcal{D}
-calculus can be given a more succinct form,
by replacing (
1.10
) with the somewhat more transparent
formula (
1.12
) below, inspired by results in
[
12
]
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
,
\theta<\psi<\pi
, and
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
. For
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
,
let

f_{\psi}(s):=\frac{f(e^{i\psi}t)+f(e^{-i\psi}t)}{2},\quad t\geq 0.

Then

(1.12)

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f(\infty)-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\psi}^{\prime}(t){\arccot}(A^{\gamma}/t^{\gamma})\,dt

where the integral converges in the uniform operator topology, and

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+M_{\psi}(A)\|f_{\psi}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq M_{\psi}(A)\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

Thus
\|\Upsilon_{A}\|\leq M_{\psi}(A)
.

See Section
8.2
for details. The
\mathcal{D}
-calculus and the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus possess natural properties of functional calculi such as spectral mapping theorems and Convergence Lemmas. These properties are studied in Section
9
.

The strength of the constructed calculi is illustrated by several examples showing
that they lead to sharper estimates than those offered by other calculi (see Section
12
for one example).
Moreover, the theory developed in this paper is successfully tested by deriving several significant estimates for functions of sectorial operators from the literature. In particular, in Section
10
, we provide a proof of permanence of the class of sectorial operators under subordination and we revisit a few basic results from semigroup theory.

In developing the
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{H}
-calculi we prove a number of results of independent interest in function theory. Apart from the theory of the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, their reproducing formulas, and boundedness of the associated operators elaborated in this paper, we emphasize the property (
4.9
) in Corollary
4.3
yielding isometric coincidence of spaces of Hardy type, Theorem
4.12
on Laplace representability of Hardy-Sobolev functions, and Theorem
5.10
on the density of rational functions in Hardy-Sobolev spaces.

Added Note:
During the preparation of this paper, we have become aware of a paper by Arnold and Le Merdy
[
5
]
who consider negative generators of bounded
C_{0}
-semigroups on Hilbert space. Inspired by ideas in
[
47
]
for the discrete case, they extend the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus for those operators, to a strictly larger Banach algebra
\mathcal{A}
in which
\mathcal{B}
is continuously embedded. Their extension is complementary to our extensions to the
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{H}
-calculi for negative generators of bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroups on Banach spaces. We are grateful to Loris Arnold for pointing out several defects in the original version of this paper.

2.
Preliminaries

Notation

Throughout the paper, we will use the following notation:

\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{C}_{+}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}z>0\}
,
\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}z\geq 0\}
,

\Sigma_{\theta}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:z\neq 0,|\arg z|<\theta\}
for
\theta\in(0,\pi)
.

For
f:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
, we say that
f
has a
sectorial limit at infinity
if

\lim_{|z|\to\infty,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}f(z)

exists for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
. Similarly,
f
has a
sectorial limit at
0
if

\lim_{|z|\to 0,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}f(z)

exists for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
. We say that
f
has a
half-plane limit at infinity
if

\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to\infty}f(z)

exists in
\mathbb{C}
. We say that
f
has a
full limit at infinity
or
at zero
if

\lim_{|z|\to\infty,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}f(z)\quad\text{or}\quad\lim_{|z|\to 0,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}f(z)

exists in
\mathbb{C}
. The notation
f(\infty)
and
f(0)
may denote a sectorial limit, a half-plane limit, or a full limit, according to context.

For
a\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we define functions on
\mathbb{C}
by

e_{a}(z)=e^{-az};\qquad r_{a}(z)=(z+a)^{-1},\,z\neq-a.

We use the following notation for spaces of functions or measures, and transforms, on
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

\operatorname{Hol}(\Omega)
denotes the space of holomorphic functions on an open subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{C}
,
H^{\infty}(\Omega)
is the space of bounded holomorphic functions on
\Omega
, and
\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Omega)}=\sup_{\Omega}|f(z)|
.

H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+}),\,1\leq p\leq\infty
, are the standard Hardy spaces on the (right) half-plane.

M(\mathbb{R}_{+})
denotes the Banach algebra of all bounded Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
under convolution. We identify
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with a subalgebra of
M(\mathbb{R}_{+})
in the usual way. We write
\mathcal{L}\mu
for the Laplace transform of
\mu\in M(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

\mathcal{LM}
is the Hille-Phillips algebra,
\mathcal{LM}:=\{\mathcal{L}\mu:\mu\in M(\mathbb{R}_{+})\}
, with the norm
\|\mathcal{L}\mu\|_{\mathrm{HP}}:=|\mu|(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and
\mathcal{L}L^{1}:=\{\mathcal{L}f:f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\}
.

dS
denotes area measure on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

For a Banach space
X
,
X^{*}
denotes the dual space of
X
and
L(X)
denotes the space of all bounded linear operators on
X
. The domain, spectrum and resolvent set of an (unbounded) operator
A
on
X
are denoted by
D(A)
,
\sigma(A)
and
\rho(A)
, respectively.

If
(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Y}})
are normed spaces of holomorphic functions on domains
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
, we will use notation as follows:

\mathcal{Y}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{X}
if
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
,
\mathcal{Y}
is a subset of
\mathcal{X}
and the inclusion map is continuous;

\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{X}
if
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
,
\mathcal{Y}
is a subset of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
inherits the norm from
\mathcal{X}
;

\mathcal{Y}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{X}
if
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}\supset\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
, and the restriction map
f\mapsto f|_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}
is a continuous map from
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
.

Boundaries of all of the sectors appearing in this paper will be oriented from top to bottom.

Elementary inequalities

We will need the following elementary lemma which gives lower bounds for
|z+\lambda|
in terms of
|z|
and
|\lambda|
, for
z
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(i)

Let
z=|z|e^{i\psi}
and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\in\mathbb{C}
, where
|\psi-\varphi|\leq\pi
. Then

(2.1)

|z+\lambda|\geq\cos\left(\frac{\psi-\varphi}{2}\right)(|z|+|\lambda|).

(ii)

Let
z\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi}
and
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\varphi}
, where
\psi,\varphi>0
and
\varphi+\psi<\pi
. Then

(2.2)

|z+\lambda|\geq\cos\left(\frac{\psi+\varphi}{2}\right)(|z|+|\lambda|).

(iii)

Let
z=|z|e^{i\psi}
and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\in\mathbb{C}
, where
|\psi|<\pi/2
and
|\varphi|\leq\pi/2
. Then

(2.3)

|z+\lambda|\geq\cos\psi\,|\lambda|,

and

(2.4)

|z+\lambda|\geq\cos\psi\,|z|.

Proof.

For (
2.1
), we may assume that
\varphi\geq\psi
and let
\theta:=(\pi-\varphi+\psi)/2\in[0,\pi/2]
. By applying a rotation of
\mathbb{C}
we may further assume that
\varphi=\pi-\theta
and
\psi=\theta
. Then

|z+\lambda|\geq\operatorname{Im}z+\operatorname{Im}\lambda=\sin\theta(|z|+|\lambda|)=\cos\left(\frac{\varphi-\psi}{2}\right)(|z|+|\lambda|).

The inequality (
2.2
) follows from (
2.1
), since
\psi+\varphi
is the maximum value of
|\psi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime}|
for
\psi^{\prime}\in[-\psi,\psi]
and
\varphi^{\prime}\in[-\varphi,\varphi]
.

The inequality (
2.4
) is obtained by considering
\operatorname{Re}(z+\lambda)
. For the inequality (
2.3
), we assume without loss of generality that
\sin\varphi\geq 0
. Note that

(2.5)

\displaystyle|\lambda+z|^{2}-|\lambda|^{2}\cos^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=\left(|z|+|\lambda|\cos(\varphi-\psi)\right)^{2}+|\lambda|^{2}\left(\sin^{2}\varphi-\cos^{2}(\varphi-\psi)\right)

\displaystyle=|z|^{2}+2|z|\,|\lambda|\cos(\varphi-\psi)+|\lambda|^{2}\sin^{2}\varphi.

If
\cos(\varphi-\psi)<0
, we have

\sin\varphi-|\cos(\varphi-\psi)|=\sin\varphi\,(1+\sin\psi)+\cos\varphi\cos\psi\geq 0.

Then the expression on the right-hand side of the first line of (
2.5
) is clearly non-negative. If
\cos(\varphi-\psi)\geq 0
, then the expression in the second line is clearly non-negative. This completes the proof.
∎

Beta function

The Beta function appears in many places in the paper. It is defined for
s,t>0
by

B(s,t)=B(t,s):=\int_{0}^{1}\tau^{s-1}(1-\tau)^{t-1}\,d\tau=2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{2s-1}\psi\sin^{2t-1}\psi\,d\psi.

In particular, for
s>-1
we will use the relations

B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\,d\psi=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(1+t^{2})^{(s+1)/2}}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma((s+1)/2)}{\Gamma(s/2+1)},

see
[
48
, p. 386]
. We note also the following limit properties:

\lim_{s\to-1}\,(s+1)\,B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)=2,\quad\lim_{s\to\infty}\,\sqrt{s}B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)=\sqrt{2\pi}.

Proof conventions

We will make extensive use of the dominated convergence theorem, often for vector-valued functions. With a few exceptions, we will not give details of the relevant dominating functions, as they are usually easily identified.

We will also use the following elementary lemma on several occasions. See
[
21
, p.21, Lemma 1]
for a proof.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space, and
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset L^{p}(\Omega,\mu)
, where
p\in[1,\infty)
. If
f_{n}\to f_{0}
a.e., and
\|f_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,\mu)}\to\|f_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,\mu)}
, then
\|f_{n}-f_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,\mu)}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

We will use Vitali’s theorem several times, usually for holomorphic vector-valued functions. We refer to the version given in
[
4
, Theorem A.5]
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Banach space of holomorphic functions on a domain
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
such that the point evaluations
\delta_{z}:f\mapsto f(z),\,z\in\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
, are continuous on
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
(\Omega,\mu)
be either an interval in
\mathbb{R}
with length measure or an open set in
\mathbb{C}
with area measure, and
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{X}
be a continuous function such that
\int_{\Omega}\|F(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}}\,d\mu(t)<\infty
.
Then the integral

G:=\int_{\Omega}F(t)\,d\mu(t)

exists as a Bochner integral in
\mathcal{X}
and it can be approximated by Riemann sums. It follows that
G
belongs to the closed linear span of
\{F(t):t\in\Omega\}
in
\mathcal{X}
.

Now assume that
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{X}
is locally bounded, where
\Omega
is an open set in
\mathbb{C}
, and that
\lambda\mapsto F(\lambda)(z)
is holomorphic on
\Omega
for all
z\in\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
.
We will use the fact that
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{X}
is holomorphic in the vector-valued sense, without further comment.
The result at this level of generality can be seen from
[
4
, Corollary A.7]
, using the point evaluations as separating functionals.
An alternative is to show that
F
is continuous, and then apply Morera’s theorem. If the definition of
F
is by an integral formula, it may also be possible to apply a standard corollary of the dominated convergence theorem which leads to an integral formula for the derivative
F^{\prime}
.

3.
The Banach spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and their reproducing formulas

In this section we introduce some spaces of holomorphic functions to which we will extend the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus of operators in Section
7
onwards.

3.1.
The spaces
\mathcal{V}_{s}

Let
s>-1
,
z=\alpha+i\beta
, and let
\mathcal{V}_{s}
be the Banach space of (equivalence classes of) measurable functions
g:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
such that the norm

(3.1)

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}:

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}|g(z)|}{|z|^{s+1}}\,dS(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|g(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})^{(s+1)/2}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(\rho e^{i\varphi})|\,d\rho\,d\varphi,

is finite, where
S
is the area measure on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
Note that

(3.2)

\mathcal{V}_{s}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}}\leq\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},\qquad g\in\mathcal{V}_{s},\;s<\sigma,

and

(3.3)

\int_{\Sigma_{\psi}}\frac{|g(z)|}{|z|}\,dS(z)\leq\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{\cos^{s}\psi}\right\}\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},\qquad g\in\mathcal{V}_{s},\,\psi\in(0,\pi/2).

The following property of functions from
\mathcal{V}_{s}
is an essential element in the arguments which lead to the representations for functions in
\mathcal{V}_{s}
in Proposition
3.7
and for
\mathcal{D}_{s}
in Corollary
3.10
, and eventually to the definition of a functional calculus for operators in (
7.5
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
be holomorphic, where
s>-1
. For every
k\geq 1
and every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,

(3.4)

\lim_{|z|\to\infty,\;z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}\,z^{k}g^{(k-1)}(z)=0.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
be holomorphic,
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,
\psi^{\prime}=(\pi/2+\psi)/2
and
b_{\psi}={\sin((\pi/2-\psi)/2)}=\cos\psi^{\prime}
. If
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
, then

\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\lambda-z|\leq b_{\psi}|z|\}\subset\{\lambda\in\Sigma_{\psi^{\prime}}:|\lambda|\geq(1-b_{\psi})|z|\}.

Let
r\in(0,b_{\psi}|z|)
. By Cauchy’s integral formula for derivatives,

g^{(k-1)}(z)=\frac{(k-1)!}{2\pi i}\int_{\{\lambda:\,|\lambda-z|=r\}}\frac{g(\lambda)}{(\lambda-z)^{k}}\,d\lambda.

Multiplying by
r^{k}
and integrating with respect to
r
over
(0,b_{\psi}|z|)
gives

\frac{(b_{\psi}|z|)^{k+1}}{k+1}|g^{(k-1)}(z)|\leq\frac{(k-1)!}{2\pi}\int_{\{\lambda:|\lambda-z|\leq b_{\psi}|z|\}}|g(\lambda)|\,dS(\lambda),

and then

|z|^{k}|g^{(k-1)}(z)|\leq\frac{(k+1)(k-1)!(1+b_{\psi})}{2\pi b_{\psi}^{k+1}}\int_{\{\lambda\in\Sigma_{\psi^{\prime}}:|\lambda|\geq(1-b_{\psi})|z|\}}\frac{|g(\lambda)|}{|\lambda|}\,dS(\lambda).

It now follows from (
3.3
) that
|z^{k}g^{(k-1)}(z)|\to 0
as
|z|\to\infty,\,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
.
∎

3.2.
The spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and the operators
Q_{s}

We now define a linear operator
Q_{s}
on
\mathcal{V}_{s},\,s>-1
. It will play a similar role to the operator
Q
on
\mathcal{W}
considered in
[
8
, Section 3]
, where
\mathcal{W}
is the Banach space of all (equivalence classes of) measurable functions
g:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

(3.5)

\|g\|_{\mathcal{W}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}|g(\alpha+i\beta)|\,d\alpha<\infty.

Indeed the definition of
Q_{1}
is formally the same as the definition of
Q
in
[
8
]
, but the domain
\mathcal{V}_{1}
of
Q_{1}
is larger than
\mathcal{W}
.

For
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
, let

(3.6)

(Q_{s}g)(z):=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+1}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\cup\{0\}.

By (
2.3
), the integral is absolutely convergent, and

(3.7)

|(Q_{s}g)(z)|\leq\frac{2^{s}\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}}{\pi\cos^{s+1}\psi},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\pi/2).

The dominated convergence theorem implies that
Q_{s}g
is continuous on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, with sectorial limits at infinity and
0
:

(3.8)

\lim_{|z|\to\infty,\,z\in{\Sigma}_{\psi}}\,(Q_{s}g)(z)=0,\qquad\psi\in(0,\pi/2),

and

(3.9)

\lim_{|z|\to 0,\,z\in{\Sigma}_{\psi}}\,(Q_{s}g)(z)=(Q_{s}g)(0),\qquad\psi\in(0,\pi/2).

Thus
Q_{s}g
is bounded and continuous on
\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
.
Moreover,
Q_{s}g
is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and

(3.10)

(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}(z)=(s+1)\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+2}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Using this, (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), we obtain that

(3.11)

|(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{(s+1)2^{s}}{\pi\cos^{s+2}\psi\,|z|}\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi},

and

|z(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{(s+1)2^{s}}{\pi\cos^{s+2}\psi}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}}\frac{|z|(\operatorname{Re}\lambda)^{s}\,|g(\lambda)|}{|z+\overline{\lambda}||\lambda|^{s+1}}\,dS(\lambda),\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

Using the dominated convergence theorem again, we obtain

(3.12)

\lim_{|z|\to 0,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}z(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}(z)=0.

We now give another formula for
Q_{s}
. Let
s=n+\delta>-1
where
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1,0\}
and
\delta\in[0,1)
, and let

(3.13)

C_{s}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(t+1)^{n+2}t^{\delta}}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-\tau)^{s}}{\tau^{\delta}}\,d\tau=B(1-\delta,s+1).

Then

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(\lambda+t)^{n+2}t^{\delta}}=\frac{C_{s}}{\lambda^{s+1}},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Indeed, both sides of this equation are holomorphic functions of
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and they coincide for
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
, so they coincide for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, by the identity theorem for holomorphic functions.
Putting
\lambda=z+\alpha-i\beta
, we obtain

(3.14)

(Q_{s}g)(z)=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi C_{s}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{s}\,g(\alpha+i\beta)\,d\beta\,d\alpha}{(z+\alpha-i\beta+t)^{n+2}}\,\frac{dt}{t^{\delta}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

For
s>-1
let
\mathcal{D}_{s}
be the linear space of all holomorphic functions
f
on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that

f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{s},

equipped with the semi-norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}:=\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}.

If
\sigma>s>-1
, then it is immediate from (
3.2
) that
\mathcal{D}_{s}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.
We will exhibit some functions in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
later in this section and in Section
3.3
.

In the rest of this section we will obtain a reproducing formula (
3.29
) for functions from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and we will describe some basic properties which will be relevant for the sequel. To this aim, we first define and study the behaviour of operators
Q_{s}
on
the scale of
\mathcal{D}_{s}
-spaces. Recall that
in
[
8
, Proposition 3.1]
we showed that
Q
maps
\mathcal{W}
into
\mathcal{B}
. However
Q_{s}
does not map the whole of
\mathcal{V}_{s}
into
\mathcal{D}_{s}
. For
s>-1
, a function
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
for which
Q_{s}g\notin\mathcal{D}_{s}
, can be defined as follows:

g(\rho e^{i\varphi}):=\left(\cos^{s+1}\varphi(\rho-\sin\varphi)\log^{2}(\rho-\sin\varphi)\right)^{-1},\\
1<\rho<2-\sin\varphi,\,\pi/4<\varphi<\pi/2,

and
g(z)=0
for all other points in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. We do not give details in this paper. Instead we will show in Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
that
Q_{s}
maps
\mathcal{V}_{s}
boundedly into
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
for any
\sigma>s
, and it maps holomorphic functions in
\mathcal{V}_{s}
into
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
We need the following auxiliary lemma which will be useful in a number of instances.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
h\in L^{1}[0,1]\cap L^{\infty}[1/2,1]
be a positive function.
Let
s>-1
,
\beta>1/2
, and

(3.15)

G_{h,\beta,s}(\varphi):=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{h(t)\,dt}{(t^{2}+2t\cos(\varphi+\psi)+1)^{\beta}}\,d\psi,\quad\varphi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2).

(a)

If
2\beta-s-2\leq 0
, then

(3.16)

K_{h,\beta,s}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\pi/2}\,G_{h,\beta,s}(\varphi)<\infty.

(b)

If
2\beta-s-2\geq 0
, then

(3.17)

\tilde{K}_{h,\beta,s}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\pi/2}\,\cos^{2\beta-s-2}\varphi\,G_{h,\beta,s}(\varphi)<\infty.

Proof.

Since
G_{h,\beta,s}(-\varphi)=G_{h,\beta,s}(\varphi)
, we may assume that
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2)
.
Now

\displaystyle G_{h,\beta,s}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{0}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{h(t)\,dt}{(t^{2}+2t\cos(\varphi+\psi)+1)^{\beta}}\,d\psi

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip 30.0pt+\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{h(t)\,dt}{(t^{2}+2t\cos(\varphi+\psi)+1)^{\beta}}\,d\psi

\displaystyle=:G_{h,\beta,s}^{-}(\varphi)+G_{h,\beta,s}^{+}(\varphi),

and we estimate these two integrals separately.

Since
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2)
,
\beta>1/2
, and
s>-1
, we have

\displaystyle G_{h,\beta,s}^{-}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\pi/2}^{0}\cos^{s}\psi\,d\psi\int_{0}^{1}h(t)\,dt=\frac{\|h\|_{L^{1}[0,1]}}{2}B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).

For the second integral, note that if
\phi=\varphi+\psi\in[0,\pi)
,

t^{2}+2t\cos\phi+1\geq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4},\quad&t\in[0,1/2],\\
(1-t)^{2}+1+\cos\phi,\quad&t\in[1/2,1].\end{cases}

Hence

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt\int_{0}^{1}\frac{h(t)}{(t^{2}+2t\cos\phi+1)^{\beta}}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq 4^{\beta}\int_{0}^{1/2}h(t)\,dt+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{h(t)}{((t-1)^{2}+(1+\cos\phi))^{\beta}}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq 4^{\beta}\|h\|_{L^{1}[0,1/2]}+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}[1/2,1]}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{(\tau^{2}+2\cos^{2}(\phi/2))^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{h,\beta}}{\cos^{2\beta-1}(\phi/2)},

for some constant
C_{h,\beta}>0
. Replacing
\varphi
by
\pi/2-\varphi
and
\psi
by
\pi/2-\psi
, and using

\omega\geq\sin\omega\geq\frac{2}{\pi}\omega,\qquad\omega\in(0,\pi/2),

we infer that if
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2)
, then

\displaystyle G^{+}_{h,\beta,s}(\pi/2-\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq C_{h,\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin^{s}\psi}{\sin^{2\beta-1}((\varphi+\psi)/2)}\,d\psi

\displaystyle\leq C_{h,\beta}\pi^{2\beta-1}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\psi^{s}}{(\varphi+\psi)^{2\beta-1}}\,d\psi.

In case (a), when
2\beta-s-2\leq 0
, we have

\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\psi^{s}}{(\varphi+\psi)^{2\beta-1}}\,d\psi\leq\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\psi^{s-2\beta+1}\,d\psi=\frac{\pi^{s-2\beta+2}}{(s-2\beta+2)2^{s-2\beta+2}}<\infty.

In case (b), when
2\beta-s-2\geq 0
,
we obtain

\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\psi^{s}}{(\varphi+\psi)^{2\beta-1}}\,d\psi\leq\varphi^{s+2-2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{s}}{(t+1)^{2\beta-1}}\,dt,

and hence

\cos^{2\beta-s-2}(\pi/2-\varphi)\,G_{h,\beta,s}(\pi/2-\varphi)\leq\varphi^{2\beta-s-2}G_{h,\beta,s}(\pi/2-\varphi)\\
\hbox{}\hskip-20.0pt\leq C_{h,\beta}\pi^{2\beta-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{s}}{(t+1)^{2\beta-1}}\,dt<\infty,

for some constant
C_{h,\beta}>0
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{D}_{s,0}
be the space of all functions
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
such that
f
has a sectorial limit
0
at infinity.
Then
(\mathcal{D}_{s,0},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}})
is a normed space, and we will see in Corollary
3.11
that it is a Banach space.

The following basic examples will play roles in several estimates later in the paper. We start with the resolvent functions and their powers.

Example 3.3
.

Let
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and
r_{\lambda}(z)=(z+\lambda)^{-1},z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Let
\gamma>0
, and consider

r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}(z):=(z+\lambda)^{-\gamma},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Let
s>-1
. Then

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{\left|\rho e^{i\psi}+|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\right|^{\gamma+1}}\,d\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{|\lambda|^{\gamma}}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{|\rho+e^{i(\varphi-\psi)}|^{\gamma+1}}\,d\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{|\lambda|^{\gamma}}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1+t^{\gamma-1}}{(t^{2}+2t\cos(\varphi+\psi)+1)^{(\gamma+1)/2}}\,dt\,d\psi,

where we have put
t=\rho
for
\rho\leq 1
and
t=\rho^{-1}
for
\rho>1
.
Now we apply Lemma
3.2
with
h(t)=1+t^{\gamma-1}
,
\beta=(\gamma+1)/2
, so
2\beta-s-2=\gamma-s-1
. Thus we obtain

(3.19)

\|r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\leq\begin{cases}\dfrac{\gamma K_{h,(\gamma+1)/2,s}}{|\lambda|^{\gamma}},&s>\gamma-1>-1,\\
\vskip 8.0pt\cr\dfrac{\gamma\tilde{K}_{h,(\gamma+1)/2,s}}{|\lambda|^{\gamma}\cos^{\gamma-s-1}\varphi},&\gamma-1>s>-1.\end{cases}

In particular, taking
\gamma=1
and a fixed
s>0
,

(3.20)

\|r_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos^{s}\psi}{|\lambda+\rho e^{i\psi}|^{2}}\,d\rho\,d\psi\leq\frac{C_{s}}{|\lambda|},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

This estimate will play a crucial role in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Next we consider some functions which appear frequently in the studies of holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroups.

Example 3.4
.

Let

f_{\nu}(z):=z^{\nu}e^{-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\quad\nu\geq 0.

We will show here that
f_{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
if and only if
s>\nu
.
Moreover, if
s>\nu
, then

(3.21)

\|f_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\leq 2B\left(\frac{s-\nu}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\nu+1).

This estimate will be crucial for operator estimates in Section
10
.

We have

f_{\nu}^{\prime}(z)=e^{-z}\left(\nu{z^{\nu-1}}-z^{\nu}\right)

and

\displaystyle\|f_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}{e^{-\rho\cos\varphi}}{\rho^{\nu-1}}\left|{\rho e^{i\varphi}}-\nu\right|\,d\rho\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s-\nu-1}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{\nu-1}|re^{-i\varphi}-\nu\cos\varphi|\,dr\,d\varphi.

We use the estimates

r|\sin\varphi|\leq|re^{-i\varphi}-\nu\cos\varphi|\leq r+\nu.

If
s>\nu
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|f_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s-\nu-1}\varphi\,d\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{\nu-1}(r+\nu)\,dr

\displaystyle=2B\left(\frac{s-\nu}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\nu+1).

If
s\leq\nu
, then

\|f_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\geq 2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s-\nu-1}\varphi\,\sin\varphi\,d\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\tau}\tau^{\nu}\,d\tau=\infty.

This establishes the claims above.

Finally we consider a function which will play an important role in our constructions of functional calculi in Section
8
.

Example 3.5
.

The function
\arccot
is defined by

(3.22)

\arccot(z)=\frac{1}{2i}\log\left(\frac{z+i}{z-i}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then
|\operatorname{Re}\arccot(z)|\leq\pi/2
,
\arccot
has sectorial limit
0
at infinity, and
its derivative is
-(z^{2}+1)^{-1}
.
It is easy to see that
\arccot\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all
s>-1
. For
s=0
, we have

(3.23)

\displaystyle\|\arccot\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0,0}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{|1+\rho^{2}e^{2i\varphi}|}\,d\varphi=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\psi}{(\rho^{4}+2\rho^{2}\cos\psi+1)^{1/2}}\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\psi}{\rho^{4}+2\rho^{2}\cos\psi+1}\right)^{1/2}\,d\rho=\sqrt{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\pi}{|\rho^{4}-1|}\right)^{1/2}\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}B(1/4,1/2)<3\pi.

See
[
48
, 2.5.16, (38)]
for the evaluation of the integral with respect to
\psi
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\sigma>s>-1
. The following hold.

(i)

\mathcal{D}_{s}
is continuously embedded in
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

(ii)

The restriction of
Q_{\sigma}
to
\mathcal{V}_{s}
is in
L(\mathcal{V}_{s},\mathcal{D}_{s})
.

(iii)

Q_{s}\in L(\mathcal{V}_{s},\mathcal{D}_{\sigma})
.

Proof.

The first statement is immediate from the definitions of the spaces and (
3.2
).

For the second statement, let
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
.
From (
3.1
), (
3.10
), and the second case of (
3.19
) with
\gamma=\sigma+1
and
h(t)=1+t^{\sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2^{\sigma}(\sigma+1)}\|Q_{\sigma}g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\quad

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\sigma+1}|g(te^{i\varphi})|}{|\rho e^{i\psi}+te^{-i\varphi}|^{\sigma+2}}\,d\rho\,d\varphi\,dt\,d\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma+1}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\sigma+1}\|r_{te^{-i\varphi}}^{\sigma+1}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}|g(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt\,d\varphi

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{K}_{h,(\sigma+2)/2,s}}{\cos^{\sigma-s}\varphi}|g(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=\tilde{K}_{h,(\sigma+2)/2,s}\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

This establishes the second statement.

For the third statement, the same estimation, but with
s
and
\sigma
interchanged, and using the first case of
(
3.19
) with
\gamma=s+1
and
h(t)=1+t^{s}
, shows that

\frac{\pi}{2^{s}(s+1)}\|Q_{s}g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,0}}\leq{K}_{h,(s+2)/2,\sigma}\|g\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

This establishes the third statement.
∎

For insight on why
\|r_{\lambda}^{\sigma+1}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}
appears in the proof above, we refer the reader to the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
be holomorphic, where
s>-1
. Then
Q_{s}g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}=g
.

Proof.

First we consider the case when
g\in\mathcal{V}_{n}
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Then
Q_{n}g
is holomorphic and (
3.6
) holds for
s=n
. It suffices to show that
(Q_{n}g)^{\prime}=g
. Let

\displaystyle I(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|g(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})^{(n+1)/2}}\,d\beta,\qquad\alpha>0,

\displaystyle J(\beta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{n}|g(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})^{(n+1)/2}}\,d\alpha,\qquad\beta\in\mathbb{R}.

From (
3.1
) and Fubini’s theorem, we see that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{n}I(\alpha)\,d\alpha<\infty
. Hence
I(\alpha)<\infty
for almost all
\alpha>0
and it follows that there exists a sequence
(\alpha_{j})_{j\geq 1}
such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{j}=\infty,\qquad\lim_{j\to\infty}I(\alpha_{j})=0.

Similarly
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}J(\beta)\,d\beta<\infty
, and so there exist sequences
(\beta_{k}^{\pm})_{k\geq 1}
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\beta_{k}^{\pm}=\pm\infty,\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}J(\beta_{k}^{\pm})=0.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
be fixed. Take
\alpha>0
with
I(\alpha)<\infty
, and let
j
be sufficiently large that
\alpha_{j}>2\alpha+\operatorname{Re}z
, and
k
be sufficiently large that
\beta_{k}^{-}<\operatorname{Im}z<\beta_{k}^{+}
. We may apply the Cauchy integral formula around the rectangle with vertices
\alpha+i\beta_{k}^{\pm}
and
\alpha_{j}+i\beta_{k}^{\pm}
, and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{n!}g^{(n)}(2\alpha+z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\beta_{k}^{-}}^{\beta_{k}^{+}}\frac{g(\alpha_{j}+i\beta)}{(\alpha_{j}+i\beta-z-2\alpha)^{n+1}}d\beta-\int_{\beta_{k}^{-}}^{\beta_{k}^{+}}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(\alpha+i\beta-z-2\alpha)^{n+1}}d\beta

\displaystyle\hbox{}\quad-i\int_{\alpha}^{\alpha_{j}}\frac{g(s+i\beta_{k}^{-})}{(s+i\beta_{k}^{-}-z-2\alpha)^{n+1}}\,ds+i\int_{\alpha}^{\alpha_{j}}\frac{g(s+i\beta_{k}^{+})}{(s+i\beta_{k}^{+}-z-2\alpha)^{n+1}}\,ds.

Letting
k\to\infty
, we obtain

(-1)^{n}\frac{2\pi}{n!}g^{(n)}(2\alpha+z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{n+1}}\,d\beta-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha_{j}+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha_{j}-i\beta)^{n+1}}\,d\beta.

Letting
j\to\infty
, we obtain

(3.24)

(-1)^{n}\frac{2\pi}{n!}g^{(n)}(2\alpha+z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{n+1}}\,d\beta.

This holds for almost all
\alpha>0
. Substituting this into (
3.6
), and then integrating by parts and using Lemma
3.1
, we infer that

(3.25)

\displaystyle(Q_{n}g)(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{2^{n+1}}{n!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{n}g^{(n)}(2\alpha+z)\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{n}g^{(n)}(\alpha+z)\,d\alpha=-\int_{0}^{\infty}g(\alpha+z)\,d\alpha.

By Lemma
3.1
, the integral

\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\prime}(\alpha+z)\,d\alpha
converges absolutely and uniformly for
z
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
So, differentiating under the integral sign we get
(Q_{n}g)^{\prime}=g
.

Now consider the case when
s=n+\delta>-1
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1,0\}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
g\in\mathcal{V}_{n}
is holomorphic. Then
g\in\mathcal{V}_{n+1}
,
n+1\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and (
3.24
) gives

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta+t)^{n+2}}\,d\beta=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{2\pi}{(n+1)!}g^{(n+1)}(2\alpha+z+t),

for
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
t>0
and almost all
\alpha>0
. We obtain from (
3.14
) and (
3.13
) that

\displaystyle C_{s}(Q_{s}g)(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{s}\,g(\alpha+i\beta)\,d\beta\,d\alpha}{(z+\alpha-i\beta+t)^{n+2}}\,\frac{dt}{t^{\delta}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\frac{2^{s+1}}{(n+1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}g^{(n+1)}(2\alpha+z+t)\,d\alpha\,\frac{dt}{t^{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(n+1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{t}^{\infty}(\tau-t)^{s}g^{(n+1)}(\tau+z)\,d\tau\,\frac{dt}{t^{\delta}}

\displaystyle=C_{s}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(n+1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{n+1}\,g^{(n+1)}(\tau+z)\,d\tau.

As in (
3.25
), it follows that
(Q_{s}g)^{\prime}=g
.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

If
g\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
is holomorphic, then
Q_{\sigma}g=Q_{s}g
for all
\sigma\geq s
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Proposition
3.7
and (
3.8
).
∎

Remark 3.9
.

The proof of the property
(Q_{n}g)^{\prime}=g^{\prime},n\geq 0,
in Proposition 3.7 uses just improper convergence of the integrals
\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{k}g^{(k)}(\alpha+z)\,d\alpha
for
0\leq k\leq n-1.

It is instructive to note that if
g\in\mathcal{V}_{n}
is holomorphic and
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, then

(3.26)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{k}|g^{(k)}(\alpha+z)|\,d\alpha<\infty

for all
k\geq 0.

Indeed if
g\in\mathcal{V}_{n},
then (
3.26
) holds for
k=n
by (
3.24
) and the definition of norm in
\mathcal{V}_{n}.
If
n\geq 1,
then using Lemma
3.1
,
we infer that
g^{(n-1)}(\alpha+z)=-\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}g^{(n)}(s+z)\,ds,
and hence
by Fubini’s theorem

\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{n-1}|g^{(n-1)}(\alpha+z)|\,d\alpha\leq\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{n}|g^{(n)}(\alpha+z)|d\alpha<\infty.

Repeating this argument, we conclude that (
3.26
) holds also for
k
such that
0\leq k<n.

If
k>n,
then (
3.26
) follows directly from (
3.24
) and the inclusion
\mathcal{V}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}_{k}.

The following representation of functions in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
has appeared in
[
2
, Corollary 4.2]
(see also
[
3
, Lemma 3.13.2]
for the case
s=1
).

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
. Then the sectorial limits

(3.27)

\displaystyle f(\infty):

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\to\infty,\;z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}\,f(z),

(3.28)

\displaystyle f(0):

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\to 0,\;z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}\,f(z),

exist in
\mathbb{C}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
. Moreover, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\cup\{0\}
,

(3.29)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=f(\infty)+(Q_{s}f^{\prime})(z)

\displaystyle=f(\infty)-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)\,d\beta}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+1}}\,d\alpha.

With
f(0)
defined as above,
f\in C(\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi})
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
3.7
that
(Q_{s}f^{\prime})^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
. The statements follow from (
3.8
) and (
3.9
).
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

For every
s>-1
the space
\mathcal{D}_{s}
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}:=|f(\infty)|+\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},

is a Banach space.

Proof.

Let
s>-1
be fixed and let
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
Then (
3.7
) and Vitali’s theorem imply that
(Q_{s}f_{k}^{\prime})_{k=1}^{\infty}
converges uniformly on each
\Sigma_{\psi}
to a limit
g
which is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Moreover
(f_{k}(\infty))_{k=1}^{\infty}
converges to a limit
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
. It follows from Proposition
3.7
that
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
converges uniformly on
\Sigma_{\psi}
to
f:=\zeta+g
. Then
(f^{\prime}_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
converges pointwise on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
to
g^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
. Applying Fatou’s lemma to the sequences
(\|f^{\prime}_{k}-f^{\prime}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}})_{n=k}^{\infty}
for fixed
k
, one sees that
\|f_{k}^{\prime}-f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}\to 0
. So
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. By (
3.8
),
f(\infty)=\zeta
and so
\|f_{k}-f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\to 0
,
k\to\infty
.
∎

The argument used in the proof of Corollary
3.11
also provides the following lemma of Fatou type (see also Lemma
4.11
).

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
s>-1
and
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{D}_{s}
be such that
\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}<\infty
and
f(z)=\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{k}(z)
exists for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

Now employing (
3.29
), the estimates (
3.7
), (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), and Lemma
3.1
, we obtain the following estimates.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
. For all
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
,

|f(z)|\leq\max\left(1,\frac{2^{s}}{\pi\cos^{s+1}\psi}\right)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi},

and

|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{(s+1)2^{s}}{\pi|z|\cos^{s+2}\psi}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

Moreover,
f
is continuous on
\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}
and

\lim_{|z|\to 0,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}zf^{\prime}(z)=0.

Remark 3.14
.

Corollary
3.13
implies that the point evaluation functionals
\delta_{z},\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, are continuous on
\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
. Using (
3.19
) and the principle set out in Section
2
, we see that the function
\lambda\mapsto r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}
is holomorphic from
\mathbb{C}_{+}
to
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, for any
s>-1
,
\gamma>0
.

3.3.
More functions in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and their properties

In this section we give more examples of functions from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and note some additional
elementary properties which will be relevant for the sequel.

Proposition 3.15
.

For
s>0
,
\mathcal{B}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

Proof.

For
s>0
and
f\in\mathcal{B}
we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=|f(\infty)|+\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{|\alpha+i\beta|^{s+1}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{\infty}+2\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\,|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(t^{2}+1)^{(s+1)/2}}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\max\{1,B(1/2,s/2)\}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Thus
\mathcal{B}
is continuously included in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

The representation (
3.29
) in Corollary
3.10
extends the reproducing formula for
\mathcal{B}
, i.e., (
3.29
) for
s=1
, to a larger class of functions.

Recall that
\mathcal{LM}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{B}
, see
[
7
, Section 2.4]
, Thus, in view of Proposition
3.15
, we have

\mathcal{LM}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{B}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s},\qquad s>0.

We will show in Corollary
5.2
that
\mathcal{B}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for every
s>0
, and hence
\mathcal{D}_{s}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
for
all
\sigma>s>0
. On the other hand, we will show in Corollary
5.2
that
\mathcal{B}
is not dense in
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{s}
for
s>0
.

For
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, let

\tilde{f}(z):=f(1/z),\qquad f_{t}(z):=f(tz),\qquad t>0,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
s>-1
and
t>0
. Then

(i)

f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
if and only if
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}

and

\|f-f(\infty)\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=\|\tilde{f}-f(0)\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}.

(ii)

If
f
is bounded away from
0
and
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, then
1/f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

(iii)

f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
if and only if
f_{t}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and
\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=\|f_{t}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{f}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\rho^{-1}e^{-i\varphi})|}{\rho^{2}}\,d\rho\,d\phi

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(re^{i\varphi})|\,dr\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

Moreover, by Corollary
3.10
,
\tilde{f}(\infty)=f(0)
.
This proves (i). The other statements are very easy.
∎

Remarks 3.18
.

1. Neither of the spaces
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is contained in the other. Indeed, the function
e^{-z}\in\mathcal{LM}\subset\mathcal{B}
but
e^{-z}\not\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
(see Example
3.4
). On the other hand, there are bounded functions in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
which are not in
\mathcal{B}
, for example the function
\exp(\arccot z)\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
and is bounded but it is not in
\mathcal{B}
(see Example
3.19
).

More generally, for
\nu\geq 0
, let
f_{\nu}(z)=z^{\nu}e^{-z},\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then
f_{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
if and only if
s>\nu
(see Example
3.4
). Note that, if
\nu>0
,
f_{\nu}
is not bounded on any right half-plane. One can show that if
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s_{0}}
, then

|f(z)|\leq\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s},0}\left(1+\frac{4\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)^{(s+1)/2},\quad z=\alpha+i\beta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

The function
\log(1+z)e^{-z}
is in
\mathcal{D}_{s},s>0
, but is also unbounded on every right half-plane.

2. Since
e^{-z}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for
s>0
, it follows from Lemma
3.17
that the functions
e^{-t/z}
are in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all
t>0,\,s>0
. This shows that functions
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
may not have full limits at infinity or at zero. However, the properties (
3.27
) and (
3.28
) in Corollary
3.10
establish values for
f
at infinity and at zero as sectorial limits.

3. The spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
, are invariant under shifts given by

(T(\tau)f)(z)=f(z+\tau),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Indeed these operators form a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
. See Section
11
for a proof. On the other hand,
\mathcal{D}_{s}
are not invariant under the vertical shifts when
\tau\in i\mathbb{R}
, as we see in the following example.

Example 3.19
.

As stated in Example
3.5
, the function
\arccot
is in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all
s>-1
.
Let

g(z)=\exp(\arccot(z)),\qquad\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

Since
|\operatorname{Re}\arccot(z)|\leq\pi/2
,
\|g\|_{\infty}=\exp(\pi/2)=g(\infty)
. For
s>-1
,
we have

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}

\displaystyle=|g(\infty)|+\|g\cdot(\arccot)^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}\leq\exp(\pi/2)(1+\|\arccot\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}).

Thus
g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all
s>-1
.

However the boundary function of
g
is not continuous at
z=\pm i
.
Indeed, for a fixed
\epsilon>0
,

\arccot(i+i\epsilon)=\frac{1}{2i}\log\left(1+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right),

hence

g(i+i\epsilon)=\exp{(-i\log(1+2/\epsilon)^{1/2})}

does not have a limit as
\epsilon\to 0+
.

Note that
g(\epsilon+i)
does not converge as
\epsilon\to 0+
. This means
that if
f(z):=g(z-i)
, then
f
does not have a sectorial limit at 0 and therefore does not belong to
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for any
s>-1
. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{s}
is not invariant under vertical shifts.

3.4.
Bernstein functions and
\mathcal{D}_{s}

Recall that a holomorphic function
g:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}_{+}
is a
Bernstein function
if it is of the form

(3.30)

g(z)=a+bz+\int_{(0,\infty)}(1-e^{-zs})\,d\mu(s),

where
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
and
\mu
is a positive Borel measure on
(0,\infty)
such that

\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{s}{1+s}\,d\mu(s)<\infty
.
The following properties of Bernstein functions
g
will be used (these properties differ slightly from those used in
[
6
]
):

(B1)

g
maps
\Sigma_{\psi}
into
\Sigma_{\psi}
for each
\psi\in[0,\pi/2]
, see
[
51
, Proposition 3.6]
or
[
6
, Proposition 2.1(1)]
.

(B2)

g
is increasing on
(0,\infty)
.

(B3)

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,

g(\operatorname{Re}z)\leq\operatorname{Re}g(z)\leq|g(z)|,\qquad|g^{\prime}(z)|\leq g^{\prime}(\operatorname{Re}z).

Here the first inequality follows from taking the real parts in (
3.30
), and the second inequality is shown in
[
7
, Section 3.5, (B3)]
.

Further information on Bernstein functions can be found in
[
51
]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
g
be a Bernstein function,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and

f(z;\lambda):=(\lambda+g(z))^{-1},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then
f(\cdot;\lambda)\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for
s>2
and

\|f(\cdot;\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\leq\frac{2^{s}}{(s-2)|\lambda|}.

Proof.

We have

f^{\prime}(z;\lambda)=-\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{(\lambda+g(z))^{2}},

and then, for
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
, using Lemma
2.1
, (B2) and (B3),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\psi};\lambda)|\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|g^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\psi})|}{|\lambda+g(\rho e^{i\psi})|^{2}}\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\cos^{2}((|\psi|+\pi/2)/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\prime}(\rho\cos\psi)}{(|\lambda|+|g(\rho e^{i\psi})|)^{2}}\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\cos^{2}((|\psi|+\pi/2)/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\prime}(\rho\cos\psi)}{(|\lambda|+g(\rho\cos\psi))^{2}}\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\cos^{2}((|\psi|+\pi/2)/2)\cos\psi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(|\lambda|+t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\cos^{2}((|\psi|+\pi/2)/2)\cos\psi}\cdot\frac{1}{|\lambda|}.

If
s>2
, then

\displaystyle|\lambda|\|f(\cdot;\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=|\lambda|\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\psi};\lambda)|\,d\rho\,d\psi

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\cos^{s-1}\psi}{\cos^{2}((\psi+\pi/2)/2)}\,d\psi=4\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\cos^{s-1}\psi}{1-\sin\psi}\,d\psi

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{1}(1+t)^{s-2}(1-t)^{s-3}\,dt<\frac{2^{s}}{s-2}.\qed

3.5.
Algebras associated with
\mathcal{D}_{s}

The spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
, are not algebras, but there are some related algebras. Consider the Banach spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}:=\mathcal{D}_{s}\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

Thus
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
is the space of bounded holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that

f(\infty):=\lim_{|z|\to\infty,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}\,f(z)

exists for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, and

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})^{(s+1)/2}}\,d\alpha\,d\beta<\infty.

Then
(\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}})
is a Banach algebra, and in particular

(3.31)

\|fg\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}},\quad f,g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}.

By Proposition
3.15
,
\mathcal{B}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
for
s>0
, and the embeddings are continuous.

Example
3.19
shows that the function
g(z):=\exp(\arccot z)
is in
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
for all
s>-1
, with
\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}
, and consequently
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
is not invariant under vertical shifts.

It follows from Lemma
3.17
that

f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\tilde{f}(z):=f(1/z)\in\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty},

and

\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}=\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}.

Moreover, the spectrum of
f
in
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
is the closure of the range of
f
. In particular, the spectral radius of
f
is
\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Now we consider the linear space

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{s>-1}\mathcal{D}_{s}.

We will show that
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
is an algebra, which opens the way to an operator functional calculus on
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

For
s,\sigma>-1
, let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
g\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.
Then

h:=fg\in\mathcal{D}_{s+\sigma+1},

and

(3.32)

\|h\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+\sigma+1}}\leq\left(2+\frac{2^{s}+2^{\sigma}}{\pi}\right)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\,\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}.

Hence,

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
is an algebra.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.13
, for
\rho>0
and
|\varphi|<\pi/2
, we have

\displaystyle|f(\rho e^{i\varphi})|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{s}}{\pi\cos^{s+1}\varphi}\right)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},

\displaystyle|g(\rho e^{i\varphi})|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{\sigma}}{\pi\cos^{\sigma+1}\varphi}\right)\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+\sigma+1,0}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s+\sigma+1}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})g(\rho e^{i\varphi})+f(\rho e^{i\varphi})g^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})\right|\,d\rho\,d\varphi

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{\sigma}}{\pi}\right)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}\,\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}+\left(1+\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\right)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\,\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,0}}.

This shows that
h\in\mathcal{D}_{q}
, and (
3.32
) follows easily.
∎

3.6.
Derivatives of functions in
\mathcal{D}_{s}

This section further clarifies the behaviour of the derivatives of functions from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and Lemma
3.22
is of independent interest. Corollary
3.23
will be used in Section
10.3
. For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, the notation
z^{m}f^{(n)}
denotes the function mapping
z
to
z^{m}f^{(n)}(z)
. Moreover,
f_{t}
is the function mapping
z
to
f(tz)
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
. Then

zf^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{s+1}
, and there exists
C^{\prime}_{s}
(independent of
f
) such that

(3.33)

\|zf^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}\leq{C}^{\prime}_{s}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}.

and

(3.34)

\|f_{t}-f_{\tau}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{s}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}}{\min\{t,\tau\}}|t-\tau|,\qquad t,\tau>0.

Proof.

Note that

\|zf^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}\leq\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s+1}}+\|zf^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s+1}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}+\|zf^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s+1}}.

So, for (
3.33
), it suffices to consider
\|zf^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s+1}}
.
The argument is similar to Example
3.3
and the proof of Proposition
3.6
.
By Corollary
3.10
,
for fixed
\sigma>s
,

f^{\prime\prime}(z)=-c_{\sigma}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho^{\sigma+1}f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})}{(z+\rho e^{-i\varphi})^{\sigma+3}}\,d\rho\,d\varphi,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

where
c_{\sigma}=(\sigma+1)(\sigma+2)\frac{2^{\sigma}}{\pi}
.
Then similar estimates to those in Example
3.3
and Proposition
3.6
give

\displaystyle c_{\sigma}^{-1}\|zf^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s+1}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}r\left|\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho^{\sigma+1}f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})}{(re^{i\psi}+\rho e^{-i\varphi})^{\sigma+3}}\,d\rho\,d\varphi\right|\,dr\,d\psi

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma}\varphi\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s+1}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})|\,dt\,d\rho}{(t^{2}+2t\cos(\varphi+\psi)+1)^{(\sigma+3)/2}}\,d\psi\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{\sigma-s}\varphi\,G_{h,\beta,s+1}(\varphi)\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})|\,d\rho\,d\varphi,

where
h(t)=t+t^{\sigma},\,t\in(0,1)
,
\beta=(\sigma+3)/2
and
G_{h,\beta,s+1}(\varphi)
is defined in (
3.15
), noting that
2\beta-(s+1)-2=\sigma-s
.
Now the estimate (
3.33
) follows from Lemma
3.2
(b).

For (
3.34
), let
g=f_{\tau}-f_{t}
. Without loss, assume that
0<t<\tau
. Then

g^{\prime}(z)=\tau f^{\prime}(\tau z)-tf^{\prime}(tz)=\int_{t}^{\tau}\left(f^{\prime}(rz)+rzf^{\prime\prime}(rz)\right)\,dr=\int_{t}^{\tau}\frac{d}{dz}(zf^{\prime}(rz))\,dr.

Hence, by Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}}\int_{t}^{\tau}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s+1}}{|z|^{s+2}}\left|\frac{d}{dz}(zf^{\prime}(rz))\right|\,dr\,dS(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\tau}\frac{\|zf^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}}{r}\,dr\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{s}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}}{t}(\tau-t),

since the
\mathcal{D}_{s+1}
-norm is invariant under the change of variable
z\mapsto rz
(Lemma
3.17
(iii)).
∎

The following corollary is easily proved by induction.

Corollary 3.23
.

If
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
z^{n}f^{(n)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
.

Remark 3.24
.

Lemma
3.22
is sharp in the sense that for any
s>0
and
\sigma\in(-1,s+1)
, there exist functions
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for which
zf^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. For example, the function
f_{\nu}(z):=z^{\nu}e^{-z}
has these properties if
\max\{0,\sigma-1\}<\nu<s
. This follows directly from Example
3.4
.

4.
Hardy-Sobolev algebras on sectors

4.1.
H^{p}
-spaces on the right half-plane and their norms

In this section and in Section
4.2
we will study the Hardy spaces
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
defined on sectors
\Sigma_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\pi)
.
The properties of such spaces
are similar to the properties of the classical Hardy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
,
though their theory seems to be more involved. The Hardy spaces
H^{p}(\Sigma_{\psi})
have been studied, mostly for
p>1
,
but the results are scattered around
various places in the literature, which is often obscure, and some proofs contain rather complicated,
incomplete or vague arguments. We propose a streamlined
(and probably new) approach avoiding the use of Carleson measures or log-convexity,
and we obtain a new result (Corollary
4.3
) on the way.
The case
p=1

does not require any significant adjustments as we illustrate below. Standard references for the theory of Hardy spaces on the right half-plane are
[
21
]
and
[
29
]
.

We set out the situation when
\psi=\pi/2
in this section, and the case of general
\psi
in Section
4.2
.
Although we are mainly interested
in
H^{1}
-spaces, we present statements
which are valid for
H^{p}
-spaces with
p\in[1,\infty)
, since the arguments are the
same for all such
p
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. The classical Hardy space
H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
in the right half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{+}
is defined as

H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})=\left\{g\in\mbox{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+}):\|g\|_{p}:=\sup_{\alpha>0}\,\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(\alpha+i\beta)|^{p}\,d\beta\right)^{1/p}<\infty\right\}.

It is well-known that
\|\cdot\|_{p}
is a norm on
H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
(H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+}),\|\cdot\|_{p})
is a Banach space.
Moreover, for almost every
t\in\mathbb{R}
there exists a sectorial limit

g(it):=\lim_{z\to it}g(z)
in
\mathbb{C}
. For every
g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
one has
\lim_{\alpha\to 0}g(\alpha+i\cdot)=g(i\cdot)
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
,
and
\|g\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}:=\|g\|_{p}=\|g(i\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}
.

One may also consider the normed space
(H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2}),\|\cdot\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})})
as the space of all

g\in\operatorname{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
such that

\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\pi/2}\,\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{p}\right)\,dt\right)^{1/p}<\infty.

and
(H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2}),\|\cdot\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})})
as

\left\{g\in\operatorname{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+}):\|g\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\pi/2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}\,dt\right)^{1/p}<\infty\right\}.

It is clear that
H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
and
H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
coincide as vector spaces, and

\|g\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\leq\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\leq 2^{1/p}\|g\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Then
H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})\subset H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and

(4.1)

\|g\|_{p}\leq\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})},\qquad g\in H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2}).

Proof.

Fix
p\in[1,\infty)
.
For fixed

\gamma\in(1/2,1)
define

g_{\gamma}(z):=(\gamma z^{\gamma-1})^{1/p}g(z^{\gamma})\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\pi/(2\gamma)}).

Note that

(4.2)

\int_{0}^{\infty}|g_{\gamma}(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(te^{i\gamma\varphi})|^{p}\,dt,

and

\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}|g_{\gamma}(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}\,dt\,d\varphi\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\|g\|^{p}_{{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}}.

By Fubini’s theorem and Hölder’s inequality there exist sequences

(t_{1,n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(t_{2,n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
0<t_{1,n}<t_{2,n}
,

t_{1,n}\to 0
,
t_{2,n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and

(4.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\,t_{1,n}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}|g_{\gamma}(t_{1,n}e^{i\varphi})|\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=0,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\,\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}|g_{\gamma}(t_{2,n}e^{i\varphi})|\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=0.

Let
\Omega_{n}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:\,t_{1,n}<|z|<t_{2,n}\}
. By Cauchy’s formula,

(4.5)

g_{\gamma}(z)=\frac{\alpha}{\pi i}\int_{\partial\Omega_{n}}\frac{g_{\gamma}(\lambda)}{(\lambda-z)(\lambda+\overline{z})}\,d\lambda,\quad z=\alpha+i\beta\in\Omega_{n},

for large
n
. Passing to the limit in (
4.5
) as
n\to\infty
, and using (
4.2
), (
4.3
) and (
4.4
),
we infer that
g_{\gamma}
satisfies the Poisson formula

(4.6)

g_{\gamma}(z)=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g_{\gamma}(it)}{(t-\beta)^{2}+\alpha^{2}}\,dt.

Hence, by Young’s inequality and (
4.2
), for every
\alpha>0
,

\|g_{\gamma}(\alpha+i\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|g_{\gamma}(i\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}.

Letting
\gamma\to 1
, Fatou’s Lemma implies (
4.1
).
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Then
H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
and

(4.7)

\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\leq\|g\|_{p},\qquad g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+}).

Proof.

First let
p=2
so that
g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Then, by

[
22
, Ch.VIII, p.508]
,
there exists

f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R},e^{\pi|t|}\,dt)
such that
f\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
, and for all
|\varphi|\leq\pi/2
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{2}\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{2\varphi t}f(t)\,dt.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{2}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{2}\bigr)\,dt

\displaystyle=2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\cosh(2\varphi t)f(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\cosh(\pi t)f(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(it)|^{2}+|g(-it)|^{2}\bigr)\,dt,

and (
4.1
) holds for
p=2
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty),\,p\not=2
, be fixed, and
g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Then

g(z)=B(z)\tilde{g}(z)
,
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, where
B
is the Blaschke product associated with
g
and
\tilde{g}
has no zeros in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then there is a well-defined holomorphic function
g_{p}(z)=[\tilde{g}(z)]^{p/2}
on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
g_{p}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Using (
4.1
)
for
p=2
,
for all
|\varphi|<\pi/2
, we have

(4.8)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{p}\bigr)\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g_{p}(te^{i\varphi})|^{2}+|g_{p}(te^{-i\varphi})|^{2}\bigr)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g_{p}(it)|^{2}\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(it)|^{p}\,dt=\|g\|_{p}^{p},

and (
4.7
) follows.
∎

Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2
imply the next statement.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Then
H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})=H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and for every
g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
,

(4.9)

\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}=\|g\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})},

and

(4.10)

\|g\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\leq\|g\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}\leq 2^{1/p}\|g\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}.

Note that the two-sided estimate (
4.10
) was proved in
[
50
]
and
[
42
]
in a more complicated way
(see also
[
1
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
45
]
,
[
54
]
,
[
55
]
).
The coincidence of norms in (
4.9
) seems not to have been noted before. It appears to be quite useful, as we will see in the proof of Corollary
4.5
below.

Remark 4.4
.

The two-sided estimate (
4.10
) is sharp (and cannot be improved). Indeed, let
p\in[1,\infty)
and let

f_{k}(z):=\frac{1}{\pi^{1/p}(z+1+ik)^{2/p}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Then for all
k
, we have
\|f_{k}\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}=1

and, by direct estimates,

\displaystyle\left(\|f_{k}\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\right)^{p}=\int_{0}^{\infty}|f_{k}(te^{-i\pi/2})|^{p}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(t-k)^{2}+1}\,dt=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\arctan k}{\pi}.

Thus,

\|f_{0}\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}=2^{1/p}\|f_{0}\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\frac{\|f_{k}\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}}{\|f_{k}\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}}=1.

In fact, for all
f\in\mathcal{H}^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
, the norm
\|f\|_{H^{p}_{*}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}
is attained at the boundary of
\Sigma_{\psi/2}
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
g\in H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
,
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Then there exist
g(\pm it):=\lim_{\varphi\to\pm\pi/2}g(te^{\pm i\varphi})
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

g(\pm i\cdot)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and

(4.11)

\lim_{\varphi\to\pm\pi/2}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(te^{i\varphi})-g(\pm it)|^{p}\,dt=0.

As a consequence, for every
g\in H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
,

(4.12)

\|g\|_{H^{p}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}=\|g(i\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

Proof.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. By Corollary
4.3
, it suffices to prove (
4.11
) for

g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
.
If
g\in H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, then as recalled above, for almost all
t\in\mathbb{R}

there exists a sectorial limit
g(it):=\lim_{z\to it}g(z)
, and
g(i\cdot)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
.
Therefore, we also have
\lim_{\varphi\to\pm\pi/2}g(te^{\pm i\varphi})=g(\pm it)
for almost all
t
.
Using this and Fatou’s Lemma, we infer from (
4.8
) that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varphi\to\pi/2}\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{p}\bigr)\,dt\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(it)|^{p}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varphi\to\pi/2}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{p}\bigr)\,dt,

and hence

(4.13)

\lim_{\varphi\to\pi/2}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|g(te^{i\varphi})|^{p}+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|^{p}\bigr)\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(it)|^{p}\,dt.

Then, by (
4.13
) and Lemma
2.2
, using once again
the pointwise a.e. convergence of
g(te^{\pm i\varphi})
to
g(\pm it)
as
\varphi\to\pm\pi/2
,
we obtain (
4.11
). Since
\|g\|_{H^{p}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}=\|g(i\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})},
we get (
4.12
) as well.
∎

For (formally) more general versions of (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) see (
4.15
) and (
4.17
) below.

4.2.
The spaces
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})

Now using the results of Section
4.1
for
\psi=\pi/2
, we develop basic properties of
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
for any
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. Define the Hardy space
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
on the sector
\Sigma_{\psi}
to be the space of all functions
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi})
such that

(4.14)

\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\psi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|f(te^{i\varphi})|+|f(te^{-i\varphi})|\bigr)\,dt<\infty.

We will also consider a non-symmetric version of
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
, defined as

H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi}):=\left\{f\in\operatorname{Hol}\,(\Sigma_{\psi}):\|f\|_{H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi})}:=\sup_{|\varphi|<\psi}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt<\infty\right\}.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\psi,\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in(0,\pi)
.

(i)

f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
if and only if
f\in H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi})
, and then

(4.15)

2^{-1}\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq\|f\|_{H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}.

(ii)

For any
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in(0,\pi)
, the map

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi_{1}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi_{2}})

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle\mapsto
