\displaystyle\frac{\psi_{1}}{\psi_{2}}z^{(\psi_{1}/\psi_{2})-1}f(z^{\psi_{1}/\psi_{2}})

is an isometric isomorphism of
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi_{1}})
onto
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi_{2}})
, and of
H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi_{1}})
onto
H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi_{2}})
.

(iii)

If
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
, then the limits

f(re^{\pm i\psi}):=\lim_{\varphi\to\pm\psi}f(re^{i\varphi})
exist a.e., and in the
L^{1}
-sense with respect to
r
. Moreover

(4.16)

f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\,\frac{f(\lambda)}{\lambda-z}\,d\lambda,\quad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

(iv)

If
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
, then

(4.17)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(|f(te^{i\psi})|+|f(te^{-i\psi})|\bigr)\,dt.

(v)

H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
and
H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi})
are Banach spaces.

Proof.

The proof of (i) is clear, and (ii) is a direct verification.
For
\psi=\pi/2
, the statements (iii) and (iv), excluding (
4.16
), were proved in Corollaries
4.3
and
4.5
, and (v) is well-known. Then the general cases are reduced to the case when
\psi=\pi/2
,
by means of (ii).

The Cauchy formula (
4.16
) is well-known for
\psi=\pi/2
(see, for example,
[
21
, Theorem 11.8]
). For general
\psi
, we may argue similarly to the proof of Lemma
4.1
, as follows.

Since
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\psi}^{\psi}|f(te^{i\varphi})|\,d\varphi\,dt<\infty.

Hence there exist sequences
(t_{1,n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(t_{2,n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
0<t_{1,n}<t_{2,n}
,
t_{1,n}\to 0,\,t_{2,n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{-\psi}^{\psi}t_{1,n}|f(t_{1,n}e^{i\varphi})|\,d\varphi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{-\psi}^{\psi}|f(t_{2,n}e^{i\varphi})|\,d\varphi=0.

By applying Cauchy’s theorem around the boundary of

\left\{z\in\partial\Sigma_{\psi-n^{-1}}:t_{1,n}<|z|<t_{2,n}\right\}

for large
n
, and taking the limit, we obtain (
4.16
).
∎

Remark 4.7
.

In addition to (
4.17
), it is also possible to prove that

\|f\|_{H^{1}_{*}(\Sigma_{\psi})}=\max\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(te^{-i\psi})|\,dt,\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(te^{i\psi})|\,dt\right).

This requires additional techniques, and it is not used in this paper.

4.3.
Functions with derivatives in
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})

For
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, let us introduce the space

\mathcal{H}_{\psi}:=\left\{f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi}):f^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})\right\}.

In view of Corollary
4.3
,

(4.18)

\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}=H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+}):=\{f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+}):f^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\},

and we may sometimes use the notation
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
instead of
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
.

Such function spaces are often called Hardy-Sobolev spaces, and we will also use
this terminology sporadically.
Spaces more general than
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
appear in
[
20
]
.
Namely, for
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi})
it was required in
[
20
]
that the boundary values of
f
exist and belong (after an appropriate “rescaling”) to a Besov space
B^{s}_{\infty,1},\,s>0
. One can develop a similar approach to those spaces, but we do not see much advantage in such generality within the present context.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\pi)
.

(i)

The function
f
extends to a continuous bounded function on
\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi}
.

(ii)

The limit

f(\infty):=\lim_{|z|\to\infty,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}}\,f(z)

exists.

(iii)

One has

(4.19)

\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq|f(\infty)|+\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}.

In particular, the evaluation functionals
\delta_{z},\,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
, are continuous on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.

Proof.

Let
\psi=\pi/2
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}=H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathcal{B}
by (
4.18
) and
[
7
, Proposition 2.4]
, the statement (i)
follows from
[
7
, Proposition 2.2(iv)]
, and (ii) follows from
[
7
, Proposition 2.4]
. In the general case, the map
f(z)\mapsto f(z^{2\psi/\pi})
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
onto
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, by Theorem
4.6
(ii), so (i) and (ii) hold for
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. The statement (iii) is easily seen.
∎

It follows from Theorem
4.8
that
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
is an algebra for every
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. We define a norm on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
by

(4.20)

\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}:=\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})},\qquad f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}.

This is easily seen to be an algebra norm. Theorem
4.8
(iii) shows that

(4.21)

\|f\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}:=|f(\infty)|+\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}

is an equivalent norm on
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})
.

The following lemma is simple, but crucial for our theory. The completeness of the norm is a standard fact, the scale-invariance is trivial, and the final isomorphism follows from Theorem
4.6
(ii).

Lemma 4.9
.

For every
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, the space
(\mathcal{H}_{\psi},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}})
is a Banach algebra. For
t>0
, the map
f(z)\mapsto f(tz)
is an isometric algebra isomorphism on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.
Moreover, for any
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in(0,\pi)
, the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\psi_{2}}

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle f(z^{\psi_{1}/\psi_{2}})

is an isometric algebra isomorphism.

We now give some examples of functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
which will play important roles in subsequent sections of this paper. The first example is of similar type to Example
3.3
.

Examples 4.10
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
.

1.

Let
\gamma>0
, and consider the function
r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}(z)=(\lambda+z)^{-\gamma},\,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
. Then

r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and

(4.22)

\|r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\frac{\gamma|dz|}{|z+\lambda|^{\gamma+1}}\leq\frac{2}{\sin^{\gamma+1}((\varphi-\psi)/2)\,|\lambda|^{\gamma}},

where we have used Lemma
2.1
. Thus
r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, and there exists
C_{\varphi,\psi,\gamma}
such that

(4.23)

\|r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq\frac{C_{\varphi,\psi,\gamma}}{|\lambda|^{\gamma}},\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\varphi},\,\varphi<\pi-\psi.

In particular, if
\gamma=1
, then

(4.24)

\|r_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq\frac{2}{\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)\,|\lambda|},\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\varphi}.

This property will be important for the proof of Lemma
5.7
and hence of Theorem
5.10
, and eventually of Theorem
1.2
(see Theorem
8.2
). A more general estimate will be given in Corollary
4.16
.

2.

Let
\gamma\in(0,\pi/(2\psi)
and
e_{\gamma,\lambda}(z):=e^{-\lambda z^{\gamma}},\,z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
.
Then
e_{\gamma,\lambda}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and

\|e_{\gamma,\lambda}\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}=\|e_{1,\lambda}\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma\psi}}\leq\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}|\lambda|e^{-\operatorname{Re}\lambda z}\,|dz|\leq\frac{1}{\cos(\varphi+\psi)}+\frac{1}{\cos(\varphi-\psi)}.

More examples can be found in Sections
4.5
and
4.6
. In particular, Lemma
4.13
shows that the restriction of any function in
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
to
\Sigma_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, belongs to
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.

The following lemma is a result of Fatou type closely related to Corollary
3.12
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
be such that
\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}<\infty
and
f(z):=\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{k}(z)
exists for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
, the functions
\{f_{k}:k\geq 1\}
are uniformly bounded on
\Sigma_{\psi}
. By Vitali’s theorem,
f
is holomorphic, and
f_{k}^{\prime}(z)\to f^{\prime}(z)
as
k\to\infty
for each
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
. By Fatou’s Lemma, for
|\varphi|<\psi
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f_{k}^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt\leq\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

Thus
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.
∎

4.4.
The spaces
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
\mathcal{L}L^{1}

In
[
7
, Proposition 2.4]
, we showed that
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{B}
. We will now show a stronger result that
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}\subset\mathcal{LM}
, where
(\mathcal{LM},\|\cdot\|)_{\mathrm{HP}}
is the Hille-Phillips algebra as in Section
2
. In particular, it shows that the Laplace transforms of singular measures on
(0,\infty)
are not in
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, which may be of interest.

Theorem 4.12
.

If
f\in H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
then there exists
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
f=f(\infty)+\mathcal{L}g
. Moreover, there is an absolute constant
C
such that

(4.25)

\|f\|_{\mathrm{HP}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}.

Proof.

Let
f\in H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\displaystyle f_{n}(z)

\displaystyle:=f(z)-f(z+n),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

\displaystyle g_{n}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2}{\pi}t(1-e^{-nt})\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha e^{-\alpha t}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)e^{-i\beta t}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\quad t>0.

Then
f_{n}\in H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and
\|f_{n}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}\leq 2\|f\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle|g_{n}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi}t(1-e^{-nt})\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha e^{-\alpha t}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}t(1-e^{-nt})\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha e^{-\alpha t}\,d\alpha=\frac{2}{\pi}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}\frac{(1-e^{-nt})}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n}{\pi}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}.

By the reproducing formula for
\mathcal{B}
(see Remark
3.16
or
[
7
, Proposition 2.20]
) and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle f_{n}(z)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)\left(\frac{1}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(z+n+\alpha-i\beta)^{2}}\right)d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(z+\alpha-i\beta)t}t(1-e^{-nt})\,dt\right)\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}g_{n}(t)\,dt,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

It follows that
(\pi/2)f_{n}^{\prime}
is the Laplace transform of
tg_{n}(t)
, and then
tg_{n}(t)
is the inverse Fourier transform of
(\pi/2)f_{n}^{\prime}(i\cdot)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. By Hardy’s inequality in the form of
[
21
, p.198]
,

(4.26)

\int_{0}^{\infty}|g_{n}(t)|\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|tg_{n}(t)|}{t}\,dt\leq\frac{\pi}{4}\|f_{n}^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})}.

Moreover,

f(z)=f(\infty)+\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(z),\qquad z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

and then by
[
49
, Theorem 1.9.2]

we obtain that

f\in\mathcal{LM}
and (
4.25
) holds.

Now let

u(z):=f^{\prime}(z+1)-\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}e^{-t}t\,d\mu(t),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Since
f\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
f^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, we have that
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Hence
u=\mathcal{L}h
for some
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, so

u(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}h(t)\,dt,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

From the uniqueness properties of Laplace transforms it follows that

-e^{-t}t\,\mu(dt)=h(t)\,dt.

Thus
\mu
is absolutely continuous on
(0,\infty)
, with Radon-Nikodym derivative
g.
Since
\mu
is a bounded measure,
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and

\mu(dt)=f(\infty)\delta_{0}+g(t)\,dt.

Hence
f=f(\infty)+\mathcal{L}g
.
∎

4.5.
The spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}

Since
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})=\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
(by Corollary
4.3
), we have shown in Theorem
4.12
that

\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}\subset\mathcal{LM}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{B}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}

if
s>0
. In the next lemma, we show that, for all
s>-1
,
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
. Moreover it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

(i)

If
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
, then
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
for every
s>-1
, and

(4.27)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle\leq B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{1,B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\right\}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}}.

(ii)

If
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
, then

f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, and

(4.28)

\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq\frac{2^{s}}{\pi\cos^{s+2}(\psi/2+\pi/4)}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}.

Thus, for all
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
and
s>-1
, there are natural continuous embeddings

\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{H}_{\psi}.

Note that the estimates (
4.23
) and (
4.27
) for functions
r_{\lambda}^{\gamma}
reproduce the estimate (
3.19
), with different constants.

Proof.

Let
s>-1
be fixed, and let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
. Using (
4.15
), we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt\,d\varphi

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\,d\varphi\right)\sup_{|\varphi|\leq\pi/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|\,dt

\displaystyle=B\left(\frac{s+1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})},

and (i) follows.

To prove (ii), note that if
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, then by Corollary
3.10
,

f^{\prime}(z)=\frac{(s+1)2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+2}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Hence using (
2.1
), for every
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt2^{-s}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(te^{\pm i\psi})|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(s+1)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{|te^{\pm i\psi}+\alpha-i\beta|^{s+2}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{s+1}{\pi\cos^{s+2}(\psi/2+\pi/4)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{(t+|\alpha+i\beta|)^{s+2}}\right)\,dt\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi\cos^{s+2}(\psi/2+\pi/4)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{|\alpha+i\beta|^{s+1}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi\cos^{s+2}(\psi/2+\pi/4)}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},

and (
4.28
) follows.
∎

For a function
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\psi})
,
\gamma>0
and
0<\varphi\leq\min\{\pi,\psi/\gamma\}
, define
f_{\gamma}\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
by

f_{\gamma}(z):=f(z^{\gamma}),\quad z\in\Sigma_{\varphi}.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
, and let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}^{\infty}\cap\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
for all
\sigma>-1
. Moreover, for each
s>-1
and
\sigma>-1
, there exist constants
C_{s,\sigma,\gamma}
and
\tilde{C}_{s,\gamma}
such that

\|f_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}^{\infty}}\leq C_{s,\sigma,\gamma}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|f_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}}\leq\tilde{C}_{s,\gamma}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.13
, (i) and (ii), and Lemma
4.9
, we see firstly that
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi\gamma/2}
, and then
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}^{\infty}
. Moreover each of the embeddings from Lemma
4.13
is continuous, and the map
f\mapsto f_{\gamma}
is isometric.
∎

Now we relate the spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to another class of spaces used in the literature on functional calculi.
For
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, let

(4.29)

\mathcal{E}_{\psi}:=\left\{f\in{\operatorname{Hol}}(\Sigma_{\psi}):\|f\|_{\psi}:=\sup_{\varphi\in(0,\psi)}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\frac{|f(z)|}{|z|}|dz|<\infty\right\}.

It is easy to see that
(\mathcal{E}_{\psi},\|\cdot\|_{\psi})
is a Banach space,
and that

\mathcal{E}_{\psi}=\{f\in{\operatorname{Hol}}(\Sigma_{\psi}):f(z)/z\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})\}.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{E}_{\psi}
and let
g(z):=f(z)/z
.
Then, for every
\varphi\in(0,\psi)
,

(4.30)

\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{\pi-\psi-\varphi}{\sin(\psi+\varphi)}+\frac{\pi-\psi+\varphi}{\sin(\psi-\varphi)}\right)\|g\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})},

where we set
\frac{0}{\sin 0}:=1.
Thus,
\mathcal{E}_{\psi}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{H}_{\varphi},\,\varphi\in(0,\psi)
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>0
.

Proof.

By Cauchy’s theorem, for every
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
,

f^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\,\frac{\lambda g(\lambda)\,d\lambda}{(z-\lambda)^{2}},

Hence, for every
\varphi\in(0,\psi)
, by Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{i\varphi})|+|f^{\prime}(\rho e^{-i\varphi})|\right)\,d\rho\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}|g(\lambda)|\,J(\lambda,\varphi)\,|d\lambda|,

where

\displaystyle J(\lambda,\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{|\lambda|}{|\rho e^{i\varphi}-\lambda|^{2}}+\frac{|\lambda|}{|\rho e^{-i\varphi}-\lambda|^{2}}\right)\ d\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{2}-2\rho\cos(\psi+\varphi)+1}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{\rho^{2}-2\rho\cos(\psi-\varphi)+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi-\psi-\varphi}{\sin(\psi+\varphi)}+\frac{\pi-\psi+\varphi}{\sin(\psi-\varphi)},

in view of
[
48
, p.311, (25)]
. Hence
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}

and (
4.30
) follows. Thus,
\mathcal{E}_{\psi}\overset{r}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
for all
\varphi\in(0,\psi)
.
Recalling Lemma
4.13
and that
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}=H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, we have proved the inclusion
\mathcal{E}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>0
,
as well.
∎

Note that the inclusions in Proposition
4.15
are strict. Indeed, if
f(z)=z(z+1)^{-1}
then one has

f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, but
f\notin\mathcal{E}_{\psi}
for any
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. Moreover, if

f(z)=\frac{z\,e^{-z}}{(z+1)\log^{2}(z+2)},

then
z^{-1}f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and
f\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
, but

f\not\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for any
s\in(-1,0)
.

The spaces
\mathcal{E}_{\psi}
are studied in
[
38
, Chapter 10]
;
see also
[
38
, Appendix H2]
,
[
34
, Section 6]
and
[
33
, Appendix C]
.
To ensure the algebra property and to relate the spaces to the
H^{\infty}
-calculus, the authors considered the algebras
H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})\cap\mathcal{E}_{\psi}
. Lemma
4.15
shows that the spaces
\mathcal{E}_{\psi}
are fully covered within the framework of the algebras
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. These algebras will be associated to the more powerful functional calculi constructed in Corollary
7.8
and Theorem
8.2
.

4.6.
Bernstein functions and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

To illustrate the relevance of the Hardy-Sobolev spaces, we show that the “resolvent” of a Bernstein function belongs to an appropriate Hardy-Sobolev space. This observation will be used in Section
10
to provide a new proof of the permanence of subordination for holomorphic semigroups, one of the main results of
[
31
]
; see also
[
6
]
and
[
7
]
.

Let
g
be a Bernstein function,
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
.
Let

f(z,\lambda):=(\lambda+g(z))^{-1},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, it follows from Lemmas
3.20
and
4.13
that
f(\cdot,\lambda)\in\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}
, and
\|f(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq C_{\psi}/|\lambda|
, where
C_{\psi}
is independent of
g
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. In order to obtain the correct angle, we will need to extend this to
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\varphi}
, where
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi/2)
.

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
g
be a Bernstein function,
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\varphi}
. Then

(4.31)

\|f(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}}\leq 2\left(\frac{1}{\sin(\min(\varphi,\pi/2))}+\frac{2}{\cos\psi\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\right)\frac{1}{|\lambda|}.

Proof.

For fixed
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
, and

\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\varphi}
, observe that

\|f^{\prime}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{|g^{\prime}(te^{i\psi})|}{|\lambda+g(te^{i\psi})|^{2}}+\frac{|g^{\prime}(te^{-i\psi})|}{|\lambda+g(te^{-i\psi})|^{2}}\right)\,dt.

Using the property (B1) for Bernstein functions and (
2.1
), we have

|\lambda+g(te^{\pm i\psi})|\geq\sin((\varphi-\psi)/2)(|\lambda|+|g(te^{\pm i\psi})|),\qquad t\geq 0.

Moreover, in view of (B3), for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|g(te^{\pm i\psi})|\geq\operatorname{Re}g(te^{\pm i\psi})\geq g(t\cos\psi)\quad\text{and}\quad|g^{\prime}(e^{\pm i\psi}t)|

\displaystyle\leq g^{\prime}(t\cos\psi).

Using (B2), we have

\displaystyle\|f^{\prime}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\prime}(t\cos\psi)}{(|\lambda|+g(t\cos\psi))^{2}}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\cos\psi\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{(|\lambda|+s)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\cos\psi\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\frac{1}{|\lambda|}.

Thus
f(\cdot,\lambda)\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and
f(\infty,\lambda)=(\lambda+g(\infty))^{-1}
. Since
|\arg\lambda|<\pi-\varphi
and
g(\infty)\in[0,\infty]
,

(4.32)

|f(\infty,\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{\sin(\min(\varphi,\pi/2))|\lambda|}.

Now (
4.31
) follows from Theorem
4.8
(ii).
∎

4.7.
Representations for functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

In this section we derive a reproducing formula for functions from
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

and obtain certain alternative representations for its kernel.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\pi)
. Let
\gamma=\frac{2\psi}{\pi}
and

(4.33)

f_{\gamma}(z):=f(z^{\gamma}),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then

(4.34)

f(z)=f(\infty)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{z^{1/\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}.

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, Lemma
4.9
implies that
f^{\prime}_{\gamma}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Hence, by Lemma
4.13
and Theorem
4.8
,
we have
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}\cap C(\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Then, in view of Corollary
3.10
,

f_{\gamma}(z)=f_{\gamma}(\infty)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{z+\alpha-i\beta}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\cup\{0\},

and (
4.34
) follows.
∎

Corollary 4.18
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
If
f_{\gamma}
is given by (
4.33
), then

(4.35)

f(z)=f(\infty)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{z^{1/\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\quad z\in\Sigma_{\pi\gamma/2}\cup\{0\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
,
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi\gamma/2}
, so (
4.35
) follows from (
4.34
).
∎

The next reproducing formula for functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
resembles

[
12
, Lemma 7.4]
, and it was, in fact, inspired by
[
12
, Lemma 7.4]
. In particular, this formula replaces the double (area) integral in
(
4.34
) with a line integral, it involves boundary values of
f^{\prime}
rather than scalings of
f^{\prime}
(such as in (
4.34
)), and it offers a different kernel which might sometimes be easier to deal with. The
\arccot
function has been defined in (
3.22
).

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}
,
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. Let
\nu=\pi/(2\psi)
, and

(4.36)

f_{\psi}(t):=\frac{f(e^{i\psi}t)+f(e^{-i\psi}t)}{2},\quad t>0.

Then

(4.37)

f(z)=f(\infty)-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\psi}^{\prime}(t)\,{\arccot}(z^{\nu}/t^{\nu})\,dt,\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}.

Proof.

Since
\arccot\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
(see Example
3.5
), (
3.29
) shows that

(4.38)

\arccot(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(\lambda+u-iv)^{-1}}{(u+iv)^{2}+1}\,dv\,du,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Let
\gamma=1/\nu
, and
f_{\gamma}
be given by (
4.33
).
By Proposition
4.17
, for
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}
,

(4.39)

f(z)-f(\infty)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{z^{\nu}+\alpha-i\beta}\,d\beta\,d\alpha.

It follows from Lemma
4.9
that
f_{\gamma}^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})=H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, so by Cauchy’s formula for functions in
H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})

[
21
, Theorem 11.8]
, we have, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,

\displaystyle f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(it)}{\lambda-it}\,dt+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(it)}{\lambda+it}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{tf_{\gamma}^{\prime}(it)}{\lambda^{2}+t^{2}}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{i\gamma}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{i\pi(\gamma-1)/2}f^{\prime}(e^{i\pi\gamma/2}t^{\gamma})t^{\gamma}-e^{-i\pi(\gamma-1)/2}f^{\prime}(e^{-i\pi\gamma/2}t^{\gamma})t^{\gamma}}{\lambda^{2}+t^{2}}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{i\psi}f^{\prime}(e^{i\psi}t^{\gamma})+e^{-i\psi}f^{\prime}(e^{-i\psi}t^{\gamma})\right)\frac{t^{\gamma}}{\lambda^{2}+t^{2}}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\psi}^{\prime}(s)s^{\nu}}{\lambda^{2}+s^{2\nu}}\,ds.

Therefore, by (
4.39
)
and (
4.38
), we obtain

\displaystyle f(z)-f(\infty)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{\psi}(s)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{s^{\nu}\,d\beta\,d\alpha}{(z^{\nu}+\alpha-i\beta)((\alpha+i\beta)^{2}+s^{2\nu})}\right)\,ds

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{\psi}(s)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\beta\,d\alpha}{((z/s)^{\nu}+\alpha-i\beta)((\alpha+i\beta)^{2}+1)}\right)\,ds

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{\psi}(s)\arccot(z^{\nu}/s^{\nu})\,ds,

so (
4.37
) holds for
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}
.
∎

Proposition
4.19
motivates a more careful study of the kernel
\arccot(z^{\nu})
. The integral representation of this kernel will be crucial in deriving fine estimates for the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus for operators in Section
8.2
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let

\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
\nu={\pi}/{(2\psi)}
.
Then

(4.40)

\displaystyle\arccot(z^{\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}V_{\psi}(z,t)\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|\,\frac{dt}{t}+\frac{1}{4i}\int_{\Gamma_{\psi}}\,\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda-z},\quad z\in\Sigma_{\psi},

where

(4.41)

\displaystyle V_{\psi}(z,t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{e^{-i\psi}}{z-te^{-i\psi}}+\frac{e^{i\psi}}{z-te^{i\psi}}\right),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\psi}

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda:|\lambda|=1,\,\arg\lambda\in(\psi,2\pi-\psi)\}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\arccot(z^{\nu})=\frac{1}{2i}\log\left|\frac{z^{\nu}+i}{z^{\nu}-i}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\arg\left(\frac{z^{\nu}+i}{z^{\nu}-i}\right),\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

Since
\nu\psi=\pi/2
, for every
t>0,\,t\neq 1
,

\lim_{z\in\Sigma_{\psi},\,z\to te^{i\psi}}\arccot(z^{\nu})=\frac{1}{2i}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\arg\left(\frac{t^{\nu}+1}{t^{\nu}-1}\right),

and

\lim_{z\in\Sigma_{\psi},\,z\to te^{-i\psi}}\arccot(z^{\nu})=-\frac{1}{2i}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\arg\left(\frac{t^{\nu}-1}{t^{\nu}+1}\right).

Here,

\arg\left(\frac{t^{\nu}+1}{t^{\nu}-1}\right)=\arg\left(\frac{t^{\nu}-1}{t^{\nu}+1}\right)=\pi\chi_{(0,1)}(t),\quad t>0,\,t\neq 1,

where
\chi_{[0,1]}
is the characteristic function of
(0,1)
.
So,

(4.42)

\lim_{z\in\Sigma_{\psi},\,z\to te^{\pm i\psi}}\arccot(z^{\nu})=\mp\frac{i}{2}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|+\frac{\pi}{2}\chi_{[0,1]}(t),

Now fix
z\in\Sigma_{\psi}
. Using

\limsup_{|\lambda|\to\infty,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\,|\lambda\arccot\lambda|<\infty
, it follows from
Cauchy’s theorem that

\arccot(z^{\nu})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\,\frac{\arccot(\lambda^{\nu})}{\lambda-z}\,d\lambda,\quad\int_{\lambda\in\partial\Sigma_{\psi},|\lambda|<1}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda-z}=\int_{\Gamma_{\psi}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda-z}.

Thus by (
4.42
),

\displaystyle\arccot(z^{\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|\left(\frac{1}{t-e^{i\psi}z}+\frac{1}{t-e^{-i\psi}z}\right)\,dt+\frac{1}{4i}\int_{\Gamma_{\psi}}\,\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda-z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}V_{\psi}(z,t)\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|\,\frac{dt}{t}+\frac{1}{4i}\int_{\Gamma_{\psi}}\,\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda-z}.\qed

Remark 4.21
.

Letting
z\to 0
in (
4.40
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|\frac{dt}{t}+\frac{\pi-\psi}{2},

hence

(4.43)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\nu}}{1-t^{\nu}}\right|\frac{dt}{t}=\frac{\psi}{2}.

5.
Dense sets in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

In this section we establish some results concerning density and approximations in our spaces.

5.1.
Dense subsets of
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and some applications

Let
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
be the linear span of
\{r_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
be the sum of
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and the constant functions.
Using Example
4.10
(1) and Lemma
4.13
, we have

(5.1)

\mathcal{\widetilde{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s},\quad s>-1.

Theorem 5.1
.

The space

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+}):=\left\{a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}(\lambda_{k}+z)^{-1}:n\in\mathbb{N},\;a_{k}\in\mathbb{C},\;\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\right\}

is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for each
s>-1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
be the closure of
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
and let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. It follows from Example
3.3
and Remark
3.14
(or a direct estimate) that the function

(R_{f})(\lambda):=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}f^{\prime}(\lambda)r_{\overline{\lambda}}

is continuous from
\mathbb{C}_{+}
to
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and it is Bochner integrable with respect to area measure
S
on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Since point evaluations are continuous on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
(Remark
3.14
), it follows from Corollary
3.10
that

Q_{s}f^{\prime}=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}}R_{f}(\lambda)\,dS(\lambda)

as the Bochner integral of a continuous function. Hence
Q_{s}f^{\prime}
belongs to the closure in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
of the linear span of the range of the integrand, which is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. Now
f=f(\infty)+Q_{s}f^{\prime}
which is in the closure of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
∎

From Proposition
3.6
, we have the continuous inclusion

\mathcal{D}_{s}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\quad\text{if $\sigma>s>-1$},

and from (
5.1
), Theorem
4.12
and Proposition
3.15
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}\subset\mathcal{LM}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{B}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{D}_{s}\quad\text{if $s>0$}.

Here
\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}
is the sum of
\mathcal{L}L^{1}
and the constant functions, and it is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{LM}
. The following density results hold.

Corollary 5.2
.

1.

If
\sigma>s>-1
, then
\mathcal{D}_{s}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

2.

For
s>-1
, the spaces
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
are dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

3.

For
s>0
, the spaces
\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}
,
\mathcal{LM}
and
\mathcal{B}
are dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

4.

For
s>0
, the spaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
,
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
,
\mathcal{L}L^{1}+\mathbb{C}
,
\mathcal{LM}
and
\mathcal{B}
are not dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

The first three statements are immediate from Theorem
5.1
.

Since any function in
\mathcal{B}
extends continuously to
i\mathbb{R}
, the same holds for the closure of
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
when
s>0
. The function

f(z)=e^{-1/z}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
for
s>0
,
(see Remarks
3.18
and Example
3.4
with
\nu=0
), but
f
is not continuous at
z=0
. This establishes the final statement.
∎

The function
g(z)=\exp(\arccot z)
considered in Example
3.19
provides another example of a function from
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\infty}
which is discontinuous on
i\mathbb{R}
, and so does not belong to the closure of
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{s}
for
s>0
.

In order to obtain operator norm-estimates for functions
f^{(n)}
applied to semigroup generators (see Theorem
10.3
), we will need a stronger version of Corollary
3.23
on differentiability of
t\to f(t\cdot)
in the
D_{s}
-norm.
We first prove a lemma, and we present the stronger statement in Corollary
5.5
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
\tau>0
,
t\in(\tau/2,2\tau)
, and define

g_{t,\tau,\lambda}(z):=\frac{r_{\lambda}(tz)-r_{\lambda}(\tau z)}{t-\tau}-zr_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\tau z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then, for every
s>-1
,

(5.2)

\lim_{t\to\tau}\,\|g_{t,\tau,\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=0.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle g_{t,\tau,\lambda}^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{t}{(tz+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{\tau}{(\tau z+\lambda)^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{t-\tau}+\frac{1}{(\tau z+\lambda)^{2}}-\frac{2\tau z}{(\tau z+\lambda)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau tz^{2}-\lambda^{2}}{(tz+\lambda)^{2}(\tau z+\lambda)^{2}}+\frac{\lambda-\tau z}{(\tau z+\lambda)^{3}}\to 0,\qquad t\to\tau.

Hence

|g_{t,\tau,\lambda}^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{C_{\tau,\lambda}}{1+|z|^{2}},\qquad t\in(\tau/2,2\tau),\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

for some
C_{\tau,\lambda}
. Since

\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{1+\rho^{2}}\,d\psi<\infty

for any
s>-1
, the dominated convergence theorem implies (
5.2
).
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
. For
\tau>0
, let
zf^{\prime}_{\tau}
denote the function mapping
z
to
zf^{\prime}(\tau z)
. Then

\lim_{t\to\tau}\left\|\frac{f_{t}-f_{\tau}}{t-\tau}-zf^{\prime}_{\tau}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}=0.

Proof.

Let
\tau>0
be fixed, and

(R_{t,\tau}f)(z):=\frac{f(tz)-f(\tau z)}{t-\tau}-zf^{\prime}_{\tau},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,t>\tau/2.

By Lemma
3.22
,
\{R_{t,\tau}:t>\tau/2\}
is a bounded subset of
L(\mathcal{D}_{s},\mathcal{D}_{s+1})
. By Lemma
5.3
,

\lim_{t\to\tau}\|R_{t,\tau}r_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s+1}}=0,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Since the linear span of the functions
r_{\lambda}
and the constants is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
(see Theorem
5.1
), the assertion follows.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1
, and let

G(t)(z):=f(tz),\qquad F_{n}(t)(z):=z^{n}f^{(n)}(tz),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\,t>0,\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then
G
and
F_{n}
map
(0,\infty)
into
\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
,
G
is
n
-times differentiable as a function from
(0,\infty)
to
\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
, and

F_{n}=G^{(n)}.

Proof.

Firstly,
f(tz)\in\mathcal{D}_{s}\subset\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
, so
G
maps
(0,\infty)
into
\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
.

The proof is by induction on
n
. The case
n=1
is given by Corollary
5.4
. Assume that
G^{(n)}=F_{n}
with values in
\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
, and let
f_{n}(z)=z^{n}f^{(n)}(z)
. Then

G^{(n)}(t)(z)=F_{n}(t)(z)=t^{-n}f_{n}(tz).

By Corollary
5.4
applied to
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
,
G^{(n)}
is differentiable with respect to
t
, when considered as a function with values in
\mathcal{D}_{s+n+1}
. Finally,

G^{(n+1)}(t)(z)=\frac{d}{dt}(z^{n}f^{(n)}(tz))=z^{n+1}f^{(n+1)}(tz)=F_{n+1}(t)(z).\qed

5.2.
Approximations via change of variables

Here we consider approximations of
f
from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
by the functions
f_{\gamma}(z)=f(z^{\gamma})
as
\gamma\to 1-
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. The following hold.

1.

Let
s>-1
, and
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. Then

(5.3)

\|f_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},

and

(5.4)

\lim_{\gamma\to 1-}\|f_{\gamma}-f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=0.

2.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. Then

(5.5)

\lim_{\gamma\to 1-}\|g_{\gamma}-g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}=0.

Proof.

1.
First let
g\in H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
. If
0<\psi<\varphi\leq\pi/2
, then it follows from Theorem
4.6
(iv) that

(5.6)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|g^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|+|g^{\prime}(te^{-i\varphi})|\right)\,dt\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|g^{\prime}(te^{i\psi})|+|g^{\prime}(te^{-i\psi})|\right)\,dt.

Hence

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}

\displaystyle=|g(\infty)|+\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|g^{\prime}(te^{i\varphi})|+|g(te^{-i\varphi})|\right)\,dt\,d\varphi

\displaystyle\geq|g(\infty)|+\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\varphi\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(|g^{\prime}(te^{i\gamma\varphi})|+|g^{\prime}(te^{-i\gamma\varphi})|\right)\,dt\,d\varphi

\displaystyle=\|g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
(see Corollary
5.2
), it follows that the map
g\mapsto g_{\gamma}
extends to a contraction on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and this contraction maps
f
to
f_{\gamma}
. Then (
5.3
) holds.

Now (
5.4
) follows from (
5.5
), (
5.3
), and the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{\pi/2}
is continuously and densely embedded in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
(see Proposition
4.13
and Corollary
5.2
).

2. Since the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}
are equivalent and
g_{\gamma}(\infty)=g(\infty)
, it suffices to show that

\|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}-g^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}|g_{\gamma}^{\prime}(z)-g(z)|\,|dz|\to 0.

By Lemma
4.9
, we have

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\gamma\psi}}|g^{\prime}(z)|\,|dz|.

Applying Theorem
4.6
(iii) to
g^{\prime}
,

\lim_{\gamma\to 1-}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\gamma\psi}}|g^{\prime}(z)|\,|dz|=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}|g^{\prime}(z)|\,|dz|

and

\lim_{\gamma\to 1-}g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)=g^{\prime}(z),\quad\text{for almost all $z\in\partial\Sigma_{\psi}$}.

Now the statement (
5.5
) follows from Lemma
2.2
.
∎

5.3.
Density of rational functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

In Theorem
5.1
and Corollary
5.2
, we established that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
and several larger spaces are dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, for
s>-1
or
s>0
. In particular, we noted that
H^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and

\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}=\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}:\,f(\infty)=0\},

with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}}=\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}.

By (
4.19
), this norm is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{\psi})
be the linear span of
\{r_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}\}
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi})
be the closure of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{\psi})
in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
.
We will prove that
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi})
. Thus the rational functions which vanish at infinity and have simple poles outside
\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi}
are dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
modulo constants.
This fact may be known, but we did not find it in the literature. Our proof involves several lemmas given below and it may be of interest as a piece of function theory. The following lemma, relating to the function spaces
\mathcal{E}_{\varphi}
from Definition (
4.29
), is the key step in our proof.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)

and let
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
.
If

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\frac{|f(\lambda)|}{|\lambda|}\,|d\lambda|<\infty,

then

(5.7)

f\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi}).

Proof.

From (
4.22
) for
\gamma=1
, the function
F(\lambda):=r_{\lambda}
maps
\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
into
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
, and is locally bounded. Moreover
F
is holomorphic (see Theorem
4.8
(iii) and Section
2
) and its derivative is
-r_{\lambda}^{2}
.

The Cauchy integral formula (
4.16
) may be written as

f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}f(\lambda)F(-\lambda)(z)\,d\lambda,\quad z\in\Sigma_{\psi}.

From (
4.24
), we obtain

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\|f(\lambda)F(-\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}}\,|d\lambda|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\frac{|f(\lambda)|}{|\lambda|}\,d\lambda<\infty.

Thus

f=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}f(\lambda)F(-\lambda)\,d\lambda

as a Bochner integral in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
, with continuous integrand, so it may be approximated in the
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
-norm by Riemann sums of the integrand, which lie in
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{\psi})
.
Hence
f\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi})
.
∎

The next step in the proof is to construct a family of functions which serve as an approximate identity for
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}
when restricted to any sector smaller than
\Sigma_{\varphi}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\varphi\in(0,\pi)
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and let

(5.8)

g_{\epsilon}(z):=\frac{2z^{\epsilon}}{1+z^{\epsilon}}(1+\epsilon z)^{-2},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0].

Then
g_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}\cap H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
and

(5.9)

\sup_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}\|g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}<\infty.

Moreover,

(5.10)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,g_{\epsilon}(z)=1,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0],

and, for
0<a<b<\infty
, there exists
C_{\varphi,a,b}
such that

(5.11)

|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(z)|\leq C_{\varphi,a,b}\cdot\epsilon,\quad z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi},\,|z|\in(a,b).

Proof.

It is clear that
g_{\epsilon}\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
and
g_{\epsilon}(\infty)=0
.
Moreover

g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(z)=\frac{2\epsilon z^{\epsilon}}{z(1+z^{\epsilon})^{2}(1+\epsilon z)^{2}}-\frac{4\epsilon z^{\epsilon}}{(1+z^{\epsilon})(1+\epsilon z)^{3}}.

Applying Lemma
2.1
, there is a constant
C_{\varphi}

such that, for
z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}
and
t=|z|
,

(5.12)

|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(z)|\leq C_{\varphi}\epsilon t^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{2}{t(1+t^{\epsilon})^{2}(1+\epsilon t)^{2}}+\frac{4}{(1+t^{\epsilon})(1+\epsilon t)^{3}}\right).

Hence

\displaystyle\|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon C_{\varphi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\epsilon}\,dt}{t(1+t^{\epsilon})^{2}(1+\epsilon t)^{2}}+4\epsilon C_{\varphi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\epsilon}\,dt}{(1+t^{\epsilon})(1+\epsilon t)^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon C_{\varphi}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(1+\epsilon t)^{2}}+2\epsilon C_{\varphi}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\epsilon-1}\,dt+4\epsilon C_{\varphi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(1+\epsilon t)^{3}}

\displaystyle=6C_{\varphi}.

This yields (
5.9
).

The property (
5.10
) is straightforward, and (
5.11
) follows from (
5.12
).
∎

Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
enable us to show that any function
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}
, when restricted to
\Sigma_{\psi},\,\psi\in(0,\varphi)
, can be approximated by rational functions (with simple poles) in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
, and let

f\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}.

Then

(5.13)

f\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi}).

Proof.

Assume first that
f\in H_{\varphi,0}
and
f(0)=0
, and let
g_{\epsilon}
be defined by
(
5.8
).
Then

fg_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}\cap H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
,
and

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\frac{|f(z)g_{\epsilon}(z)|}{|z|}\,|dz|<\infty.

By Lemma
5.7
,
fg_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi})
.

Note that

\|f(1-g_{\epsilon})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}}\leq\|f^{\prime}(1-g_{\epsilon})\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}+\|fg^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}.

We will prove that

(5.14)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\|f^{\prime}(1-g_{\epsilon})\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}=0,

and

(5.15)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\|fg^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}=0.

By (
4.19
) and (
5.9
),

\sup_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}\|g_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}\leq\sup_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}\|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}<\infty,

so, using (
5.10
) and
the dominated convergence theorem, we have

\|f^{\prime}(1-g_{\epsilon})\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)(1-g_{\epsilon}(\lambda))|\,|d\lambda|\to 0,\quad\epsilon\to 0.

For
0<a<b<\infty
,

\displaystyle\|fg^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}|f(\lambda)||g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\lambda)|\,|d\lambda|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|z|>b,\,z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\,|f(z)|\,\int_{|\lambda|>b,\,\lambda\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)|\,|d\lambda|

\displaystyle\hbox{}\qquad+\sup_{|z|<a,\,z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\,|f(z)|\,\int_{|\lambda|<a,\,\lambda\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)|\,|d\lambda|

\displaystyle\hbox{}\qquad+\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}\int_{|\lambda|\in(a,b),\,\lambda\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)|\,|d\lambda|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{|z|>b,\,z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\,|f(z)|+\sup_{|z|<a,\,z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\,|f(z)|\right)\|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}

\displaystyle\hbox{}\qquad+2(b-a)\|f\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}\sup_{|z|\in(a,b),\,z\in\partial\Sigma_{\varphi}}\,|g^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(z)|.

Letting first
\epsilon\to 0
, using (
5.9
)-(
5.11
) along with Vitali’s theorem, and then letting
a\to 0
and
b\to\infty
, using
f(0)=f(\infty)=0
, we obtain

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\|fg^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi})}=0.

We have now proved the assertions (
5.14
) and (
5.15
). Thus we obtain (
5.13
) under the additional assumption that
f(0)=0
.

Now let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}
be arbitrary. Then consider

f_{0}(z):=f(z)-2f(0)\left(\frac{1}{z+1}-\frac{1}{z+2}\right),

and note that

f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi,0}\cap H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varphi}),\qquad f_{0}(0)=0.

Then, by the above,

f_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\varphi}),

and hence (
5.13
) holds.
∎

We now approximate functions
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
by functions from
\mathcal{H}_{\psi^{\prime},0},\,\psi^{\prime}>\psi
, using the change of variables from Proposition
5.6
.

For
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
and
f_{\gamma}(z)=f(z^{\gamma})
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we now have

f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{\gamma},0},\qquad\varphi_{\gamma}:=\min\{\gamma^{-1}\psi,\pi\}>\psi.

By (
5.5
),

\lim_{\gamma\to 1-}\,\|f-f_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}}=0.

By Lemma
5.9
,
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi})
. Thus we obtain the following result that the rational functions with simple poles are dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. Then

(5.16)

\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma_{\psi}).

6.
Convergence Lemmas

In this section we formulate Convergence Lemmas for functions in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, composed with fractional powers.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
s>-1
and
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{D}_{s}
be such that

\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}<\infty,

and for every
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
there exists

f_{0}(z):=\lim_{k\to\infty}\,f_{k}(z).

Let
g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
satisfy

g(0)=g(\infty)=0.

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
, let

(6.1)

f_{k,\gamma}(z)=f_{k}(z^{\gamma}),\quad g_{\gamma}(z)=g(z^{\gamma}),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Then

(6.2)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=0.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.12
,
f_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. So without loss of generality,
we can assume that
f_{0}=0
.
By Corollary
4.14
,
g_{\gamma}
and
f_{k,\gamma}
belong to the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
, so
f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. Moreover

B:=\sup_{k\geq 1}\left(\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}+\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\infty}\right)<\infty.

Let
0<r<R<\infty
and
\Omega_{r,R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:r\leq|z|\leq R\}
. By Vitali’s theorem,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\left(|f_{k,\gamma}(z)|+|f_{k,\gamma}^{\prime}(z)|\right)=0

uniformly on
\Omega_{r,R}
. Therefore the integrals

\int_{\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}_{k,\gamma}(z)g_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z),\quad\int_{\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f_{k,\gamma}(z)g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z),

tend to
0
as
k\ \to\infty
. Moreover,

\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}_{k,\gamma}(z)g_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z)\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}|g_{\gamma}(z)|\,\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},

and

\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f_{k,\gamma}(z)g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z)\leq\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z).

Hence

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\|f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\leq B\left(\sup_{\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}|g_{\gamma}(z)|+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Omega_{r,R}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,dS(z)\right).

Letting
r\to 0
and
R\to\infty
, we obtain the assertion (
6.2
).
∎

The following result is a convergence lemma for
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, analogous to Lemma
6.1
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
be such that

\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}<\infty,

and for every
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
there exists

f_{0}(z):=\lim_{k\to\infty}\,f_{k}(z).

Let
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
satisfy

g(0)=g(\infty)=0.

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N},
let
f_{k,\gamma}
and
g_{\gamma}
be given by (
6.1
).
Then

(6.3)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}=0.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Lemma
6.2
.

By Lemma
4.11
,
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. Thus, we will assume that
f_{0}=0
.
Let
\gamma\in(\psi/\pi,1)
. By Lemma
4.9
,

g_{\gamma}
and
f_{k,\gamma}
belong to the algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\psi/\gamma}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, so
f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. Moreover

B:=\sup_{k\geq 1}\left(\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}+\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\right)<\infty.

Let
0<r<R<\infty
and
I_{r,R}=\{z\in\partial\Sigma_{\psi}:r\leq|z|\leq R\}
. By Vitali’s theorem,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\left(|f_{k,\gamma}(z)|+|f_{k,\gamma}^{\prime}(z)|\right)=0

uniformly on
I_{r,R}
. Therefore the integrals

\int_{I_{r,R}}|f^{\prime}_{k,\gamma}(z)g_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|,\quad\int_{I_{r,R}}|f_{k,\gamma}(z)g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|,

tend to
0
as
k\ \to\infty
. Moreover,

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|f^{\prime}_{k,\gamma}(z)g_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|\leq\sup_{z\in\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|g_{\gamma}(z)|\,\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}},

and

\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|f_{k,\gamma}(z)g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|\leq\|f_{k,\gamma}\|_{H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|.

Hence

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\|f_{k,\gamma}g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq B\left(\sup_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|g_{\gamma}(z)|+\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}\setminus I_{r,R}}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(z)|\,|dz|\right).

Letting
r\to 0
and
R\to\infty
, we obtain (
6.3
).
∎

7.
The
\mathcal{D}
-calculus and its compatibility

Here we discuss functional calculus for sectorial operators
A
of angle less than
\pi/2
and functions
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. Since
f
is bounded on a closed sector containing the spectrum of
A
(Corollary
3.10
),
f(A)
may be considered via the extended holomorphic (sectorial) calculus. If
A
is injective then
f(A)
can be defined that way as a closed operator, but we will show that
f(A)
is a bounded operator when
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. Our methods provide estimates for
\|f(A)\|
, and we will adapt the results in Section
8
to take account of the angle of sectoriality, by using fractional powers of operators (cf. Corollary
4.14
).

Recall that a densely defined operator
A
on a Banach space
X
is
sectorial of angle

\theta\in[0,\pi/2)
if
\sigma(A)\subset\overline{\Sigma}_{\theta}
and, for each
\varphi\in(\theta,\pi]
,

(7.1)

M_{\varphi}(A):=\sup\left\{\|z(z+A)^{-1}\|:z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\varphi}\right\}<\infty.

The
sectorial angle

\theta_{A}
of
A
is the minimal such
\theta
. Note that
M_{\varphi}(A)
is a decreasing function of
\varphi
.

Let
\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
stand for the class of all sectorial operators of angle
\theta
for
\theta\in[0,\pi/2)
on Banach spaces, and denote
\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-):=\bigcup_{\theta\in[0,\pi/2)}\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
. Then
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
if and only if
-A
generates a (sectorially) bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
of angle
(\pi/2)-\theta_{A}
, in the sense that the semigroup has a holomorphic extension to
\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\theta_{A}}
which is bounded on each smaller subsector. Note that these semigroups are sometimes called
sectorially
bounded holomorphic semigroups in the literature. However, in this paper, we will adopt the convention that bounded holomorphic semigroups are bounded on sectors. We will denote the semigroup as
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
, and
e^{-tA}
then agrees with
e_{t}(A)
defined in the Hille-Phillips calculus, where
e_{t}(z)=e^{-tz}
. One may consult
[
35
]
for the general theory of sectorial operators, and
[
4
, Section 3.7]
for the theory of holomorphic semigroups.

Let
A
be a closed, densely defined operator on a Banach space
X
such that

(7.2)

\sigma(A)\subset\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M_{A}:=M_{\pi/2}(A)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\|z(z+A)^{-1}\|<\infty.

Then
\|A(z+A)^{-1}\|\leq M_{A}+1,\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and Neumann series (see
[
53
, Lemma 1.1]
) imply that
\sigma(A)\subset\Sigma_{\theta}\cup\{0\}
and

(7.3)

\|z(z+A)^{-1}\|\leq 2M_{A},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\theta},

where

\theta:=\arccos(1/(2M_{A}))<\pi/2.

So
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)\subset\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
. Conversely, if
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
where
\theta\in[0,\pi/2)
, then (
7.2
) holds. Thus
-A
generates a bounded holomorphic semigroup if and only if
(
7.2
) holds. The constant
M_{A}
is a basic quantity associated with
A
,
and we call it the
sectoriality constant
of
A
. Note that
M_{tA}=M_{A}
for all
t>0
.

A set
S
of sectorial operators on the same Banach space
X
is
uniformly sectorial
of angle
\theta
if
S\subset\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
and, for each
\varphi\in(\theta,\pi)
, there exists
C_{\varphi}
such that
M_{\varphi}(A)\leq C_{\varphi}
for all
A\in S
. Thus
S
is uniformly sectorial of some angle
\theta<\pi/2
if and only if each
A\in S
satisfies (
7.2
) and
\sup_{A\in S}M_{A}<\infty
.

In the presentation of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus that follows, we assume that the reader is familiar with the holomorphic functional calculus for sectorial operators, as in
[
35
]
, and in particular with the Hille-Phillips calculus for negative generators of bounded
C_{0}
-semigroups. We will make extensive use of fractional powers of sectorial operators in the form
(A+z)^{-\gamma}
where
\gamma>0
. If
\gamma
is not an integer, these operators are fractional powers which can be defined in many ways (see
[
44
]
), including using the holomorphic functional calculus (see
[
35
, Chapter 3]
). All these approaches are consistent with each other.
Since
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}_{n}
, it is possible to define the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus without using fractional powers, and this would simplify some proofs (for example, Lemma
7.1
becomes trivial, and the formulas (
7.4
) and (
7.8
) would not be needed). Thus we could define the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus without using fractional powers, and in particular we could define the fractional powers
(z+A)^{-\gamma}
for all
\gamma>0
.
This definition would be consistent with other definitions (see Theorem
7.4
). Then we could define the
\mathcal{D}_{s}
-calculus for all
s>-1
in the way described below, using fractional powers in (
7.5
).

The following simple lemma for fractional powers is a version of the moment inequality applied to the sectorial operator
(A+z)^{-1}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and
\gamma>0
. Let
\lceil\gamma\rceil
be the ceiling function of
\gamma
, i.e. the smallest integer in
[\gamma,\infty)
. Then

\|(A+z)^{-\gamma}\|\leq M_{A}^{\lceil\gamma\rceil}/|z|^{\gamma},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Proof.

When
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}
, the estimate is trivial.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. By the compatibility of our calculus with the holomorphic functional calculus for fractional powers, we may use the following standard Stiletjes formula (see
[
4
, (3.52)]
, for example):

(7.4)

(A+z)^{-\gamma}=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\gamma}(A+z+t)^{-1}\,dt,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Next, let
z=\rho e^{i\varphi}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then, using Cauchy’s theorem,

\displaystyle(A+z)^{-\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{e^{i\varphi\infty}}w^{-\gamma}(A+\rho e^{i\varphi}+w)^{-1}\,dw

\displaystyle=e^{i(1-\gamma)\varphi}\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-\gamma}(A+\rho e^{i\varphi}+se^{i\varphi})^{-1}\,ds.

So,

\|(A+z)^{-\gamma}\|\leq M_{A}\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-\gamma}(\rho+s)^{-1}\,ds=\frac{M_{A}}{\rho^{\gamma}}.

In other cases,
\gamma=(\lceil\gamma\rceil-1)+\delta
where
\delta\in(0,1)
, and the estimate follows from the two previous cases.
∎

Now let
s>-1
be fixed and let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
We define

(7.5)

f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A):=f(\infty)-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)(A+\alpha-i\beta)^{-(s+1)}\,d\beta\,d\alpha.

Note that when
s=1
and
f\in\mathcal{B}
, (
7.5
) coincides with the definition of
f(A)
as given by
the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus in
[
7
]
, cf. (
1.4
).

This definition is valid as the following simple proposition shows.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and
s>-1
.

1.

The map
f\mapsto f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)
is bounded from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to
L(X)
.

2.

For
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,

(7.6)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\,f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A+\epsilon)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A),

in the operator norm topology.

Proof.

Lemma
7.1
and (
7.5
) imply that

(7.7)

\|f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+\frac{2^{s}M_{A}^{\lceil{s+1}\rceil}}{\pi}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

Thus the boundedness of the map
f\mapsto f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)
from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to
L(X)
follows.

By a standard Laplace transform representation for negative fractional powers
[
4
, (3.56)]
,

(7.8)

(A+z)^{-(s+1)}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s}e^{-tz}e^{-tA}\,dt,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

By the dominated convergence theorem,
z\mapsto(A+z)^{-(s+1)}
is continuous (even holomorphic) on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
in the operator norm topology.

The operators
(A+\epsilon)_{\epsilon\geq 0}
are uniformly sectorial of angle
\theta
, more precisely,
M_{A+\epsilon}\leq M_{A}

[
35
, Proposition 2.1.1 f)]
. By Lemma
7.1
, this implies that

(7.9)

\|(A+\epsilon+z)^{-(s+1)}\|\leq M_{A}^{\lceil{s+1}\rceil}|z|^{-(s+1)},\qquad\epsilon\geq 0,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Now (
7.6
) follows from applying (
7.5
) to
A+\epsilon
, letting
\epsilon\to 0+
, and using the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

The property (
7.6
) can be compared with Corollary
11.2
where a direct proof is given that the shifts form a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
. To deduce (
7.6
), one also needs that if
f_{\epsilon}(z)=f(z+\epsilon)
, then
(f_{\epsilon})_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A+\epsilon)
. By Theorem
5.1
, it suffices that this holds for
f=r_{\lambda},\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, i.e., to show that
(r_{\lambda})_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)=(\lambda+A)^{-1}
. We show this in Theorem
7.6
, but the argument uses (
7.6
).

Let
f_{\text{HP}}(A)
stand for a function
f
of
A
defined by the Hille-Phillips functional calculus when
f
is in the Hille-Phillips algebra
\mathcal{LM}
, and let
f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)
denote a function
f
of
A
given by the holomorphic functional calculus when
f
is in the domain of that calculus. The following statement shows that both calculi are compatible with the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus,
and moreover that the definitions of
f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)
agree for the various values of
s
for which
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
.

(i)

If
A
is injective, then

(7.10)

f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)=f_{\mathrm{Hol}}(A).

(ii)

If
\sigma\geq s
then

(7.11)

f_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A).

(iii)

If
f\in\mathcal{LM}\cap\mathcal{D}_{s}
, then

(7.12)

f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A)=f_{{\rm HP}}(A).

In particular, (
7.12
) holds if
f\in\mathcal{LM}
and
s>0
.

Proof.

We start by proving (
7.10
). Assume that
A
is injective, and
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
, where
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
. Let
\psi\in(\theta,\pi/2)
. Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
, and assume (without loss of generality) that
f(\infty)=0
. By the definition of the holomorphic functional calculus,

A(1+A)^{-2}f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\frac{\lambda f(\lambda)}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}(\lambda-A)^{-1}\,d\lambda.

Since

f(\lambda)=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{(\lambda+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+1}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

Fubini’s theorem and Cauchy’s theorem imply that

\displaystyle A(1+A)^{-2}f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)\quad

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\psi}}\,\frac{\lambda(\lambda-A)^{-1}}{(\lambda+1)^{2}(\lambda+\alpha-i\beta)^{s+1}}\,d\lambda\right)d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}A(1+A)^{-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)(A+\alpha-i\beta)^{-(s+1)}d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=A(1+A)^{-2}f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A).

Hence (
7.10
) holds.

Now we no longer assume that
A
is injective. We infer by (
7.10
) that

f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A+\epsilon)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}(A+\epsilon)

for all
\epsilon>0
and
\sigma\geq s
.
Letting
\epsilon\to 0
and using Proposition
7.2
, we obtain the assertion (
7.11
).

Finally, if
f\in\mathcal{LM}\cap\mathcal{D}_{s}
for some
s>-1
, then
f\in\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and using (
7.11
), we have

f_{\text{HP}}(A)=\Phi_{A}(f)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A).\qed

Remark 7.5
.

If
f
has zero polynomial limits at zero and at infinity in the sense of
[
35
, p.27]
, then the proof above does not require the regularisation factor
\lambda(\lambda+1)^{-2}
. Hence
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)=f_{\mathrm{Hol}}(A)
regardless of the injectivity of
A
.
One can show that
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)=f_{\mathrm{Hol}}(A)
even when
f
belongs to the extended Riesz-Dunford function class, (for example,
f(z)=e^{-tz},\,t>0
),
but we omit a discussion of this here (cf. the proof of Lemma
7.11
).

Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{s}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
if
-1<s\leq\sigma
, and the space

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{s>-1}\mathcal{D}_{s}

is an algebra, by Lemma
3.21
. Thus it is a plausible and natural domain for a functional calculus, which we now define.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
. The formula (
7.5
) defines an algebra homomorphism:

\displaystyle\Psi_{A}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\mapsto L(X),\qquad\Psi_{A}(f)=f_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1.

Moreover,

(i)

\Psi_{A}(r_{\lambda})=(\lambda+A)^{-1},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

(ii)

\Psi_{A}
is bounded in the sense that for every
s>-1
there exists
C_{s}(A)
such that

(7.13)

\|\Psi_{A}(f)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+C_{s}(A)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}.

Specifically, (
7.7
) holds.

Moreover,
\Psi_{A}
is the unique algebra homomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
to
L(X)
which satisfies
(i)
and
(ii)
.

The homomorphism
\Psi_{A}
will be called the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus
.

Proof.

It follows from (
7.11
) that
\Psi_{A}
is well-defined by (
7.5
), and from (
7.10
) that
(r_{\lambda})_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}(A+\epsilon)=(\lambda+\epsilon+A)^{-1}
for
\epsilon>0
. Letting
\epsilon\to 0
and using (
7.6
) gives (i). Moreover (
7.13
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
7.2
.

We will now prove that
\Psi_{A}
is a homomorphism. Let
f,g\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{D}_{r}
and
g\in\mathcal{D}_{t}
for some strictly positive
s
and
t
, hence

fg\in\mathcal{D}_{s+t+1}
by Lemma
3.21
.
Since
\mathcal{LM}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for every
s>0
by Corollary
5.2
,
there exist
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(g_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
from
\mathcal{LM}

such that

f_{n}\to f\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}_{s}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{n}\to g\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}_{t},\qquad n\to\infty,

and then, in view of Lemma
3.21
,

f_{n}g_{n}\to fg
in
\mathcal{D}_{s+t+1}
as
n\to\infty
.

By the product rule for the HP-calculus and (
7.12
),

\Psi_{A}(f_{n}g_{n})=(f_{n}g_{n})_{\mathrm{HP}}(A)=\Psi_{A}(f_{n})\Psi_{A}(g_{n}),\qquad n\geq 1.

Passing to the limit when
n\to\infty
and using (
7.13
), it follows that

\Psi_{A}(fg)=\Psi_{A}(f)\Psi_{A}(g).

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\to L(X)
be an algebra homomorphism satisfying (i) and (ii). Then
\Psi
and
\Psi_{A}
coincide on
\{r_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\}
. Since
A
is densely defined,
\Psi(1)=1
(see
[
8
, Section 6, Observation (2)]
, so
\Psi
and
\Psi_{A}
coincide on the span of these functions which is dense in

\mathcal{D}_{s}
(Theorem
5.1
). Since
\Psi
and
\Psi_{A}
are both bounded on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, it follows that they coincide on each
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and hence on
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

If
A
is an operator for which a
\mathcal{D}
-calculus exists with the properties (i) and (ii) given in Theorem
7.6
, then
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
. This follows from (
3.20
) and the properties (i) and (ii). By combining this with Theorem
7.6
, we obtain Theorem
1.1
.
Note also that, if (i) holds for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, then it holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, by the resolvent identity.

The Banach algebras
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty},\,s>-1
, are subalgebras of
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, so we obtain the following corollary by restricting the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus.

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
and
s>-1
. Then there exists a bounded algebra homomorphism
\Psi_{A}^{s}:\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{s}\mapsto L(X)
such that

(i)

\Psi_{A}^{s}(r_{\lambda})=(\lambda+A)^{-1},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

(ii)

\Psi^{s}_{A}
is bounded in the
\mathcal{D}_{s}
-norm, i.e., there exists
C_{s}(A)
such that

\|\Psi_{A}(f)\|\leq C_{s}(A)\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}.

Moreover
\Psi_{A}^{s}
is the unique algebra homomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
to
L(X)
which satisfies
(i)
and
(ii)
.

From now onwards, we will write
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
instead of
\Psi_{A}(f)
, for
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
.
When
f(z)=e^{-tz}
, we will continue to use the notation
e^{-tA}
for
f(A)
, since the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus agrees with the HP-calculus by Theorem
7.4
. So
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
form the
C_{0}
-semigroup generated by
-A
, and it extends to a bounded holomorphic semigroup.

Let
g:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}_{+}
be a holomorphic function and assume that
r_{\lambda}\circ g\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Since the functions
(r_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}
satisfy the resolvent identity, the operators

((r_{\lambda}\circ g)_{\mathcal{D}}(A))_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\subset L(X)

also satisfy the resolvent identity, i.e., they form a pseudo-resolvent. In particular their kernels and their ranges are independent of
\lambda
, and they form the resolvent of an operator
B
if and only if the common kernel is
\{0\}
, and the domain of
B
is the common range of the pseudo-resolvent (see
[
57
, Section VIII.4]
).

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
A,B\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
,
and let
g:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}_{+}
be holomorphic. Assume that, for each
s>-1
, there exists
\sigma>-1
such that

(a)

For all
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
f\circ g\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, and

(b)

For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
(r_{\lambda}\circ g)_{\mathcal{D}}(A)=(\lambda+B)^{-1}
.

Then
(f\circ g)_{\mathcal{D}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}}(B)
for all
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

By assumption (a), Corollary
3.13
and the Closed Graph Theorem,
f\mapsto f\circ g
is a bounded map from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Moreover the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus for
A
is a bounded map from
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
to
L(X)
. Hence the composition is a bounded map from
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to
L(X)
, and by assumption (b) it sends
r_{\lambda}
to
(\lambda+B)^{-1}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Moreover the maps collectively form an algebra homomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
to
L(X)
. By the uniqueness in Theorem
7.6
, this map is the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus for
B
.
∎

In the context of Corollary
7.9
, the operator
B
is sometimes written as
g(A)
, but the precise meaning depends on the specific situation.

There is also a version of Corollary
7.9
for fixed values of
s
and
\sigma
, using the
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
- and
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}^{\infty}
-calculi.

Examples 7.10
.

Examples of functions
g
and operators
B
which satisfy the conditions of Corollary
7.9
include the following.

1.

g(z)=z^{-1}
, if
A
is injective (with dense range);
\sigma=s
,
B=A^{-1}
. Then
f\circ g
is the function
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
as in Lemma
3.17
. Note that
\tilde{f}_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
is defined as a bounded operator on
X
, even if
A
is not injective. If
A_{0}
is the restriction of
A
to
X_{0}
, the closure of the range of
A
, then
\tilde{f}_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
acts as
A_{0}^{-1}
on
X_{0}
and as the sectorial limit
f(\infty)
on the kernel of
A
. If
X
is reflexive this determines
\tilde{f}_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
on
X
.

2.

g(z)=tz
, where
t>0
;
\sigma=s
,
B=tA
. See Lemma
3.17
.

3.

g(z)=z+\eta
, where
\eta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
;
\sigma=s
,
B=A+\eta
. See Remark
7.3
.

4.

g(z)=z^{\gamma}
, where
\gamma\in(0,1)
;
s>\-1,\sigma>-1
;
B=A^{\gamma}
(as defined in the holomorphic functional calculus). See Corollary
4.14
for the assumption (a) in Corollary
7.9
, and Corollary
7.12
below for the assumption (b). The result also holds for
\gamma\in(1,\pi/(2\theta))
.

5.

Examples (2), (3) for
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+},
and (4) above are Bernstein functions. By Lemma
3.20
,
r_{\lambda}\circ g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for all Bernstein functions
g
and
s>2
. We will show in the proof of Theorem
10.5
that
(r_{\lambda}\circ g)_{D}(A)=(\lambda+g(A))^{-1}
, where
g(A)
is a sectorial operator.

In Example
7.10
(4) above, we have introduced a fractional power
A^{\gamma}
, where
\gamma>0
. These operators are defined in various ways, including the extended holomorphic functional calculus.
To justify the example, we need the following lemma about fractional powers, which is probably known at least in simpler form. For
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows easily from a standard result
[
44
, Proposition 5.1.4]
.
We give a proof that uses the holomorphic functional calculus for fractional powers as in
[
35
, Section 3.1]
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
, and
\nu>0
. In the operator norm topology,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}((A+\epsilon)^{\gamma}+z)^{-\nu}=(A^{\gamma}+z)^{-\nu},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Proof.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
be fixed, and let

f(\lambda)=\left(\lambda^{\gamma}+z\right)^{-\nu},\qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Then
f\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and, by considering the derivative of
\mu\mapsto(\mu+z)^{-\nu}
, we see that there exists a constant
C
(depending on
z
) such that

|f(\lambda)-f(0)|\leq C|\lambda|^{\gamma},\quad|f(\lambda)|\leq C|\lambda|^{-\gamma\nu},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Thus
f
has polynomial limits at
0
and
\infty
, and so
f
belongs to the extended Riesz-Dunford class defined in
[
35
, Lemma 2.2.3]
. In other words,

g_{0}(\lambda):=f(\lambda)-z^{-\nu}(1+\lambda)^{-1}

has polynomial decay at
0
and
\infty
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
(independent of
\epsilon
) such that

(7.14)

|f(\lambda+\epsilon)-f(\epsilon)|\leq C^{\prime}|\lambda|^{\gamma},\quad|f(\lambda+\epsilon)|\leq C^{\prime}|\lambda|^{-\gamma\nu},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\,\epsilon\in(0,1).

Let

g_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=f(\lambda+\epsilon)-f(\epsilon)(1+\lambda)^{-1}.

Using the definition of the primary functional calculus
[
35
, Section 2.3.1]
,
we have

\displaystyle f(A+\epsilon)-f(A)

\displaystyle=g_{\epsilon}(A)-g_{0}(A)+(f(\epsilon)-f(0))(I+A)^{-1},

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(A)-g_{0}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Sigma_{\psi}}\left(g_{\epsilon}(\lambda)-g_{0}(\lambda)\right)(\lambda-A)^{-1}\,d\lambda,

where
\psi\in(\theta,\pi/2)
. By the dominated convergence theorem,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\|(g_{\epsilon}(A)-g_{0}(A)\|=0.

The pointwise convergence of
g_{\epsilon}-g_{0}
to zero is clear, and the existence of an integrable majorant follows easily from (
7.14
).
∎

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
f_{\gamma}(z)=f(z^{\gamma})
. Then

1.

In operator norm,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}f_{\mathcal{D}}((A+\epsilon)^{\gamma})=f_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma}).

2.

(f_{\gamma})_{\mathcal{D}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma})
.

Proof.

The proof of (1) follows from Lemma
7.11
in essentially the same way as the last paragraph of the proof of Proposition
7.2
.

By Corollary
4.14
,
h\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. By (
7.10
) and the Composition Theorem for the holomorphic functional calculus
[
35
, Theorem 2.4.2]
, we have
h_{\mathcal{D}}(A+\epsilon)=f_{\mathcal{D}}((A+\epsilon)^{\gamma})
. Letting
\epsilon\to 0+
and using (
7.6
) and (1), we obtain (2).
∎

8.
The calculus on Hardy-Sobolev algebras

Given the negative generator
A
of a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on a Banach space
X
, the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus allows us to extend the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus to a much larger class of functions. A drawback of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus is that it does not respect
the sectoriality angle of
A
, so the results within the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus are independent of the sectoriality angle and confined to holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. To remedy that problem, we introduce in this section a version of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus adjusted to an appropriate Hardy-Sobolev algebra on a sector in the right half-plane. While the Hardy-Sobolev algebra has a “stronger” norm, it appears to be an adequate substitute for
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
in the setting of sectors, and it has significant applications, as we will see in Section
10
.

The basic idea is a very simple change of variable in the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus. If
\Psi^{s}_{A}
is the
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
-calculus for a sectorial operator
A
, then one sets

\Upsilon_{A}(f):=\Psi^{s}_{A^{\gamma}}(f_{1/\gamma})
for appropriate values of
\gamma
, determined by the sectoriality angle
\theta_{A}
of
A
. This definition does not depend on the precise choice of
\gamma
, by Corollary
7.12
. The definitions also agree for different
s>-1
by Theorem
7.4
(ii), and we set
s=0
for convenience. As we show below this eventually leads to a new calculus for Hardy-Sobolev algebras on sectors.

Throughout this section, we assume that

A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
,
0<\theta<\psi<\pi
, and we let

\gamma:=\pi/(2\psi)
, and, as before,

M_{\psi}(A):=\sup_{z\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}}\,\|z(A+z)^{-1}\|.

Then
A^{\gamma}\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and
\theta_{A^{\gamma}}=\gamma\theta_{A}\in(\theta_{A}/2,\pi/2)

[
35
, Proposition 3.1.2]
. We are particularly interested in cases where
\psi
is close to
\theta
, so that
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
is as large as possible.

8.1.
The operator
f(A)
for
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

Recall from Lemmas
4.9
and
4.13
(i) that if
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and
f_{1/\gamma}(z)=f(z^{1/\gamma}),
then

f^{\prime}_{1/\gamma}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+}),\qquad f_{1/\gamma}(\infty)=f(\infty),

and consequently
f_{1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Together with Proposition
4.17
this motivates the following definition of the operator
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
by means of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus applied to
A^{\gamma}
:

(8.1)

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A):=f_{1/\gamma}(\infty)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{1/\gamma}(\alpha+i\beta)(A^{\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta)^{-1}\,d\beta\,d\alpha.

The right-hand side of (
8.1
) converges in the uniform operator topology, and, by (
7.7
) and (
4.27
),

(8.2)

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+\frac{M_{A^{\gamma}}}{\pi}\|f_{1/\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0},0}\leq|f(\infty)|+M_{A^{\gamma}}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\psi)}\leq M_{A^{\gamma}}\|f\|^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

If
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
and
A
is injective, then
f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)
can also be defined using the holomorphic functional calculus and the Composition Rule within it
[
35
, Theorem 2.4.2]
:

f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)=f_{1/\gamma,\operatorname{Hol}}(A^{\gamma}):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}[A^{\gamma}(1+A^{\gamma})^{-2}]^{-1}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\,\frac{\lambda f(\lambda^{1/\gamma})}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}(\lambda-A^{\gamma})^{-1}\,d\lambda,

for
0<\theta<\omega<\psi
. The following proposition shows that our definition (
8.1
) of
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
coincides with
f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)
, when
A
is injective, and various other properties
are easily deduced from the definition above and corresponding properties of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, and
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
, where
0\leq\theta<\psi<\pi
.

(i)

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
does not depend on the choice of
\psi
.

(ii)

If
\nu\in(0,1)
and
f_{\nu}(z)=f(z^{\nu})
, then
(f_{\nu})_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{H}}(A^{\nu})
.

(iii)

If
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
\theta<\pi/2
, then
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
.

(iv)

If
A
is injective, then
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)
.

(v)

In the operator norm topology,
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)=f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
.

Proof.

Statements (i), (ii) and (iii) follow from Corollary
7.12
(ii) and Lemma
4.9
. Statement (iv) follows from (iii) and Theorem
7.4
(i). Statement (v) follows from Proposition
7.2
.

An alternative direct proof of (iv) can be given as follows.
We may assume that
f(\infty)=0
. Since
f_{1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
, Corollary
3.10
gives

f_{1/\gamma}(z)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f_{1/\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)}{z+\alpha-i\beta}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Using Fubini’s theorem and some basic properties of the holomorphic functional calculus,
we obtain

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip-5.0ptA^{\gamma}(1+A^{\gamma})^{-2}f_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{1/\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\,\frac{\lambda(\lambda-A^{\gamma})^{-1}}{(\lambda+1)^{2}(\lambda+\alpha-i\beta)}\,d\lambda\right)d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=-A^{\gamma}(1+A^{\gamma})^{-2}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{1/\gamma}^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)(A^{\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta)^{-1}d\beta\,d\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=A^{\gamma}(1+A^{\gamma})^{-2}f_{\mathcal{H}}(A),

and (iv) follows.
∎

Now we can formally define the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
, where
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
. For any
