\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
the formula (
8.1
)
defines a bounded algebra homomorphism:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{A}:\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}\mapsto L(X),\qquad\Upsilon_{A}(f)=f_{\mathcal{H}}(A).

The homomorphism
\Upsilon_{A}
satisfies

\Upsilon_{A}(r_{\lambda})=(\lambda+A)^{-1}
for all
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
, and it is the unique homomorphism with these properties.

The homomorphism
\Upsilon_{A}
will be called the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus
for
A
.

Proof.

The boundedness of
\Upsilon_{A}
follows from either (
8.13
) or (
8.2
).
The homomorphism property is implied by Theorem
7.8
.
Indeed, employing the functional calculus
\Psi^{0}_{A}
on
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{0}
given by Corollary
7.8
,
one has

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{A}(fg)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{0}_{A}((fg)_{1/\alpha})=\Psi^{0}_{A}(f_{1/\alpha}g_{1/\alpha})

\displaystyle=\Psi^{0}_{A}(f_{1/\alpha})\Psi^{0}_{A}(g_{1/\alpha})=\Upsilon_{A}(f)\Upsilon_{A}(g).

The uniqueness follows from Theorem
5.10
.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

If
A
is any operator for which there is an
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
-calculus as in Theorem
8.2
, then
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
for some
\theta\in(0,\psi)
. This follows from (
4.24
), and in combination with Theorem
8.2
this yields the proof of Theorem
1.2
. If
\Upsilon_{A}(r_{\lambda})=(\lambda+A)^{-1}
for some
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
then this holds for all
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
, by the resolvent identity.

8.2.
The operator
\arccot(A^{\gamma})
and the
\arccot
-formula

In this section, we derive an alternative to the formula (
8.1
) for the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus,
in the form of an operator counterpart of Proposition
4.19
for scalar functions. In addition to its intrinsic interest, it helps us to compare our approach with the approach developed by Boyadzhiev
[
12
]
, as we do at the end of this section.

We introduce as an operator kernel the function

g(z):=\arccot(z)=\frac{1}{2i}\log\left(\frac{z+i}{z-i}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

already considered in Example
3.5
. Note that
g\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
g(\infty)=0
,
g^{\prime}(z)=-(z^{2}+1)^{-1}
,
and (
4.38
) holds:

\arccot(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(z+\alpha-i\beta)^{-1}}{(\alpha+i\beta)^{2}+1}\,d\beta\,d\alpha\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
, where
\theta\in[0,\pi)
. Let
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
and
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
.
By the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus in (
7.5
) and (
7.7
),

(8.3)

\arccot(A^{\gamma}):=\arccot_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(A^{\gamma}+\alpha-i\beta)^{-1}}{(\alpha+i\beta)^{2}+1}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,

where the integral converges in the operator norm, and

(8.4)

\|\arccot(A^{\gamma})\|\leq\frac{M_{A^{\gamma}}}{\pi}\|\arccot z\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}<3M_{A^{\gamma}},\;\;M_{A^{\gamma}}:=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\,\|z(A^{\gamma}+z)^{-1}\|.

We will provide an alternative estimate for the operator
\arccot(A^{\gamma}/s^{\gamma})
.
Following (
4.40
), we may formally write

(8.5)

\displaystyle\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\gamma}}{1-t^{\gamma}}\right|\left((t-e^{i\psi}A)^{-1}+(t-e^{-i\psi}A)^{-1}\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{1}{4i}\int_{|\lambda|=1,\,\arg\lambda\in(\psi,2\pi-\psi)}\,(\lambda-A)^{-1}\,d\lambda.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
and
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
, where
0\leq\theta<\psi<\pi
. The operator
\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})
is well-defined and

(8.6)

\|\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})\|\leq M_{\psi}(A)\frac{\pi}{2}.

Proof.

Using (
4.43
), one notes that

\displaystyle\|\arccot(A^{\gamma})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{\psi}(A)}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\gamma}}{1-t^{\gamma}}\right|\,\frac{dt}{t}+\frac{M_{\psi}(A)}{4}\int_{|\lambda|=1,\;\arg\lambda\in(\psi,2\pi-\psi)}\,\frac{|d\lambda|}{|\lambda|}

\displaystyle=M_{\psi}(A)\left(\frac{\psi}{2}+\frac{(\pi-\psi)}{2}\right)=M_{\psi}(A)\frac{\pi}{2}.\qed

The next lemma shows that the formula
\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})
coincides
with the definition of
\arccot(A^{\gamma})
by the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus. When
A
is injective,
\arccot(A^{\gamma})
is defined in the holomorphic functional calculus by

(8.7)

\arccot_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A^{\gamma}):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}[A(1+A)^{-1}]^{-1}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\,\frac{\lambda\arccot(\lambda^{\gamma})}{\lambda+1}(\lambda-A)^{-1}\,d\lambda,

where
0<\theta<\omega<\psi<\pi
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Under the assumptions above,
\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})=\arccot_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma})
. If
A
is injective, then

\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma})=\arccot_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A^{\gamma})
.

Proof.

Assume first that
A
is injective. Using (
8.7
), (
4.40
) and (
8.3
)
we obtain

\displaystyle A(1+A)^{-1}\arccot_{\rm{Hol}}(A^{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\,\frac{z\arccot(z^{\gamma})}{z+1}(z-A)^{-1}\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\gamma}}{1-t^{\gamma}}\right|\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\,\frac{z}{z+1}(z-A)^{-1}\,V_{\psi}(z,t)\,dz\right)\,\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{1}{4i}\int_{|\lambda|=1,\,\arg\lambda\in(\psi,2\pi-\psi)}\,\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Sigma_{\omega}}\frac{z(z-A)^{-1}}{(z+1)(\lambda-z)}dz\right)d\lambda

\displaystyle=A(A+1)^{-1}\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\log\left|\frac{1+t^{\gamma}}{1-t^{\gamma}}\right|\left((t-e^{i\psi}A)^{-1}+(t-e^{-i\psi}A)^{-1}\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip 30.0pt+A(A+1)^{-1}\frac{1}{4i}\int_{|\lambda|=1,\,\arg\lambda\in(\psi,2\pi-\psi)}\,(\lambda-A)^{-1}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=A(A+1)^{-1}\arccot_{{\rm int}}(A^{\gamma}).

Thus the second statement holds. Moreover,

\arccot_{\operatorname{Hol}}(A^{\gamma})=\arccot_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma})
, by Theorem
7.4
, and the first statement follows.

If
A
is not injective, we have
\arccot_{\rm int}((A+\epsilon)^{\gamma})=\arccot_{\mathcal{D}}((A+\epsilon)^{\gamma})
from the case above. When
\epsilon\to 0+
, the left-hand side converges in operator norm to
\arccot_{\rm int}(A^{\gamma})
by applying the dominated convergence theorem in (
8.5
), and the right-hand side converges to
\arccot_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\gamma})
by Proposition
7.2
.
∎

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
and
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
, where
0\leq\theta<\psi<\pi
. If
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, and
f_{\psi}
is given by (
4.36
), then

(8.8)

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f(\infty)-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\psi}^{\prime}(t)\,\arccot(A^{\gamma}/t^{\gamma})\,dt

where the integral converges in the uniform operator topology, and

(8.9)

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+\frac{M_{\psi}(A)}{2}\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq{M_{\psi}(A)}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

Moreover, if
f(\infty)=0
, then

(8.10)

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq\frac{M_{\psi}(A)}{2}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in(\theta,\psi)
be fixed.
Let
g(z)=\arccot(z)
and
g_{\gamma}(z)=\arccot(z^{\gamma})
. Since
g\in\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\gamma\varphi}
,
g_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
. Now

(8.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|g^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\rho e^{\pm i\varphi})|\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{\gamma-1}|g^{\prime}(\rho^{\gamma}e^{\pm i\gamma\varphi})|\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}|g^{\prime}(te^{\pm i\gamma\varphi})|\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{|1+t^{2}e^{\pm 2i\gamma\varphi}|}

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{|1+\rho^{2}e^{2i\psi}|}

\displaystyle=2\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{((\rho^{2}-1)^{2}+4\rho^{2}\cos^{2}\psi)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{d\rho}{(\rho+\cos\psi)^{2}-(1+\cos^{2}\psi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1+\cos^{2}\psi)^{1/2}}\log\left(\frac{1+\cos\psi+\sqrt{1+\cos^{2}\psi}}{1+\cos\psi-\sqrt{1+\cos^{2}\psi}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\log\left(\frac{6}{\cos\psi}\right).

Let
q_{t}(z)=\arccot(z^{\gamma}/t^{\gamma}),\,t>0
. By scale-invariance (Lemma
4.9
),
\|q_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}}=\|g_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}}
. It follows from Lemma
2.2
that
t\mapsto q_{t}
is continuous from
(0,\infty)
to
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
.
Hence, in view of Proposition
4.19
, we have

(8.12)

f=f(\infty)-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\psi}^{\prime}(t)\,q_{t}\,dt,

where the integral is understood as a Bochner integral in
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
.
By applying the bounded operator
\Upsilon_{A}
to both sides of (
8.12
),
we obtain (
8.8
). The estimate (
8.9
) follows from (
8.8
), (
8.10
), and Lemmas
8.4
and
8.5
.
∎

Remarks 8.7
.

1. If
M_{\psi}(A)=1
in (
8.9
), that is, if
-A
generates a holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup which is contractive on
\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\psi}
, then the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus is contractive. This seems to be a new feature which has not been present in constructions of other calculi in the literature.

2. An alternative to the estimate (
8.9
) is

(8.13)

\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|\leq|f(\infty)|+3M_{A^{\gamma}}\|f_{\psi}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

This is obtained from (
8.8
), using the estimates (
7.7
) (with
s=0
) and (
3.23
) to obtain the estimate
\|\arccot(A^{\gamma})\|\leq 3\pi M_{A^{\gamma}}
. The constant
3
is not optimal.
It is possible to provide explicit bounds for
M_{A^{\gamma}}
in terms of
M_{A}
. However we refrain from doing so in this paper, and we refer the interested reader to
[
6
, Propositions 5.1 and 5.2]
.

Finally in this section, we discuss the relations between
[
12
]
and the present work.
For
\psi\in(0,\pi)
, as in
[
12
]
, let

\displaystyle k_{\psi}(t)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\log\left|\coth\left(\frac{\pi t}{4\psi}\right)\coth\left(\frac{\pi t}{4(\pi-\psi)}\right)\right|.

Note that
k_{\psi}
is an even function on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\|k_{\psi}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1.

For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),
let

(f\circ k_{\psi})(t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(s)k_{\psi}\left(\log(t/s)\right)\,\frac{ds}{s},\qquad t>0,

and for
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
,
\theta\in[0,\psi)
, define

(8.14)

W_{\psi}(A,t)=-\frac{A}{2}\left(e^{-i\psi}(A-e^{-i\psi}t)^{-2}+e^{i\psi}(A-e^{i\psi}t)^{-2}\right).

It was proved in
[
12
, Theorem 3.1]
that if
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
,

A
has dense range, and

(8.15)

\int_{0}^{\infty}|\langle W_{\psi}(A,t)x,x^{*}\rangle|\,dt<\infty,\qquad x\in X,\,x^{*}\in X^{*},

then
A
admits
a bounded
H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})
-calculus given by

(8.16)

\langle f(A)x,x^{*}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle W_{\psi}(A,t)x,x^{*}\rangle(f_{\psi}\circ k_{\psi})(t)\,dt,\quad x\in X,\,x^{*}\in X^{*},

where the integral converges absolutely (in the weak sense).
Conversely, if
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
and
\varphi\in(\theta,\psi)
are such that
A
has a bounded
H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varphi})
-calculus,
then (
8.15
) holds.
(Note that in this situation
A
has a bounded
H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})
-calculus given by (
8.16
), by the uniqueness of the calculus.)
The formula (
8.16
) is obtained in
[
12
]
by rather involved Fourier analysis, and some technical details are omitted in
[
12
]
.

In
[
12
, Proposition 5.1]
it is observed that, if
f\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})
and is holomorphic in a larger sector, and
f^{\prime}_{\psi}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
f^{\prime}_{\psi}(\infty)=0
(this assumption is not relevant), then one can integrate by parts and rewrite (
8.16
) as

(8.17)

f(A)=\int_{0}^{\infty}V_{\psi}(A,t)(f_{\psi}^{\prime}\circ k_{\psi})(t)\,\frac{dt}{t},

where

V_{\psi}(A,t)=-\frac{t}{2}\left(e^{-i\psi}(A-te^{-i\psi})^{-1}+e^{i\psi}(A-te^{i\psi})^{-1}\right),

and the integral converges absolutely. This formally leads to the estimate (
8.9
).
While our reproducing
\arccot
-formula (
8.8
) was inspired by (
8.17
), it is not easy to put formal considerations in
[
12
]
into the theory of functional calculi considered in this paper. One can relate (
8.17
) to (
8.8
) and show that the formulas are essentially equivalent within the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus. This requires a number of technicalities and we intend to communicate them elsewhere. Here we note only that

f^{\prime}_{\psi}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
f\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\psi})
(for
\psi=\pi/2
) do not imply that
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
in general,
as shown by an intricate example kindly communicated to us by A. Borichev.

9.
Convergence Lemmas and Spectral Mapping Theorems

9.1.
Convergence Lemmas

Given a negative semigroup generator
A
, a Convergence Lemma for the holomorphic functional calculus is a useful result allowing one to deduce the convergence of
(f_{k}(A))_{k=1}^{\infty}
to
f(A)
from rather weak assumptions on convergence of
(f_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
to
f
; see
[
14
, Lemma 2.1]
,
[
35
, Proposition 5.1.4]
and
[
9
, Theorem 3.1]
, for example.

The following result is similar to a Convergence Lemma for the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus in
[
7
, Theorem 4.13 and Corollary 4.14]
(see also
[
8
, Section 8.1]
). However, the different Convergence Lemmas deal with different classes of functions. To adjust the Convergence Lemma from
[
7
]
to the current setting, we apply the change of variables method used in previous sections, and derive a variant of the Convergence Lemma for the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus.

In the following result,
f(A)
refers to the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
.
Let
s>-1
and let
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{D}_{s}
be such that

(9.1)

\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}<\infty,

and there exists

f(z):=\lim_{k\to\infty}\,f_{k}(z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Let
g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
satisfy

g(0)=g(\infty)=0.

Then

(9.2)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|(f(A)-f_{k}(A))g(A)\|=0.

In particular, if
A
has dense range, then

(9.3)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|f(A)x-f_{k}(A)x\|=0,

for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

By assumption there exists
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
such that
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
.
By Corollary
3.12
we have
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
.
Thus, without loss of generality, we can assume that
f\equiv 0
.

Let
\gamma\in(1,\pi/(2\theta))
.
Then by Corollary
7.12
, using the notation of (
6.1
),

f_{k}(A)=f_{k,1/\gamma}(A^{\gamma})\qquad\text{and}\qquad g(A)=g_{1/\gamma}(A^{\gamma}).

Since
g_{1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\infty}
,
f_{k,1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\infty}
and
f_{k,1/\gamma}g_{1/\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\infty}
(see Corollary
4.14
), and the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus is an algebra homomorphism, we have

f_{k}(A)g(A)=f_{k,1/\gamma}(A^{\gamma})g_{\gamma}(A^{\gamma})=(f_{k,1/\gamma}g_{1/\gamma})(A^{\gamma}).

Now (
9.2
) follows from Lemma
6.1
and the continuity of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus given by Proposition
7.2
(1).

Let
g(z)=z(1+z)^{-2}
and note that
g\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and
g
vanishes at zero and at infinity. If
A
has dense range,
then the range of

g(A)=A(1+A)^{-2}
is dense as well (see
[
41
, Proposition 9.4]
, for example). Since
\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}(A)\|<\infty
, (
9.2
) implies (
9.3
).
∎

In the following result,
f(A)
refers to the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta),
and let
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
.
Let
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
be such that

\sup_{k\geq 1}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}<\infty,

and there exists

f(z):=\lim_{k\to\infty}\,f_{k}(z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}.

Let
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
satisfy

g(0)=g(\infty)=0.

Then

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|(f(A)-f_{k}(A))g(A)\|=0.

In particular, if
A
has dense range, then

\lim_{k\to\infty}\,\|f(A)x-f_{k}(A)x\|=0,

for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to Theorem
9.1
. Corollary
3.12
is replaced by Lemma
4.11
, the compatibility with fractional powers follows from the definitions and Proposition
8.1
(i), Corollary
4.14
is replaced by Lemma
4.9
, Lemma
6.1
is replaced by Lemma
6.2
, and (
7.6
) is replaced by Corollary
7.12
.
∎

9.2.
Spectral mapping theorems

Given a semigroup generator
-A
, a spectral mapping theorem for a functional calculus
\Xi_{A}
signifies informally
that
\Xi_{A}
is associated to
A
in a “natural” way.
However, in general the spectral “mapping” theorem
states only the inclusion
f(\sigma(A))\subset\sigma(\Xi_{A}(f))
.
Equality may fail here even for functions such as
e^{-tz}

and for rather simple operators
A
; see
[
23
, Section IV.3]
, for example.
While one may expect only the spectral inclusion as above,
the equality
f(\sigma(A))\cup\{f(\infty)\}=\sigma(\Xi_{A}(f))\cup\{f(\infty)\}
sometimes holds if
A
inherits some properties of bounded operators such as strong resolvent estimates.
Note that the spectral mapping theorem may not hold
even for bounded operators if the functional calculus possesses only weak
continuity properties, as discussed in
[
10
]
.

The statement below shows that the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus
possesses the standard spectral mapping properties.
It is similar to
[
7
, Theorem 4.17]
, with the addition of a statement about approximate eigenvalues. Recall that for
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, its values
f(\infty)
at infnity and
f(0)
at
0
are defined by (
3.27
) and (
3.28
).
This convention is used below.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
,
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

1.

If
x\in D(A)
and
Ax=\lambda x
, then
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)x=f(\lambda)x
.

2.

If
x^{*}\in D(A^{*})
and
A^{*}x^{*}=\lambda x^{*}
, then
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)^{*}x^{*}=f(\lambda)x^{*}
.

3.

If
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are unit vectors in
D(A)
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|Ax_{n}-\lambda x_{n}\|=0
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)x_{n}-f(\lambda)x_{n}\|=0
.

4.

One has
\sigma(f_{\mathcal{D}}(A))\cup\{f(\infty)\}=f(\sigma(A))\cup\{f(\infty)\}
.

Proof.

The statements (1) and (2) are direct corollaries of (
7.5
) and the reproducing formula for the
\mathcal{D}_{s}
-spaces given in Corollary
3.10
.

For (3), we use the
F
-product of the semigroup
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
, as introduced in
[
18
]
. Let
Y
be the Banach space of all bounded sequences
\mathbf{y}:=(y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
X
such that
\lim_{t\to 0+}\|e^{-tA}y_{n}-y_{n}\|=0
uniformly in
n
, where
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
is the bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup generated by
-A
. Let
Z
be the closed subspace of
Y
consisting of the sequences
\mathbf{y}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|y_{n}\|=0
, and let
\widetilde{Y}=Y/Z
and
Q:Y\to\widetilde{Y}
be the quotient map. Then
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
induces a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup
(e^{-t\tilde{A}})_{t\geq 0}
on
\widetilde{Y}
, whose negative generator
\widetilde{A}
is given by

D(\widetilde{A})=\{Q(\mathbf{y}):y_{n}\in D(A),(Ay_{n})\in Y\},\qquad\widetilde{A}(Q\mathbf{y})=Q((Ay_{n})).

Then
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{n})\in Y
,
Q\mathbf{x}\in D(\widetilde{A})
and
\widetilde{A}Q(\mathbf{x})=\lambda Q(\mathbf{x})
. It follows from (1) that
f_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{A})Q\mathbf{x}=\lambda Q\mathbf{x}
. However it is very easy to see that
f_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{A})Q\mathbf{x}=Q((f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)x_{n}))
(see
[
18
, Theorem 1.7(i)]
), and this establishes (3).

To prove the spectral mapping theorem in (4),
we follow the Banach algebra method used in
[
7
]
for similar purposes and inspired by
[
37
, Section 16.5]
and
[
16
, Section 2.2]
. We may assume without loss of generality that
f(\infty)=0
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the bicommutant of
\{(z+A)^{-1}:-z\in\rho(A)\}
in
L(X)
, so
\mathcal{A}
is a commutative Banach algebra and the spectrum of
f_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
in
\mathcal{A}
coincides with the spectrum in
L(X)
.
Observe that
\sigma(A)\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}\cup\{0\}
.

Let
\chi
be any character of
\mathcal{A}
.
If
\chi((1+A)^{-1})=0
, then
\chi((z+A)^{-1})=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and hence
\chi(f(A))=0=f(\infty)
. Otherwise,
by the resolvent identity,
\chi((z+A)^{-1})=(z+\lambda)^{-1}
for some
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Let
s>-1
be such that
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. Noting that the Stieltjes representation (
7.4
) converges in the uniform operator topology, we infer that
\chi((z+A)^{-(s+1)})=(z+\lambda)^{-(s+1)},z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
.
Applying
\chi
to (
7.5
) gives

\chi(f_{\mathcal{D}}(A))=-\frac{2^{s}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)(\lambda+\alpha-i\beta)^{-(s+1)}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,

and then, by the reproducing formula (
3.29
) for
\mathcal{D}_{s}
-functions (valid on
\mathbb{C}_{+}\cup\{0\}
), we obtain

\chi(f_{\mathcal{D}}(A))=f(\lambda)\in f(\sigma(A)).

Hence
\sigma(f_{\mathcal{D}}(A))\cup\{0\}\subset f(\sigma(A))\cup\{0\}
.
To prove the opposite inclusion, note that if
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
is fixed, then there is a character
\chi
such that
\chi((z+A)^{-1})=(z+\lambda)^{-1}
, so the above argument can be reversed yielding

\chi(f_{\mathcal{D}}(A))=f(\lambda)
, and thus finishing the proof.
∎

Next, using the same approach via Banach algebras, we prove the analogous spectral result for the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus.

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
and
f\in\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}
for some
\theta<\psi<\pi
, and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

1.

If
x\in D(A)
and
Ax=\lambda x
, then
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)x=f(\lambda)x
.

2.

If
x^{*}\in D(A^{*})
and
A^{*}x^{*}=\lambda x^{*}
, then
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)^{*}x^{*}=f(\lambda)x^{*}
.

3.

If
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are unit vectors in
D(A)
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|Ax_{n}-\lambda x_{n}\|=0
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)x_{n}-f(\lambda)x_{n}\|=0
.

4.

One has
\sigma(f_{\mathcal{H}}(A))\cup\{f(\infty)\}=f(\sigma(A))\cup\{f(\infty)\}
.

Proof.

The proofs of (1) and (2) are straightforward consequences of the reproducing formula (
4.34
). Moreover, (3) is deduced from (1) in the same way as in Theorem
9.3
.

The proof of (4) is similar to the corresponding proof in Theorem
9.3
, based on the formula (
8.1
) which converges in the uniform operator topology.
Let
\gamma=\pi/(2\psi)
. By the spectral mapping theorem for the holomorphic functional calculus
[
35
, Theorem 2.7.8]
, or
[
44
, Theorem 5.3.1]
, one has

(9.4)

\sigma((A^{\gamma}+t-i\beta)^{-1})=\{(\lambda^{\gamma}+t-i\beta)^{-1}:\lambda\in\sigma(A)\}\cup\{0\},\quad t>0,\,\beta\in\mathbb{R}.

As in the proof of Theorem
9.3
, let
\mathcal{A}
be the bicommutant of
\{(z+A)^{-1}:-z\in\rho(A)\}
in
L(X)
. Then
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\in\mathcal{A}
and the spectrum of
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
in
\mathcal{A}
coincides with the spectrum in
L(X)
.
Let
\chi
be any character of
\mathcal{A}
, and let
f\in\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}
be such that
f(\infty)=0
. If
\chi((1+A^{\gamma})^{-1})=0
, then, as above,
\chi((z+A^{\gamma})^{-1})=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, hence

\chi(f_{\mathcal{H}}(A))=0=f(\infty)
. Otherwise
\chi((z+A^{\gamma})^{-1})=(\lambda^{\gamma}+z)^{-1}
for some
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Applying
\chi
to (
8.1
)
and using the representation (
4.34
) for
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
-functions, one gets

\chi(f_{\mathcal{H}}(A))=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}_{1/\gamma}(t+i\beta)(\lambda^{\gamma}+t-i\beta)^{-1}\,d\beta\,dt=f(\lambda).

Hence
\sigma(f_{\mathcal{H}}(A))\cup\{0\}\subset f(\sigma(A))\cup\{0\}
.

On the other hand, if
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
, then by (
9.4
) there is a character
\chi
such that
\chi((z+A^{\gamma})^{-1})=(z+\lambda^{\gamma})^{-1}
. So using (
4.34
) again, we infer that
f(\lambda)\in\sigma(f_{\mathcal{H}}(A))
.

Combining the two paragraphs above yields (4).
∎

Our spectral mapping theorems differ from known spectral mapping theorems for the holomorphic functional calculus (see
[
34
]
or
[
35
, Section 2.7]
) in at least three respects. We do not assume that
A
is injective, we cover a wider class of functions including some with a mild singularity at zero (for example,
e^{-1/z}
), and our proofs are completely different.

10.
Some applications to norm-estimates

In this section we apply directly the
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{H}
-calculi that we have constructed
to obtain some operator norm-estimates. In particular,
we obtain uniform bounds on the powers of Cayley transforms
and on the semigroup generated by the inverse of a semigroup generator.
We then compare the results to known estimates in the literature.
We also revisit the theory of holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroups and obtain several basic estimates
along with some slight generalizations.

10.1.
Norm-estimates via the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, and
V(A)
be the Cayley transform
(A-I)(A+I)^{-1}
of
A
.
We now review several important estimates from the literature in the framework of the constructed
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{H}
-calculi.

Recall that
-A
is the generator of a bounded holomorphic semigroup
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
. Let
e_{t}(z)=e^{-tz},\,t\geq 0,\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. Then
e_{t}\in\mathcal{LM}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and

e^{-tA}=(e_{t})_{\mathrm{HP}}(A)=(e_{t})_{\mathcal{D}}(A).

Corollary 10.1
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
, so that (
7.3
) holds.

(i)

One has

\|V(A)^{n}\|\leq 1+32(1+(\sqrt{2}\pi)^{-1})M_{A}^{2},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

(ii)

One has

\|e^{-tA}\|\leq 2M_{A}^{2},\qquad t\geq 0.

(iii)

For every
\nu>0
one has

\|A^{\nu}e^{-tA}\|\leq 2^{\nu+2}t^{-\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)M_{A}^{\lceil\nu\rceil+2},\qquad t\geq 0.

If, in addition, the inverse
A^{-1}
exists and is densely defined, then
A^{-1}
generates
a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup
(e^{-tA^{-1}})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying

\|e^{-tA^{-1}}\|\leq 1+2M_{A}^{2},\qquad t\geq 0,

and, for every
\nu>0
,

\|A^{-\nu}e^{-tA^{-1}}\|\leq 2^{\nu+2}t^{-\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)M_{A}^{\lceil\nu\rceil+2},\qquad t\geq 0.

Proof.

By Lemma
12.1
and Theorem
7.6
, for every
s>0
,

\|V(A)^{n}\|\leq 1+2^{s+4}\pi^{-1}(B(s/2,1/2)+2^{-s/2})M_{A}^{\lceil{s}\rceil+1},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Setting
s=1
we get the assertion (i).

By Proposition
3.15
or by Example
3.4
and Lemma
3.17
(iii), the function
e^{-tz}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for
s>0
and
t>0
, and by (
3.21
) and (
7.7
),

\|e^{-tA}\|\leq 2^{s}\pi^{-1}B(s/2,1/2)M_{A}^{\lceil{s}\rceil+1},\qquad t\geq 0.

So the estimate (ii) follows by setting
s=1
above.

If
f_{\nu}(z):=z^{\nu}e^{-tz}
,
\nu>0
, then
f_{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
if and only if
s>\nu
, and in that case
\|f_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=2t^{-\nu}B((s-\nu)/2,1/2)\Gamma(\nu+1)
(see Example
3.4
).
Since
f_{\nu}
has zero polynomial limits at zero and at infinity,
(f_{\nu})_{\mathcal{D}}(A)
coincides with
A^{\nu}e^{-tA}

as defined by the holomorphic functional calculus (see Remark
7.5
).
Using (
3.21
), it follows that, for every
s>\nu
,

\|A^{\nu}e^{-tA}\|\leq\frac{2^{s+1}t^{-\nu}}{\pi}B\left(\frac{s-\nu}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\nu+1)M_{A}^{\lceil s\rceil+1}.

Setting
s=\nu+1
, the first assertion in (iii) follows. The other two estimates are consequences of Lemma
3.17
(i) and the estimates for
e^{-tA}
and
A^{\nu}e^{-tA}
obtained above.
∎

The results in Corollary
10.1
are not new, and it serves as an ilustration of the utility of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus. We have not aimed at finding the best possible estimates, but it seems that the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus provides bounds that are fairly precise whenever it is applicable.
The power-boundedness of
V(A)
was shown in
[
15
]
and
[
46
]
, using different methods. In
[
7
, Corollary 5.9]
, a weaker result was shown using the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus (so all operators satisfying (
1.3
)). Corollary
10.1
shows how the
\mathcal{D}
- and
\mathcal{H}
-calculi can give a sharper estimate than the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus in the case of sectorial operators.
Part (ii) above is one of many estimates for the bound on a bounded holomorphic semigroup in terms of its sectorial bound, and it is clearly not sharp.
A careful estimation in
[
7
, Lemma 4.7]
of the bound obtained via the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus gave a bound of order
M_{A}\log M_{A}
when
M_{A}
is large. See also
[
52
, Theorem 5.2]
where the result was established for the first time. Estimates of the form given in part (iii) have been known for a long time, but usually without showing the dependence on
M_{A}
.

Next we consider estimates similar to Corollary
10.1
(iii). In Lemma
10.2
and Theorem
10.3

f(A)
refers to the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-).
If
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1,
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

(10.1)

(z^{n}f^{(n)})(A)=C_{s,n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)A^{n}(A+\alpha-i\beta)^{-(s+n+1)}\,d\beta\,d\alpha,

where

C_{s,n}={(-1)}^{n+1}2^{s}\frac{\Gamma(s+n+1)}{\Gamma(s+1)}.

Proof.

By Corollary
5.5
and the boundedness of the
\mathcal{D}_{s+n}
-calculus,
(z^{n}f^{(n)})(A)
coincides with the derivative of order
n
of the function
t\mapsto f(tA)
evaluated at
t=1
. The formula (
7.5
) for
f(tA)\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
can be differentiated repeatedly with respect to
t
by a standard method, and putting
t=1
then gives the formula (
10.1
).
∎

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\pi/2-)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, and assume that
f^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
for
k=1,\dots,n
. Then

(10.2)

(z^{n}f^{(n)})(A)=A^{n}f^{(n)}(A).

Moreover, if
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},\,s>-1
, then

(10.3)

\|t^{n}A^{n}f^{(n)}(tA)\|\leq\frac{2^{s}\Gamma(s+n+1)}{\pi\Gamma(s+1)}(M_{A}+1)^{n}M_{A}^{\lceil{s}\rceil+1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\quad t>0.

In particular, for
f(z)=e^{-z}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
,

(10.4)

\|t^{n}A^{n}e^{-tA}\|\leq 2(n+1)!(M_{A}+1)^{n}M_{A}^{2},\qquad t>0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

We will prove, by induction on
n
, that (
10.2
) holds for all functions
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
such that
f^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
for
k=1,\dots,n
. . First, assume that
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. Then

(1+A)^{-1}(zf^{\prime})(A)+(1+A)^{-1}f^{\prime}(A)=f^{\prime}(A),

This implies that
(zf^{\prime})(A)=Af^{\prime}(A)
.

Now assume that, for some
n\geq 1
,
(z^{k}g^{(k)})(A)=A^{k}g^{(k)}(A)
for
k=1,\dots,n
, for all functions
g
such that
g^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
for
k=0,\dots,n
. Let
f^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
for
k=0,1,\dots,n+1
. Applying the base case (
k=1
) to the function
z^{n}f^{(n)}
(noting that this function and its first derivative are in
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, by Corollary
3.23
), we obtain

(z(z^{n}f^{(n)})^{\prime})(A)=A(z^{n}f^{(n)})^{\prime}(A).

Then applying the inductive hypothesis with
k=n
to the function
f
, and with
k=n-1
and
k=n
to the function
f^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(z^{n+1}f^{(n+1)})(A)

\displaystyle=A\left(n(z^{n-1}f^{(n)})+(z^{n}f^{(n+1)})\right)(A)-n(z^{n}f^{(n)})(A)

\displaystyle=A^{n+1}f^{(n+1)}(A).

This completes the proof of the inductive hypothesis for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and hence proves (
10.2
)

Since
M_{A}=M_{tA}
for all
t>0
, it suffices to prove (
10.3
) for
t=1
. From Lemmas
7.1
and
10.2
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip-15.0pt\|A^{n}\,f^{(n)}(A)\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{s}\Gamma(s+n+1)}{\pi\Gamma(s+1)}(M_{A}+1)^{n}M_{A}^{\lceil{s}\rceil+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f^{\prime}(\alpha+i\beta)|}{|\alpha-i\beta|^{s+1}}\,d\beta\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{s}\Gamma(s+n+1)}{\pi\Gamma(s+1)}(M_{A}+1)^{n}M_{A}^{\lceil{s}\rceil+1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}.\qed

Remark 10.4
.

In Theorem
10.3
, the assumption that
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
f^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty},k=1,2,\dots,n
, can be replaced by the assumption that
f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
and
f^{(n)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
, by using a result of Lyubich
[
43
]
. See Corollary
11.3
.

10.2.
Norm-estimates via the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus

Now we use the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus to provide a new proof that holomorphy of operator semigroups generated by
-A
is preserved for subordinate semigroups generated by
-g(A)
where
g
is a Bernstein function. This was proved for the first time in
[
31
]
.

If
-A
is the generator of a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup
(e^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}

on a Banach space
X
, and
g
is a Bernstein function given by
(
3.30
),
then the operator

(10.5)

g_{0}(A)x:=ax+bAx+\int_{(0,\infty)}\left(x-e^{-tA}x\right)\,d\mu(t),\quad x\in D(A),

is closable, and
g(A)
can be defined as the closure of
g_{0}(A)
. Thus
D(A)
is a core for
g(A)
, and one can prove that
-g(A)
generates a contraction
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
.
Several equivalent definitions of
g(A)
are possible, and we refer the reader to
[
51
]
,
[
30
]
and
[
31
]
.
If
A
is injective, then
g(A)
is well-defined within the (extended) holomorphic functional calculus
and is given by (
10.5
) as above; see Proposition
8.1
and
[
31
, Propositions 3.3 and 3.6]
.

The next statement shows that the so-called semigroup subordination preserves the holomorphy of
C_{0}
-semigroups along with the holomorphy angles. It was one of the main results of
[
31
]
, settling a question raised by Kishimoto and Robinson
[
39
]
.
See also
[
6
]
and
[
7
]
for generalizations and other proofs.

Theorem 10.5
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
, where
\theta\in[0,\pi/2)
,
and let
g
be a Bernstein function as in
(
3.30
).
Then
g(A)\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
. More precisely, for all
\psi\in(\theta,\pi/2)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
, and

\lambda\in{\Sigma}_{\pi-\varphi}
,

(10.6)

\|\lambda(\lambda+g(A))^{-1}\|\leq 2M_{\psi}(A)\left(\frac{1}{\sin(\min(\varphi,\pi/2))}+\frac{2}{\cos\psi\sin^{2}((\varphi-\psi)/2)}\right).

Proof.

Let
\psi\in(\theta,\pi/2)
,
\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
, and

\lambda\in{\Sigma}_{\pi-\varphi}
. If

f(z)=f(z;\lambda):=(\lambda+g(z))^{-1},\qquad z\in\Sigma_{\psi},

then by Corollary
4.16
we have
f\in\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{\psi}
.
We will show that
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=(\lambda+g(A))^{-1}
. It then follows from (
8.9
) and (
4.31
) that (
10.6
) holds. Since the choice of
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
and

\varphi\in(\psi,\pi)
is arbitrary, this shows that the operator
g(A)
is sectorial
of angle
\theta
.

If
A
is injective, then
f(A)
and
\lambda+g(A)
are consistently defined in the holomorphic functional calculus,
and therefore
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=f(A)=(\lambda+g(A))^{-1}
(see
[
35
, Theorem 1.3.2f)]
).

When
A
is not injective, we follow the approach proposed in the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 4.8]
.

Since
A+\epsilon
is invertible, we have

(\lambda+g(A+\epsilon))^{-1}=f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon).

By Proposition
8.1
(v),

(10.7)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)-f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\|=0.

Let
x\in D(A)
. Since
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)
commutes with
(1+A)^{-1}
,
we have
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x\in D(A)
, and
by (
10.5
),

(10.8)

\displaystyle x-(\lambda+g(A))f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x=[g(A+\epsilon)-g(A)]f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x

\displaystyle\hbox{}{\hskip 30.0pt}=\epsilon bf_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x-\int_{(0,\infty)}(1-e^{-\epsilon t})e^{-tA}f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x\,d\mu(t).

It follows from (
10.7
) that

C_{\lambda}:=\sup_{\epsilon\in(0,1]}\|f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)\|<\infty,

and hence

(10.9)

\displaystyle\|x-(\lambda+g(A))f_{\mathcal{H}}(A+\epsilon)x\|

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon bC_{\lambda}\|x\|+C_{\lambda}K_{A}\int_{(0,\infty)}(1-e^{-\epsilon t})\,d\mu(t)\,\|x\|

\displaystyle\to 0,\qquad\epsilon\to 0,

where
K_{A}:=\sup_{t>0}\|e^{-tA}\|
. Since
\lambda+g(A)
is closed, it follows firstly that

(\lambda+g(A))f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)x=x,\quad x\in D(A).

Since
D(A)
is dense in
X
and
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
is bounded, it follows secondly that

(\lambda+g(A))f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)x=x,\qquad x\in X.

Since
\lambda+g(A)
and
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)
commute on
D(A)
,

f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)(\lambda+g(A))x=x,\qquad x\in D(A).

Since
D(A)
is a core for
g(A)
, it follows that this holds for all
x\in D(g(A))
. Thus,
f_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=(\lambda+g(A))^{-1}
, as required.
∎

Remarks 10.6
.

1. A new feature of Theorem
10.5
is an explicit sectoriality constant for
g(A)
, given by the right hand-side of (
10.6
).
This could be valuable when applying the result to families of sectorial operators.
Thus (
10.6
) offers an improvement over similar estimates in
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
and
[
31
]
, where the sectoriality constants for
g(A)
are rather implicit.

2.
We take this opportunity to correct a parsing misprint in the proof
[
6
, Theorem 4.9]
.
One should replace
f(A)
with
f(A)+z
in the third and fourth displays on
[
6
, p.932]

(see (
10.8
) and (
10.9
) above for similar formulas).

Finally, as an illustration, we show how the holomorphy of
C_{0}
-semigroups generated by operators
-A^{\gamma}
fits within the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus, and how estimates of similar type to Corollary
10.1
(ii) can be obtained from the representation of the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus and the function
\arccot
, as in Theorem
8.6
.
The following result is similar to
[
12
, Corollary 5.2]
,
and a generalization of the main result in
[
17
]
to non-integer
\gamma
. See also
[
15
, Remark 2, p.83]
.

Corollary 10.7
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Sect}(\theta)
,
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, and
\gamma\in(0,\pi/(2\theta))
. Then
(e^{-tA^{\gamma}})_{t\geq 0}
is a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup of angle
(\pi/2)-\gamma\theta
. More precisely, if
\psi\in(\theta,\pi/(2\gamma))
and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\varphi}\in\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\gamma\psi}
, then

(10.10)

\|e^{-\lambda A^{\gamma}}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{\cos(\gamma\psi+\varphi)}+\frac{1}{\cos(\gamma\psi-\varphi)}\right)M_{\gamma\psi}(A).

Proof.

Let
\psi\in(\theta,\pi)
. Since the
\mathcal{H}
-calculus
\Upsilon_{A}
is a homomorphism and
\Upsilon_{A}(e^{-\lambda z^{\gamma}})=e^{-\lambda A^{\gamma}}
for every
\lambda\in\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\gamma\psi}
, the family
(e^{-\lambda A^{\gamma}})_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\gamma\psi}}
is an operator semigroup.
By Theorem
4.8
and the argument in Section
2
, the map
\lambda\mapsto e^{-\lambda z^{\gamma}}
is holomorphic from
\Sigma_{(\pi/2)-\gamma\psi}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
, so
\lambda\mapsto e^{-\lambda A^{\gamma}}
is also holomorphic.
The estimate (
10.10
) follows from Example
4.10
(2) and (
8.10
), and it shows boundedness of the semigroup on each relevant sector.
∎

Note that, for
\gamma=1
, Corollary
10.7
provides a sharper bound than Corollary
10.1
(ii).

11.
Appendix: Shifts on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

The shift semigroups on the space
\mathcal{B}
had an important role in the study of the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus in
[
7
]
and
[
8
]
. While the semigroups are not essential in this paper, we think they will be important for further research, and so we describe their properties on the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. In this appendix we prove that the shifts
(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}
given by

(T(\tau)f)(z):=f(z+\tau),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\quad\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

form a holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
for each
s>-1
. We also show that a similar statement holds for shifts on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for each
\psi\in(0,\pi)
.

We consider first the space
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and we begin by proving that the semigroup
(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi}}
of operators is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, for each
s>-1
and
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
s>-1
,
\psi\in(0,\pi/2),
and
a=\tan\psi
. For all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi}
, we have

(11.1)

\|T(\tau)f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\leq C_{a,s}\|f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}},\quad f\in\mathcal{D}_{s},

where

\displaystyle C_{a,s}

\displaystyle:=\frac{(s+1)2^{s}B((s+1)/2,1/2)}{\pi\cos\psi\cos^{s+2}\psi_{a}}+2^{s+1},

\displaystyle\psi_{a}

\displaystyle:=\arctan\big(a+\sqrt{1+a^{2}}\big).

Proof.

Let
\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
. We have

\displaystyle\|T(\tau)f\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq|f(\infty)|+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{+}}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}(z+\tau)|\,dS(z)=|f(\infty)|+J(\tau),

where
dS
denotes area measure on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and

J(\tau):=\int_{\operatorname{Re}z\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z-\operatorname{Re}\tau)^{s}}{|z-\tau|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}(z)|\,dS(z).

Let

\displaystyle W(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}z\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau,\;|z-\tau|\leq|\tau|\right\},

\displaystyle W_{0}(\tau):

\displaystyle=W(\tau)-\tau=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:\;|z|\leq|\tau|\right\}.

If
\operatorname{Re}z\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau
and
z\notin W(\tau)
, then
|z|\leq|z-\tau|+|\tau|\leq 2|z-\tau|
. Hence

(11.2)

\displaystyle J(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{W(\tau)}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z-\operatorname{Re}\tau)^{s}}{|z-\tau|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}(z)|\,dS(z)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\hskip 30.0pt+2^{s+1}\int_{\operatorname{Re}z\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}|f^{\prime}(z)|\,dS(z)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{z\in W(\tau)}\,|f^{\prime}(z)|\int_{W_{0}(\tau)}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}\,dS(z)+2^{s+1}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

Moreover,

(11.3)

\int_{W_{0}(\tau)}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{s}}{|z|^{s+1}}\,dS(z)=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\int_{0}^{|\tau|}\cos^{s}\varphi\,d\rho\,d\varphi=|\tau|B((s+1)/2,1/2).

For
z\in W(\tau)
, we also have
\operatorname{Re}z\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau
and

|\operatorname{Im}z|\leq|\operatorname{Im}\tau|+|\tau|\leq\left(a+\sqrt{1+a^{2}}\right)\operatorname{Re}\tau.

Hence
z\in\Sigma_{\psi_{a}}
and
|z|\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau
, so by Corollary
3.13
,

(11.4)

\max_{z\in W(\tau)}\,|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\max_{z\in\Sigma_{\psi_{a}},\,|z|\geq\operatorname{Re}\tau}\,|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{(s+1)2^{s}}{\pi\operatorname{Re}\tau\cos^{s+2}\psi_{a}}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}}.

Inserting the estimates (
11.3
) and (
11.4
) into (
11.2
) and using

|\tau|\leq\operatorname{Re}\tau/\cos\psi
, we obtain

J(\tau)\leq\left(\frac{(s+1)2^{s}B((s+1)/2,2)}{\pi\cos\psi\cos^{s+2}\psi_{a}}+2^{s+1}\right)\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{V}_{s}},

and (
11.1
) follows.
∎

Corollary 11.2
.

For any
s>-1
, the family
T:=(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}
is
a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
of angle
\pi/2
. The generator of the semigroup is
-A_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}
, where

D(A_{\mathcal{D}_{s}})=\{f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}:f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}\},\qquad A_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}f=-f^{\prime}.

Proof.

By Theorem
11.1
,
T
is bounded on
\Sigma_{\psi}
for each
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)
, and as noted in Remark
3.14
the function
\lambda\mapsto r_{\lambda}
is a holomorphic function from
\mathbb{C}_{+}
to
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, so
\tau\mapsto T(\tau)r_{\lambda}
is holomorphic. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
(Theorem
5.1
), it follows that
T
is strongly continuous on
\Sigma_{\psi}\cup\{0\}
, and moreover, for
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
, the map
\tau\mapsto T(\tau)f
is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

The proof of the statement about the generator is almost identical to the proof for the space
\mathcal{B}
in
[
7
, Lemma 2.6]
.
∎

The following corollary justifies Remark
10.4
about the assumptions in Theorem
10.3
.

Corollary 11.3
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
,
s>-1,
and assume that
f^{(n)}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Then
f^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}
for
k=1,2,\dots,n-1
.

Proof.

Consider the operator
Ag=-g^{\prime}
on
\operatorname{Hol}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and its part
A_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}
in the subspace
\mathcal{D}_{s}
. The operators
A_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}+m,\,m=1,2,\dots,n
, are surjective on
\mathcal{D}_{s}
, and
g=0
is the only solution in
\mathcal{D}_{s}
to
\prod_{m=1}^{n}(A+m)g=0
. The statement follows
from
[
43
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

The space
\mathcal{B}
is invariant under vertical shifts:
f(z)\mapsto f(z+i\sigma)
for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. However the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{s}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\infty}
are not invariant under vertical shifts. See Example
3.19
.

Now we will show that the family of shifts
T
also forms a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
for every
\psi\in(0,\pi)
. If
\psi>\pi/2
, then
T(\tau)
are defined for
\tau\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}
.
For this aim, we will recall the Gabriel inequality for holomorphic functions.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded convex domain in
\mathbb{C}
and let
\Gamma\subset\overline{\Omega}
be a convex curve.
Then there exists a universal constant
K>0
(not depending on
f,\Omega
and
\Gamma
) such that, for all
f\in\operatorname{Hol}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega})
,

(11.5)

\int_{\Gamma}|f(z)|\,|dz|\leq K\int_{\partial\Omega}|f(z)|\,|dz|.

Clearly
K\geq 1.
Moreover, it can be shown that if
\Gamma
is closed, then
2<K<3.7
(see
[
11
, p.457]
, for example). The inequality was conjectured by J. Littlewood and first proved by Gabriel in
[
24
, Theorem I]
. It is thoroughly discussed in
[
11
, Selected Seminars, 2, 4 and 5]
and
[
32
, Section 5]
providing simpler proofs, more general versions and additional insights.

Theorem 11.5
.

Let
\psi\in(0,\pi)
and
\psi_{0}=\min\{\psi,\pi-\psi\}
. The
family
T=(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi_{0}}}
is
a bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup of angle
\psi_{0}
on each of the spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
.
The generator
-A_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}
of
T
on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
is given by

D(A_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}})=\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}:f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}\},\qquad A_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}f=-f^{\prime}.

Proof.

We will show first that the family of shifts
T
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}

for every
\psi\in(0,\pi).
Then the result
follows quickly by a density argument.

Let
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
so that for
f=g^{\prime}
one has
f\in\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi}).

Assume first that
\psi\in(0,\pi/2]
, and let
\psi^{\prime}\in(0,\psi)
and
\alpha:=\sin(\psi-\psi^{\prime})
.
By the mean value inequality, for any
r>0
and
\varphi\in(-\psi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
we have

(11.6)

\displaystyle r|f(re^{i\varphi})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\pi\alpha^{2}r}\int_{|z-re^{i\varphi}|\leq\alpha r}|f(z)|\,dS(z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\pi\alpha}\int_{-\psi^{\prime}}^{\psi^{\prime}}\int_{(1-\alpha)r}^{(1+\alpha)r}|f(\rho e^{i\varphi})|\,d\rho\,d\varphi

\displaystyle\to 0,

as
r\to 0
or
r\to\infty
, by the dominated convergence theorem.

Now let
\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi}
and
\varphi\in(0,\psi).

Let

(11.7)

\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi}=\{\tau+te^{i\varphi}:t\geq 0\}\cup\{\tau+te^{-i\varphi}:t\geq 0\}.

Let
\psi^{\prime}>\max(|\arg\tau|,\varphi)
, and take
r\in(0,1)
such that
0<r<|\tau|<1/r
. We now apply Gabriel’s inequality (
11.5
) with

\Omega_{r}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}z>r\cos\psi^{\prime},|z|<1/r,|\arg z|<\psi^{\prime}\}

and

\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi,r}:=\{z\in\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi}:|z|\leq 1/r\}\cup\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1/r,\,|\arg(z-\tau)|\leq\varphi\}.

We obtain

\displaystyle K^{-1}\int_{\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi,r}}|f(z)|\,|dz|\leq\int_{\partial\Omega_{r}}|f(z)|\,|dz|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r}^{1/r}|f(\rho e^{i\psi^{\prime}})|\,d\rho+\int_{r}^{1/r}|f(\rho e^{-i\psi^{\prime}})|\,d\rho+2\pi r^{-1}\sup_{\varphi\in(-\psi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})}|f(e^{i\varphi}/r)|

\displaystyle\hbox{}\qquad+2r\sin\psi^{\prime}\sup_{\varphi\in(-\psi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})}\left|f\left(\frac{r\cos\psi^{\prime}}{\cos\varphi}e^{i\varphi}\right)\right|,

where
K>0
is given by (
11.5
).

By (
11.6
),
the last two terms converge to zero as
r\to 0.

From Theorem
4.6
(iv) it follows that

K^{-1}\int_{\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi}}|f(z)|\,|dz|\leq\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi^{\prime}})}\leq\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}.

Thus, since the choice of
\varphi\in(0,\psi)
was arbitrary, we have

(11.8)

\|T(\tau)f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq K\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}.

Now we consider the case when
\psi\in(\pi/2,\pi)
and
\tau\in\Sigma_{\pi-\psi}.

Let for
\varphi\in(0,\psi)

the path
\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi}
be given by (
11.7
),
and
\Gamma^{\pm}_{\tau,\varphi}:=\{\tau+te^{\pm i\varphi}:t\geq 0\}.

Define the half-planes

\mathbb{C}_{+}^{\psi}:=-ie^{i\psi}\Sigma_{\pi/2}

and
\mathbb{C}_{-}^{\psi}:=ie^{-i\psi}\Sigma_{\pi/2}

so that
\Gamma^{\pm}_{\tau,\varphi}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\pm}^{\psi}.

Letting
\widetilde{f}(z)=f(ie^{-i\psi}z),z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{\psi},

and applying (
11.8
) with
\psi=\pi/2,

\widetilde{f}\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})
in place of
f,

and
\widetilde{\tau}=ie^{-i\psi}\tau
in place of
\tau,

we obtain that

\displaystyle K\int_{e^{i\psi}\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|\,|dz|=K\|\widetilde{f}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\geq\|T(\widetilde{\tau})\widetilde{f}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\pi/2})}\geq\int_{\Gamma^{+}_{\tau,\varphi}}|f(z)|\,|dz|.

Similarly

K\int_{e^{-i\psi}\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|\,|dz|\geq\int_{\Gamma^{-}_{\tau,\varphi}}|f(z)|\,|dz|.

Thus, taking into account Theorem
4.6
(iv),
we infer that

2K\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\geq K\left(\int_{e^{i\psi}\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|\,|dz|+\int_{e^{-i\psi}\mathbb{R}}|f(z)|\,|dz|\right)\geq\int_{\Gamma_{\tau,\varphi}}|f(z)|\,|dz|.

Since as above the choice of
\varphi\in(0,\psi)
is arbitrary, we then have

\|T(\tau)f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}\leq 2K\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\psi})}

if
\psi\in(\pi/2,\pi),
and then if
\psi\in(0,\pi)
in view of (
11.8
).

Hence for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi},

(11.9)

\|T(\tau)g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq\max(1,2K)\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\leq 2K\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}.

A direct verification shows that
\|T(\tau)r_{\lambda}-r_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}\to 0,\tau\to 0
, for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\Sigma}_{\psi}
.
It now follows from (
11.9
) and Theorem
5.10
that
(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi_{0}}}
is a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi,0}
and then on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
. The holomorphy of
(T(\tau))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\psi_{0}}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
follows from Theorem
4.8
(iii) and the method discussed in Section
2
.

The claim about the generator
-A_{\mathcal{H}_{\psi}}
can be justified
along the lines of the proof of a similar fact for the space
\mathcal{B}
in
[
7
, Lemma 2.6]
.
∎

Remark 11.6
.

Following a more conventional approach, one may try to prove Theorem
11.5
by reducing the estimates to the half-plane case and applying Carleson’s embedding theorem for
H^{1}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
.
However, the technical details become rather cumbersome, so we prefer to use Gabriel’s inequality allowing for a more transparent argument.

12.
Appendix: The
\mathcal{D}
-calculus vs the HP- and the
\mathcal{B}
-calculi

It is natural to compare the strength of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus with some other functional calculi, such as the recently constructed
\mathcal{B}
-calculus and the well-known HP-calculus.
To show the advantages of our
\mathcal{D}
-calculus with respect to the
\mathcal{B}
-calculus and the HP-calculus,
as an illustrative example, we consider the family of functions
\{f_{n}:n\geq 1\}
given by

f_{n}(z)=\left(\frac{z-1}{z+1}\right)^{n},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

This family is contained in
\mathcal{LM}
(see
[
8
, Section 6]
), and it arises naturally in the study of asymptotics for powers of Cayley transforms of semigroup generators.
It is shown in
[
7
, Lemma 3.7]
and
[
8
, Lemmas 5.1 and 5.2]
that

\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\asymp\log n\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|f_{n}\|_{{\rm HP}}\asymp n^{1/2},\qquad n\to\infty.

We will show that the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus provides sharper estimates for the corresponding operator functions.
To this aim we need the next lemma.

Lemma 12.1
.

For
s>0
,

\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}\leq 1+16\left(B(s/2,1/2)+2^{-s/2}\right),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

Let
s>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. We have

f_{n}(\infty)=1,\quad f^{\prime}_{n}(z)=2n\frac{(z-1)^{n-1}}{(z+1)^{n+1}},

and then

\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}=1+8n\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}(\rho,\psi)\,d\rho}{\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1}\,d\psi=1+8nJ_{n},

where

g_{n}(\rho,\psi)=\left(\frac{\rho^{2}-2\rho\cos\psi+1}{\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}.

Let
J_{n}=J_{1,n}+J_{2,n}+J_{3,n}
,
where

\displaystyle J_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{2}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}(\rho,\psi)\,d\rho}{\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1}\,d\psi,

\displaystyle J_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\pi/4}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{1}^{2}\frac{g_{n}(\rho,\psi)\,d\rho}{\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1}\,d\psi,

\displaystyle J_{3,n}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\cos^{s}\psi\int_{1}^{2}\frac{g_{n}(\rho,\psi)\,d\rho}{\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1}\,d\psi.

We estimate each of the summands
J_{1,n}
,
J_{2,n}
, and
J_{3,n}
separately.
First,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}\,g_{n+2}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle=2(n+1)\frac{(\rho^{2}-1)\cos\psi}{(\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1)^{2}}g_{n}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2(n+1)}{3}\frac{\cos\psi\,g_{n}(\rho,\psi)}{(\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1)}>0,\quad\rho>2.

Hence

\displaystyle J_{1,n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2(n+1)}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s-1}\psi\int_{2}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}\,g_{n+2}(\rho,\psi)\,d\rho\,d\psi

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2(n+1)}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s-1}\psi\,d\psi<\frac{B(s/2,1/2)}{n}.

Next, for all
\rho\in(1,2)
and
\psi\in(\pi/4,\pi/2)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi}\,g_{n+2}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle=2(n+1)\frac{\rho(\rho^{2}+1)\sin\psi}{(\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1)^{2}}g_{n}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(n+1)}{2}\frac{\,g_{n}(\rho,\psi)}{(\rho^{2}+2\rho\cos\psi+1)}>0,

so

\displaystyle J_{2,n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{(n+1)}\int_{1}^{2}\int_{\pi/4}^{\pi/2}\cos^{s}\psi\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}\,g_{n+2}(\rho,\psi)\,d\psi\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{(n+1)}\int_{\pi/4}^{\pi/2}(\cos^{s}\psi)^{\prime}\,d\psi=\frac{2}{2^{s/2}(n+1)}.

Finally, since

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in(1,2),\,\psi\in(0,\pi/4)}g_{n}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle=g_{n}(2,\pi/4)=\left(\frac{5-2\sqrt{2}}{5+2\sqrt{2}}\right)^{(n-1)/2}<\frac{2}{n},

we have

J_{3,n}\leq\frac{2}{n}\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\cos^{s}\psi\,d\psi<\frac{B(s/2,1/2)}{n}.

Summing up the above estimates above, the assertion of the lemma
follows.
∎

The following corollary showing sharpness of the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus is immediate.

Corollary 12.2
.

For any
s>0
,

\frac{\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}}{\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}}\asymp\log n,\qquad\frac{\|f_{n}\|_{{\rm HP}}}{\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{s}}}\asymp n^{1/2},\quad n\to\infty.

Remark 12.3
.

Curiously, for
s=0
the asymptotics of
\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
match those of
\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}
,
and one does not get any advantages using the
\mathcal{D}
-calculus in this case. Specifically, one can show that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(12.1)

1+2\log(n+1)\leq\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\leq 8(4+\log(n+1)).
